“Are you going to tell her she doesn’t have to go to the hospital?”

“I’m coming in.”

We stared at each other for another second before she stepped aside. “Fine.”

I followed Astrid up the stairs, where I’d never been before. I’d hung out in the kitchen downstairs and mostly spent time in the courtyard out front where Juya sold baked goods.

Upstairs, it smelled of stale baking and medicine, which made my heart hurt. One of her helpers, Osman, a Colombian man dressed in blue pajamas from the fifties, though he was only in his twenties, sat gray and listless halfway up the stairs. I squeezed his shoulder as I went by.

“I was here for dinner and she fainted, and she’s only gotten worse,” Astrid said. “Orange, it’s bad.”

I squeezed her shoulder, too. “It’ll be okay.”

She opened the door at the top of the stairs, and I squinted in the dim light.

A canopy bed with a brilliant multi-colored woolen blanket stood in one corner. The windows were covered with red curtains that let in dim pink light from a streetlight. A woven rug that almost matched the bedspread lay across the hardwood floor. The room looked homey and cozy. An earthy smell permeated everything, as though we were stepping into a jungle.

I crossed the threshold and took a second to screw my face into a smile as I caught sight of the tiny lump in the bed. “What’s this I hear about you lazing around?”

As Juya coughed, I straightened my shoulders and stepped around the foot of the bed to go to her side. Her head was propped up on a white lace pillow, her thick gray hair spread across it. Her face looked almost like a prune, so wrinkled I could barely see her eyes. She looked old—much older than she usually did.

She whispered, the faintest accent coloring her vowels, “It’s just a—what’s the word—an ant.”

“A bug?” I sat down on the bedspread and took her gnarled hand in mine. It was ice cold.

Juya coughed again and curled into herself. Osman shoved past Astrid and picked up a teapot on the bedside table and filled a delicate porcelain cup. That was where the smell was coming from.

I had no healing powers. I could move earth around, and I could use human emotions to play little jokes, but I’d never felt less like joking and more like crying. “Juya,” I said gently, “what can we do?”

“Nothing. It’s nothing.”

“It’s not something—” I glanced at Astrid. “—*weird* that I could take care of for you?”

Juya chuckled, a shadow of her usually joyous laugh. *“Duende*, you cannot pull the roof down to make this better.”

“What will everyone say when they see the building falling apart?”

Her eyes flew open, revealing black irises. “What are you talking about?”

I leaned close to her and whispered, “This place looks like an abandoned building.”

“To you even?”

She closed her eyes, and the room shivered. I shivered with it.

She gasped and lay back against her pillow. “There. I hadn’t known I’d locked my home to everyone.” She looked up at me, her face ashen. “I had not meant to.”

I took her hand again. “Well, good. Because we’re opening the bakery for you.”

“That’s the perfect idea.” Astrid clapped her hands.

Juya was a goddess; she granted boons the way I made bargains. She *needed* them, and exactly the moment she was weakest, she was missing her principal source of power.

“I feel…” Her eyes lost focus and drifted toward the ceiling. “*Vieja*.”

“I don’t know what that means,” I whispered, stroking her hand.

“Old.”

I was going to protest that she wasn’t at all old, but she counted her age in millennia. She’d never seemed weighed down by the years until now. She delighted in simple pleasures that had long escaped my notice, choosing to spend her eternity baking and making small talk. The seemingly minor exchanges fed her power after all these years.

I leaned down and rested my lips against her forehead. It was cool. I consoled myself that at least she didn’t have a fever, but she shouldn’t be this cold. Not a goddess born in the Colombian rainforest.

“You’re not old.”

Juya smiled. But I could tell the effort cost her, and she was only doing it to placate me.

I opted for Astrid’s platitudes and ramped up my energy, cracked my knuckles, and said, “Well, you’ll see that everyone still needs you after they eat my baking for a day.”

She closed her eyes, and her head drifted to the side. My stomach bottomed out. I blinked away my tears as I went downstairs.

Juya couldn’t go. There were very few people I trusted, and she quite possibly was at the top of the list. She’d been the only magical creature who’d asked nothing of me and who’d actually tried to *help* when I’d shown up in DC for my father’s inheritance, thinking it was finally some financial assistance and learning I was a goblin instead.

She didn’t try to take them or take advantage of me. She helped protect me, and when she’d heard of my mother’s trouble, she’d written me a check—no questions asked. Early onset Alzheimer’s is pricey to treat.

After repairing my smile, I turned right at the base of the stairs into her kitchen with its 1950s Formica table, a huge fridge from the same era, and a cast-iron stove that dominated half the room, now sitting cold. The clock on the wall said 12:30 am. I felt unbearably tired and off myself.

Osman stepped into the kitchen. His black hair was plastered to his head with water that was still dripping onto his ear. He’d traded pajamas for a pair of worn jeans and a thick black sweater.

“Can you get that monster started?” I asked, pointing to the stove.

He nodded, then added, *“Gracias*, Orange.”

I raised my eyebrows. He’d never been a huge fan of mine. The first time we met, he’d tried to drag Juya away from the trickster *duende* who might do her harm.

He must’ve been thinking of that moment because he grinned ruefully. “I’m glad you don’t scare easy, goblin.”

I flashed him a crooked smile. “Oh, I scare so easily. But then I do it.”

“Do what?”

“Well, today it’s baking for a goddess.”

“She isn’t that,” he said, cocking his head.

I spun around. “What is she, exactly?”

He shook his head. “I don’t have the right words. Maybe goddess is right, but not like yours.”

“Fair.” The only goddesses I knew about were Roman and Greek, who were nothing like Juya, to say the least. I opened the fridge and cupboards, reassured to find eggs, flour, sugar, and baking powder. The science of cake was the same no matter what you were.

Even my poor recipes took on new life in Juya’s kitchen. The snickerdoodles burst with flavor from her cinnamon that did not taste like any cinnamon I’d ever bought in a grocery store. Scones seemed to levitate off the tray, perfectly mixed no matter what I did.

Hours later, Astrid popped her head into the kitchen without an empty tray in her hands. “They’ve eaten everything.”

I sagged against the stove, then jumped away when the cast iron burned. “I don’t believe it.”

“Customers bought anything we put on the table just to help Juya. One tried to buy my keys.”

I snorted. “Pity shopping. I’ll take it.”

“Hey, they were great! Osman is closing things down.”

For the first time since I’d been with Juya upstairs, I sat, fighting the dragging exhaustion in every finger. I gestured to the counter, where a series of bags sat propped against one another.

Astrid and I were still debating names: “mug cake in a bag” or “bag cake.” I’d invented them a few weeks ago after my oven broke at the hostel where I was living. I put the wet ingredients in one bag and the dry ingredients in another and put a sticker on them explaining to mix and microwave for one minute.

In Astrid’s fevered imaginings, they transformed into the new hot thing in grocery stores across the country. We were going to build a company, and we were going to make it big with a brand-new concept for the baking aisle. No one had yet been lazy enough to include both wet and dry ingredients in their mug cake mixes.

The only reason I’d agreed to go into business with her was that my only role would be creating the actual products. Strategy, market research, aisle frontage, and who knew what else—that was all Astrid. What’s before type A? If there was a type more on top of life than A, that was Astrid.

“I put some peanut butter and chocolate in that one,” I told her. “And that’s a spice cake mixture.”

Astrid picked one up. “We should have a third bag for icing.”

I shook my head and was going to keep quiet, but I knew she expected my baking advice. “If you put cold icing on a hot cake, it melts. And since they’ll be baking them at home, they’re probably not going to wait.”

“What about frosting? And how is frosting different from icing?”

“Butter. Which would be harder to make shelf-stable. For now, everything in those bags can sit out.”

She pointed at me. “Do you think we’re ready for me to take these to school and see if freshmen in the dorms like them?”

I looked at the plastic baggies and their handwritten labels. I fought a wave of fear. “As long as you sell them for five cents. Freshmen eat anything, right?”

“Oh, no, a dollar at least.” Laughing, she stacked them into her worn backpack.

“How on earth do you keep the faith?”

She turned to me, her brown eyes sober. “I didn’t get the job.”

“They’re idiots.”

She was only three months from graduation and frantically searching for an internship or a job. This one had only been an admin thing, but it was salaried.

“This is DC,” she said. “Someone needs a grunt.”

“No, someone needs an insanely driven college graduate with a business degree.”

“Thanks.”

“You’re a million times more competent than I am,” I said, “and I keep getting new jobs all the time. I’m totally not worried about you.”

She laughed again. “Orange, no offense, but I’m not going to clean things for a living. My grandma did that for forty years. I will not do that.”

“You won’t have to.” I didn’t tell her I’d already lost the cleaning job I’d picked up on Craigslist a few weeks ago; crime-fighting and regular schedules didn’t mesh very well.

“You should finish your degree!” she said with some of her old steel I feared. “Then you wouldn’t have to, either. Even without your degree, you have most of an education—and from Berkeley.”

I shook my head. “I’m allergic to offices. And math. I’ll be a baker at… whatever we’re calling this company when it gets hugely successful, right? I’ll get at least a penny of every cake you sell.”

“In twenty years, we’ll be breaking even!”

I fought the familiar panic that came on whenever I thought about my degree, but I had bigger problems than matriculation—including a dead body, a possible serial killer, Juya’s illness, the mysterious Lubber Fiend, and all goblins in the territory I still didn’t know the edges of. I was kind of surprised a new challenger hadn’t found me here.

“Oh, yeah, I forgot to tell you,” Astrid said. “This weird little dude with a bad tan job stopped by the courtyard this morning. He kept talking about a challenge. Kept asking for somebody named Peeling? I told him no one was here by that name. But he looked a little like you.”

I closed my eyes. “I spoke too soon.”

Astrid’s forehead crinkled. “You didn’t say anything.”

“Never mind. Just get those to school and let me know how people like them. We have to keep trucking, right?”

“Neither of us is becoming truckers.”

I grimaced. “It would probably pay better.”

“Don’t even joke.” She hitched her backpack straps onto her shoulders. With all the textbooks she carted everywhere, it took two tries. She leaned forward to balance its weight.

“*Duende*!” We all turned as a treble voice shrieked from the courtyard.

When I saw Osman run by the kitchen, I shouted, “Would you please tell your helpers I will not hurt you? I’m here to help you!”

“Battle stations!” the voice cried again. “*Duende* in the courtyard!”

“In the courtyard?” I ran for a window, fighting more diaphanous read curtains to see out. A purple-skinned goblin with large ears stood in the middle of Juya’s courtyard with a stick in his hands.

As if he knew I was there, he looked up at the front of the house, saluted me with a fist to his shoulder, and said in a squeaky American accent, “I challenge thee!”

# 7

“I am Mert, and I challenge thee. To a fight to the death,” the little red goblin repeated when I got outside with Astrid. He held a club almost as big as he was.

“What is he talking about?” she asked as I steered her past him.

“Family game. Uh, role-playing. LARPing. You’re right. He’s a cousin.” I hustled her to the gate and out of the courtyard. “Get to class! Let me know how they sell!”

When she turned to walk away, I saluted the goblin as I’d seen Everglades do on the train, pounding my fist into my chest. I didn’t know if it was an official goblin thing or if he was trying to be dramatic, but it felt right. And then I realized I’d pounded the right side of my chest and quickly switched hands. He watched this with his head cocked.

“Let’s get this over with,” I said grimly.

And then I remembered the goblin’s promise to find the Lubber Fiend. I paused at the gate to take in this scrawny creature. This couldn’t be the second most powerful goblin in my hopefully tiny territory, could it?

When I stepped closer, my feet flew across a thin layer of ice, and I landed on my back in a flail of arms. I gazed at the grey sky as rain sprinkled my face. How was the sun coming up and the temperature dropping?

The goblin’s head replaced the sky as he leaned over me, his club dangling on the ground. “It’s no fun if you die on your own.”

I curled my body to sit up. “Ow.”

He leaped out of the way and asked, “Not dead?”

“There’s no dying! None of us are really dying here, right?”

The little guy ignored my question and hefted his club.

I climbed to my feet, reasonably sure nothing was busted. “He said nobody would die unless… Are you the Lubber Fiend?”

His skin lightened from maroon to pink and he spun around three times, water flinging out from his clothes and sopping hair. “The Fiend is coming?”

My breath whooshed out. If this wasn’t the Fiend and if Everglades was correct that they were coming in reverse order of power, this creature was one step up. He really had no chance at all.

He was wearing homemade hand-spun clothes, and his maroon hair dangled around him in oily clumps. Whatever hole he’d crawled out of, it hadn’t been a vaunted park near the Everglades. This dude hadn’t been near civilization in a while. I had to find the Lubber Fiend to stop disturbing poor, powerless goblins.

“He’s on his way as we speak,” I said, hoping he’d flee at that.

For a moment, he looked like he was going to. Instead, he squeezed his club and squared his feet with an audible gulp. He said again in a quavering voice, “I am Mert, and I challenge thee.”

He swung his club.

I leaped out of the way, called my magic, and tossed a chunk of cement at him. His club went slack in his hands as cement crumbled and pelted him. “You made no bargain!”

“No bargain required.” I cracked my knuckles in satisfaction.

“No bargain?”

“I bargain with myself.”

“Well… Well, we’ll just see who the best is!” His voice grew higher and higher with every word.

“The best what?” I took a careful step forward, searching for a spot of cement without ice.

“The best goblin!”

He swung his club again, and I dodged again.

“Yeah, but by what criteria are we judging? Who’s the best fighter?” I leaped backward when he attacked, forgetting the ice, and landed in another heap. “Ow. Why would fighting matter?”

His bushy brows furrowed as he considered that. “The best!”

The door to Juya’s opened and Osman peeked out.

“Stay inside!” I shouted as I climbed to my feet again.

Mert swung his club and clipped me on my collarbone.

“Ow! Stop that!” I faced him and knocked his club away again. “Can we skip to the end? You will not land a punch. I’m human.”

“And you dare claim to be—”

“Half. My father was the *Peallaidh*, and I am his heir.” I let out a long breath, fighting my fury. “I’m so sick of this! Why didn’t one of you stop by just to say hi?”

Anger heated my chest. I didn’t want to keep getting attacked and falling on the ice. I brushed my sopping hair out of my eyes. “Just give up!”

He came at me again and pounded his club into the concrete at my feet. It fractured.

The rocks beneath my feet had always been wonderful allies, but they parted for him, and I found myself in an icy mass of mud. “This is my friend’s front step,” I said. “Stop destroying things!”

I tried to leave the courtyard and draw him away from Juya’s townhouse, but he’d found my weak point. Grinning, he pounded at the door with his club.

“Stop it!”

The door splintered and flew off its hinges. He dashed in, and someone screamed inside. I raced after him, a sick sense of foreboding joining my anger and roiling my stomach.

Mert advanced on Teresa, who only had a rolling pin in one hand for defense.

He lunged for her. I lunged for him.

I missed. He didn’t.

The club hit her arm with a wicked crack. The rolling pin clattered to the floor, and Mert landed on her at the base of the stairs.

Teresa whimpered as Mert roared.

A red haze veiled my vision, and all the anger building in my body flowed into my fists. I launched into the air and landed on the goblin’s back.

He choked Teresa, and I pulled his matted, dirty hair. Hard.

“Don’t you dare!” I screeched. I reared up and backhanded him in the temple. It stunned him enough for me to maneuver him off Teresa, who looked even more shocked.

“Get out of here!” I screamed as I hauled Mert away by his neck.

Osman jumped down the stairs muttering about *duende* as he pulled her up with him.

I turned Mert around so I could hit him again. His nose cracked and spurted blood that looked almost black. I followed that first punch with a few more, reveling in the feel of my knuckles meeting his cheekbone.

When he stopped resisting, I didn't register it. I didn’t care.

Even as my brain told me Teresa was safe, I kept hitting him until Osman jumped down and tried to grab a fist. As I reared back to hit him too, his screams finally penetrated.

“Orange! *¡Alto! Dios mio, duende.* Stop!”

I lunged away from them, all my rage gone, replaced by solid horror. “Is he dead? Did I kill him?”

# 8

My knuckles were ripped to shreds and throbbed in time with my pounding heart, but Mert had it far worse. Blood streamed from a cut under the goblin’s left eye, and bruises already bloomed across his chest. I kneeled near him again and patted his torso. I swallowed hard when I felt a rib wobble.

Osman crouched to take the pulse in his wrist. “He’s alive.”

Something deep in me unclenched, but I couldn’t calm the nausea. I’d never, ever lost control like this in my life. I’d been attacked a lot since I’d learned about my goblin half, but I’d never retaliated like this, with so much anger.

I turned to Teresa and Osman to see if they were okay, but they flinched away from me. There was no familiar exasperation in their eyes, just naked fear.

“Sorry,” I whispered as I twisted for the door.

“Orange!” Osman shouted as I stepped over the ruined wood.

I stumbled into the courtyard. A thin film of ice covered the entire world around me, and it seemed to get thicker with every raindrop. I fell against the gate as I tried to get it open and then again against a parked car as I tried to stay upright and get away.

I failed.

I crumpled onto the pavement between an old pickup truck and a barren oak tree in its little patch of dirt between sidewalk slabs. Trying to quell my revulsion, I stared at my hands. My bloodied knuckles were already healing with my magic, but I didn’t want them to. I needed the reminder.

Slowly, my breathing returned to normal, and nobody followed. I scrubbed the crystals off my eyelashes and the tears and rain off my face, fighting the draining adrenaline as freezing water soaked through my jeans.

“He isn’t dead,” I told myself.

Between the rain that washed away the blood and my body’s ability to heal itself, my skin looked untouched in moments, but what I’d done stained my conscience, maybe my soul. If those things were real.

“Stop it. Just stop. Your soul is fine,” I reassured myself. My soul *was* fine. If I had one. “Stop it!” I told myself firmly.

I was a normal human goblin, and I’d been attacked. I’d ended it. It happened every single day around the world. Yes, I’d gone a little bit beyond defending myself (okay, a lot beyond defending myself), but I didn’t suddenly need Scharf to arrest me.

I needed to turn off the horror and fear that were making my hands shake and my stomach heave and fix this.

“Mert isn’t dead.” I took a breath. “I didn’t kill him.”

I was turning to return and talk to Mert and make sure he didn’t try to kill himself to repair his honor in his condition when my phone rang. I dug it out of my back pocket.

“Where are you?” It was Scharf.

I muted my phone so I could say, “Whoops!”

I usually tried to get home if I told her I was going home since her professional paranoia extended to everyone in her life, but with Juya sick, I’d forgotten.

I unmuted and said, “Would you believe me if I said I was on your couch and invisible?”

Something thumped. “I’m sitting on the couch, so no.”

I sighed. “I’m at Juya’s.”

“How am I supposed to believe that, either? Never mind. We have an interview this morning with the restaurant manager and some other witnesses.”

I waited for a second to see if she would correct herself. When she didn’t, I repeated, “*We*?”

“Yeah, *we*. Isn’t this what you bargained for?”

“But I thought you didn’t need my help.” I grabbed the pickup to haul myself upright and the tree for leverage to get to the sidewalk.

“I need to know if anyone is lying.”

This was one thing I could bargain for: the truth.

“Oh, so now I’m helpful,” I huffed, just a little.

“Orange, I’m sorry about yesterday.” She trailed off, and I said nothing. “It was a rough night. Let’s just move forward, okay?” Only Scharf says things like “move forward,” in real life.

“I’m sorry too,” I said after a few seconds, “about the dumpster and everything.”

A branch cracked above my head, and I ducked out from under it and landed on my butt again.

“You were trying to help. I know that.”

I sighed. “Where should I meet you?” Assuming I could get anywhere in this storm.

“I’ll pick you up.”

“No!” I did not want to wait around Juya’s for Scharf to make her way to me. “No, it’s too far north. Pick me up at the Metro in Chinatown.” My orange line materialized at my statement, pointing south. I glanced towards her townhouse once before I followed it, salving my shrieking conscious with the fact that I had the bargain to fulfill with Scharf, and Mert couldn’t have been in better hands. The branch crashed down where I had been sitting moments before in a cacophony of ice, wood, and water.

<<<>>>

I sat in the front seat of Scharf’s new Buick and turtled down into my orange leather jacket, debating whether to break the silence. She had brought it with her and handed it to me when she pulled alongside the metro entrance. It had belonged to her ex, but I’d inherited it when I got here from California with nowhere near the wardrobe necessary to deal with the DC winter. It seemed like weeks since I’d been truly warm.

She maneuvered out from under the gate and down the street of restaurants. The rain had calmed a little, though her wipers were still on high. Through the water, the streets looked even worse in the light of day. Sports memorabilia, bottles, food, lanterns, and trash littered the sidewalk and pavement. Boards were nailed to various shop windows above piles of glass. It would be a while before the scars of the riot faded.

“So. Jack. Demon,” she said as she pulled up in front of *Pasta Joe’s.*

“Oh, hell no, we are not having that conversation right now.”

“*Hell* no?”

I clenched my fists without realizing it. I loosened my fingers, horror drowning my sudden anger. *Are you going to punch everybody who annoys you?*

“Please, no,” I mumbled.

Something in my voice must have alerted her because she turned off the car but didn’t move. “Are you okay?”

“Absolutely!” I said with manic cheer and pawed at my door handle.

“Orange.”

My hands stilled and I turned to her, forcing calm and a smile. I wasn’t the one laying battered on the floor after a fight. “I’m okay.”

She just stared at me for five more seconds as tried to keep my face still.

“Should I go invisible for this?” I asked a little desperately.

She took a slow breath and nodded. “Yeah. With BAU involved, I can’t be bringing in consultants.”

“What’s BAU again?” I asked.

“Behavioral analysis. Probing motive, patterns, anticipating behavior, that sort of thing.”

“And that gobbledygook means?”

“Psychotherapy for psychopaths.”

I laughed. “That was actually funny.”

She smiled for a moment and my shoulders finally relaxed too. Last night had been strange; we’d been at each other’s throats. I loved to annoy her, but I hadn’t meant to anger her. More than her apology, her attempt at humor put us on solid ground.

We got out of the car. I clutched at the hood to stay upright on the ice and get to the curb.

“Careful with the car!”

I let go. Her old department-issued car hadn’t survived our last case, so this was a new requisition. Scharf had called it a girl car when she saw it because it was the color of an eggplant, a deep purple, but she could barely explain what happened to the other one, so she hadn’t protested.

“Who are we interviewing?” I asked, looking down the row of shops: bank, music shop, the *Long Yinyue Teashop* where David worked, and *Pasta Joe’s*, the sight of the murder.

“Alibis,” Scharf said. “I want alibis from everybody and their dogs so we can cross them off the list.”

“And if they don’t have an alibi?” I asked.

“That’s where you come in.”

“Bargains are better than alibis.”

Scharf started towards the door of the teashop. If someone didn’t have an alibi, they could deny the murder and I’d know if they were telling the truth or not.

“Agent Scharf? Where are you going?”

Scharf pivoted slowly. Three people walked towards us from a huge black SUV: Carlisle, Scharf’s too-beautiful colleague in violent crimes; Fuller, the profiler extraordinaire; and his niece, Melissa, with her notepad.

I froze before I remembered I was invisible. “You didn’t tell me the cavalry was coming.”

“It’s a joint investigation,” Scharf muttered to her belt buckle. “I don’t know what the journalist is doing here.”

“Scharf?” Fuller demanded.

“I’m going to interview David Bao, the first witness.”

“No, no, no. We have to talk to the manager,” Fuller said, waving his hands with every word. “We have to find the new employee. That is the Restaurant Killer. He stays six weeks before he strikes.”

“It’s standard procedure to interview the outcry witness,” Scharf said.

“We don’t have time for standards!” Fuller retorted, and Melissa scribbled madly.

“How about I get his alibi, just real quick, and I’ll call you if it’s suspect?” Scharf asked.

“Good plan,” Carlisle said and gestured for Melissa to precede him toward *Pasta Joe’s.*

“Miss Fuller can’t accompany us,” Scharf said with genuine shock.

“It’s fine,” Melissa said and dug in a slinky purse wrapped crosswise across her chest. “I have a pass. I have full access to the investigation. For the biography.”

Scharf looked like she wanted to protest, but eyed Fuller. “The bureau is vetting the manuscript before it’s published?”

“Of course!” Melissa replied, her head bobbing like she was stuck to the front dash of a car.

Scharf sighed and said, “We’ll meet back here to compare notes in a few.”

Melissa tucked a wet curl behind her ear. “That’s great. And if I could just get a quick interview about your background…”

Scharf ignored her and opened the door to the teashop, which had a handwritten sign that said, “Closed” on it above an older handwritten sign that said, “We have coffee.” The door creaked and hit a tinkling charm that sounded ominous.

Scharf held it open, and I slipped behind her into the cloying scent of dried tea. The shop was a long, thin room with a counter and workspace down one side and narrow tables along the other. Pots, cups, and jars of green and black dried leaves covered both long walls. A battered aluminum urn sat on the counter with a sign that said, “Coffee - $2.”

I leaned against a half wall next to the door, and the wall twanged.

I leaped back and bumped into Scharf, who tried to pretend she’d planned on stepping sideways.

“Don’t touch anything,” Scharf muttered and moved forward.

“We’re back to that?” I replied, but she didn’t turn. I leaned over the wall to see a keyboard and realized I’d leaned against the back of a low piano. I hurried to catch up with Scharf.

She approached the counter where David Bao and an older couple sat on stools in front of it.

“My name is Agent Laura Scharf with the FBI violent crimes office here in DC.”

I hoped my attempt to calm David down last night with magic hadn’t lingered. He looked catatonic.

The old man half bowed as he took her hand. He was dressed in a rumpled shirt with a short collar. “My name is Chen, and this is my wife, Li-Na. David said you wanted to speak with us?”

“Yes, thank you for your time. We need… We need a clearer picture of the night. Can you walk me through yesterday?”

“Make a deal!” I said urgently.

She took a deep breath. “Perhaps I could have some delicious tea once you’ve told me your… your involvement yesterday?”

“So that you can eliminate us from the suspect list,” Mrs. Bao said slowly.

Mr. Bao shrugged helplessly. “She watches *Law and Order.*”

Scharf gave a curt nod. “Yes, I'm sorry.”

“We—” Mr. Bao started.

“Not done yet!” I insisted. Until the chime in my head signaled, there was no bargain between us, and I would not know if they were telling the truth.

“Does that sound like a deal?” Scharf asked.

“Oh, of course,” Mrs. Bao said and shifted uncomfortably.

“Yes,” Mr. Bao said, and the chime sounded.

“Good to go,” I told her and sank onto a stool at the end of the counter.

“Your… Your yesterday?” Scharf repeated.

“We were open all day,” Mr. Bao said. “Busy with the celebrations and packing. Many people came for tea.”

The old woman said to her husband’s shoulder, “Of course, they were here for tea.”

He mumbled something in Chinese into her ear.

“Packing?” Scharf asked.

“We have rented a shop in Rockville in Maryland,” Mrs. Bao said.

“Our lease is up, and the rent is doubling,” Mr. Bao added.

“And too many people ask for lattes,” David spoke for the first time. I was glad he was listening.

“And that,” Mr. Bao said.

“So your movements?” Scharf prompted when the silence stretched.

“I am on the parade committee,” Mrs. Bao said. “I worked all day on the street. There were many hands to hold.”

“Politicians,” Mr. Bao said, like a curse.

Mrs. Bao smiled, though she didn’t look at all amused. “There were many hands to hold,” she repeated.

“What next?” Scharf asked.

“We went to a friend’s house around four?” Mr. Bao said, glancing at his wife.

Mrs. Bao nodded.

“They had asked Li-Na to play.”

“Play what?” I asked before I remembered I was invisible.

“I teach piano and perform,” Mrs. Bao said, seeming to answer my question. She smoothed her perfectly straight, gray hair cut in a bob.

“We did not return home until we received a call about the… About Rodger,” Mr. Bao said.

“Did you know him?”

They both nodded.

“He worked next door almost a year.” Mrs. Bao turned to her husband. “Or more than a year?”

He nodded. “At least. He was here for the holidays last year,”

“He came in for tea,” Mrs. Bao added.

Mr. Bao smiled. It was weak, but it transformed his face. “He came in for coffee. We set him straight.”

I thought I heard a noise at the back of the shop, another twang of strings. I got up and wandered towards it, taking in the shelves stuffed with games and floor-to-ceiling varieties of teas. To the left at the end of the counter, I noticed an archway cut into the wall into a shop next door. A shadow flickered in the opening.

I jumped as I came face-to-face with a coiling serpent. I blinked and realized it was only a large red vase on a shelf. I stayed there, senses outstretched for noise or movement, but nothing else happened.

“Who works there?” I asked.

Scharf twitched as if brushing off an annoying fly, but she pointed to the opening. “How is that store connected to yours? Was anyone working in there last night?”

All three Baos swung their heads towards the back of the shop in obvious surprise.

“Mr. Liu owns the music shop,” Mr. Bao said. “We broke a hole in the wall several years ago to encourage business between us. He closes at three. Did he yesterday?”

Mrs. Bao nodded. “Without fail. He went to his home long before Rodger…” She gulped and tried again. “Rodger…” She shrugged helplessly.

Scharf took her notebook out of the front pocket of her navy suit jacket. “We’ll need to talk with him. His name is Liu, yes? Spell that?”

Mr. Bao did and said, “He could not have. He is not capable of such a thing.”

Scharf shook her head sadly. “In my line of work, you learn everyone is capable of just about anything.”

Mr. Bao shook his head. “He lost a leg a long, long time ago. He walks with two crutches. He is not capable, I mean.”

“Okay, thanks,” Scharf said and closed her notebook. She would still talk to him, but I thought the Baos were right. This would have taken significant strength.

When they didn’t add anything else, Scharf asked, “Did you know of a new worker next door?” She concealed the importance of that question by fiddling with her notebook.

Mrs. Bao shrugged. “There were many. Unless they visited us, I did not keep track. Rodger lasted longer than most.”

“Was he upset recently? Any kind of behavior change?”

“He was a… contemplative person.” Mrs. Bao shrugged delicately.

I said, “Hunh?”

Scharf froze, but when nobody else looked at me, she cranked her shoulders down and asked, “What does that mean?”

“He hated everything,” David said flatly.

“We spoke of music often,” Mrs. Bao added. “He played the cello.”

“Did he like that?” Scharf asked.

“Did he like it? That’s an interesting question. He wanted to play perfectly, though there is no such thing.”

“He had little joy in his life,” Mr. Bao added.

“But he was always polite,” Mrs. Bao insisted.

“Did you see him yesterday?” Scharf asked when the qualifiers wound down.

All three shook their heads.

“Take me through the rest of the day.”

Mrs. Bao sniffed. “We came back when David called with—”

“With the news,” Mr. Bao added.

Mrs. Bao raised a shaking hand towards Scharf. “David said it was some kind of repeat. That this man has killed before.”

“I explained all this to you, *Gu Ma*. They thought they caught him before, but they were wrong.”

“We’re going to need the name of your friends where you played,” Scharf said, flipping a page in her notebook.

Mrs. Bao drew a piece of paper from behind the counter. “We have prepared that.”

Scharf looked at me for a second, an eyebrow raised.

I shook my head and said, “The tea.”

She hadn’t drunk any tea. For a bargain to be fulfilled, both sides had to be completed. I thought back over our first words and winced. “Also, David wasn’t in the bargain.”

She stiffened and cast a pleading glance my way. I shrugged helplessly.

She sighed. “I might have that glass of tea now if it’s not too much trouble.”

“Of course.” Mrs. Bao jumped up and swung around the counter. She started pouring hot water into and out of a teapot at a sink behind the counter. Scharf dug out a five-dollar bill.

“Our treat,” Mr. Bao said.

“No, I insist. Policy.”

Mrs. Bao righted the teapot without looking and began measuring two small scoops of leaves into the pot before adding more hot water. The activity seemed to soothe her. As she worked, she said, “This used to be such a vibrant neighborhood. Now there are only chains.”

I blinked. “Chains?”

“Chain *stores*, you mean?” Scharf asked.

“Yes, the large stores come in and write their names in Mandarin and call it community.” Mrs. Bao set a dark red clay teapot and two tiny cups that matched it in front of Scharf. The pot was squat and small, enough for a cup or two of tea, and a design was printed into the clay of the pot and around the cups as well, a serpent head and a field of stars.

“That’s beautiful,” Scharf said, picking up a cup.

Mrs. Bao smiled. “My husband makes them.”

Scharf looked at Mr. Bao. “Impressive.”

I got up to examine one more closely, reminding myself to not pick one up. We didn’t need to explain disappearing teacups.

Scharf poured the tea into one of the tiny cups. It was the color of rust. She blew on the tea and took a quick drink. I waited for the chime to see if he had told the truth, but I didn’t hear it. “You have to think it is delicious. You said *delicious*.”

She looked down at her cup and muttered, “Oh, God.”

She smiled and drank. Still no chime. I shook my head, and Scharf grimaced. “Now that’s a flavor.”

“Pu-erh fermented green tea,” Mrs. Bao said, and lovingly caressed the pot.

“Think about how delicious it will be when we know if they did it,” I told her.

Scharf took another sip, and this time, the chime sounded.

I played back over their statements. “The two of them are in the clear. Their alibi will hold.”

Scharf looked at David. “Can we chat for a moment, since you were here?”

“I didn’t do it!”

I groaned. That would have been the perfect thing to hear if I’d had a bargain in place. Scharf glanced at me, and I shook my head.

“Repeat that. And I’ll…” She cast around for something. “Drink the rest of this cup of tea.”

“Okay?” David said, looking at her like she was completely insane.

“So long as it’s the truth,” I said quickly.

“So long as it’s the truth,” Scharf repeated.

The chime signaled the start of a bargain.

“I didn’t do it?” David said slowly.

Scharf quickly downed the rest of the tea. The second chime confirmed he told the truth. Scharf examined the bottom of her cup. “It grows on you.”

Mr. Bao refilled her cup with a small smile.

“He’s telling the truth,” I said. “He didn’t do it.”

I expected her to get up, but she didn’t. She just sipped more tea and asked, “Did you notice anyone around? See anyone out of the ordinary? Anyone with an interest in Rodger in the weeks leading up to—to last night?”

David shook his head.

“I know this is difficult. That night, in the alley. Did you see anyone else there?”

David shook his head again. “I heard running though, footsteps.”

Scharf stiffened. “Where?”

“Down the alley, away from the street.”

“When?”

He shook his head. “It’s a blur. Right before I saw you and your friend.”

I gasped. We’d missed the murderer by seconds.

“Okay, Anything else?” Scharf asked, concealing any reaction.

He looked to his aunt and uncle and shook his head. “It’s a blur.”

Scharf dug a business card out of the front pocket of her jacket and handed it to him. “If that changes, get in touch, day or night.”

Glass shattered somewhere outside, and Scharf jumped to her feet.

“We thought this was done!” Mrs. Bao shouted as Scharf carefully put down the teacup and then bolted for the front door.

“Stay here!” she shouted as she yanked it open.

I followed her, needing magic to make me fast enough to get through the closing door.

The front window of *Pasta Joe’s* had exploded onto the street*.* A giant stainless-steel pot and a squiggly pile of noodles rolled amidst the shattered glass.

Scharf drew her weapon, keeping it by her thigh. “Open the door.”

“Me?” I shrieked.

“Magic,” she whispered through clenched teeth.

“Oh, yeah.” I wrenched open *Pasta Joe’s* front door with a thought, and Scharf ducked inside in a weird dodging dance that was as natural to her as brushing her teeth. I hardened my skin and followed her.

Inside, a large Italian man in an apron stood in the center of the wrecked dining room. I could only assume this was Joe of *Pasta Joe's*. He brandished a wooden spoon at the elder Fuller who was sprawled next to one of the few upright chairs in the room, looking stunned. Carlisle and Melissa crouched at his side.

Fuller laid eyes on Scharf and pointed to the cook. “Arrest him!”

“Did he hit you?”

“He… He insulted me!”

Scharf sagged and holstered her weapon. I did not let down my guard.

“He insulted *me*!” Joe said. He didn't sound Italian; he sounded like he was from Brooklyn.

Scharf put out two hands in a universal placating gesture. “What happened?”

Carlisle and Melissa eyed each other over the profiler’s pomaded silver locks. Melissa whispered to him about her uncle’s brilliance at rapport as a master of human behavior, and that this was an interview technique and Scharf was ruining it.

I gritted my teeth and resisted the urge to unleash my magic. Maybe I could make a teeny tiny bargain and leach all the silicon from her breasts.

“I told you the truth. The truth is not an insult,” Fuller insisted and pulled himself upright to sit in the chair.

“What exactly did you say?” Scharf asked.

“The average IQ of workers in the service industry is ten points lower than the average IQ of anyone in law enf—”

Joe charged, and I grabbed for the earth in his body to keep him from strangling Fuller.

I wanted to let him. I don't know where he pulled that statistic, but as a long-time worker in the service industry with tons of friends who were also in the service industry, I took issue with the statement.

I forced down my anger. Was I seriously going to punch anyone who upset me? And what was Fuller thinking?

“The foremost profiler in the FBI is terrible at building rapport,” I mumbled.

“This interview is over,” Scharf said. “Come on.”

She walked over to Fuller, and I helped her pull him to his feet with magic. Once he was moving, Carlisle and his niece followed like two little, perfect ducklings. Joe stood aside so they could get out the door and slammed it behind them as an automatic doorbell bleated. I stared at the closed door and then at Joe, who threw a chair at the wall. I looked at the front window fringed in jagged shards of glass too big to get through.

I was trapped.

“Crap.”

# 9

I stood just inside the door to *Pasta Joe’s.* After a few more thrown chairs, Joe’s anger seemed to burn out, and he started cleaning up with muttered curses. He did not seem inclined to leave the room.

So I had the option of magically opening the door, going through the small, shattered hole in the front window, or heading for the back where the dead body had been.

Reluctantly, I chose door number three and headed for the back. There were swinging red shutters into the back like in an old-fashioned saloon, and I slid under them. The thin, galley-style kitchen was spotless, with a stove and a sink on one side and bare steel countertops on the other. At the back, there was a tiny hand-washing sink beside metal coolers and dry storage with plates, cutlery, huge brown bags of pasta, and rice on metal shelves. The body was gone.

I knew the body was gone, but I still shook as I dodged around another metal shelf full of takeout containers and flimsy utensils that had not been a part of the original restaurant design. Gingerly, I stepped over the spot and opened the back door. I closed it just as carefully and escaped into the alley.

While the storefront had faded in daylight, the alley exploded into technicolor debris. Trash, some from last night’s investigation and some from the beginning of time, spilled out of multiple maroon dumpsters and coated the rippling asphalt. The potent scent of rot assaulted my nostrils.

I jammed my hands into the pockets of my orange coat and trudged towards the street. It was still raining steadily, and the ground was treacherous. Half a dozen pools of water were quickly turning to ice. My California blood hadn’t enjoyed all the snow when I first got here, but freezing sleet was infinitely worse.

When I saw a blue hand poking out from beside a dumpster, I thought it was a prop or costume at first. Upon closer inspection, however, I realized the body it was attached to wasn’t garbage.

Worse, I recognized him.

Heart accelerating, I bent down to get a better look at my first goblin challenger, Everglades, lying dead in a muddy puddle. His eyes bugged out of his head, which sat at an awkward angle on his bruised neck.

Last night, Everglades had jumped off a moving train into the street without a scratch. And now here he was dead, choked to death by someone’s bare hands. The marks on his throat looked suspiciously finger-shaped.

Horror melted into fear, and I spun with a splash, my gaze ping-ponging around the alley as I gathered magic, terrified that his killer was lurking. I could *see* many small bits of earth: rats, bugs, and wind-blown, rain-washed trash, but nothing the size of his attacker.

I was alone.

My second instinct was to scream for Scharf, but what was she going to do? What would she say when she saw his blue skin and jagged ears? Did illusions last in death? If someone found me dead, would they see an orange, bushy-haired, pointy-eared goblin or my current peach complexion, smooth blond hair, and normal ears?

I heard footsteps and turned around again, magic at the ready. Scharf and the profiler Fuller walked into the alley.

While the FBI charged in, I stood invisible, surrounded by puddles and a dead goblin.

“I had him,” Fuller said. “I could’ve broken him.”

“That was an interview, not an interrogation,” Scharf replied quietly.

“What is the difference?” Fuller asked, genuinely puzzled.

They stopped just short of the dumpster, and I took a slow step closer to the body with the vague plan of making us both invisible, at least until Fuller skedaddled.

“So, there was no new employee?” Scharf asked. I took another step.

Fuller threw his hands up. “No new employee.”

Carlisle and Melissa entered the alley, and I rolled my eyes. Fortunately for me, they only had eyes for each other.

“And Joe had an alibi?” Scharf asked.

Fuller sighed. “Damned airtight. He was at a bar near his house with three friends watching the hockey game. One of them is a judge in Alexandria.”

“That’s pretty airtight,” Scharf said mildly. “So it wasn’t Joe.”

Fuller shook his head. “The victim was a total wimp, so it could have been anyone.”

“Excuse me?” Scharf asked in a dangerously mild voice.

“He had fits,” Fuller said.

“Epilepsy,” Carlisle added. “He had epilepsy and was not very strong. And he’d been alone in the shop.”

Scharf turned to pace away from the agents, closer and closer to the hand, though she hadn’t seen it yet. I inched closer.

She turned back and I took another step. “Don’t go near any of the employees again.”

“We have a murder to solve, sweetheart,” Fuller said. “We can’t do that if we can’t talk to the potential murderer in case his itty-bitty feelings get bruised.”

Scharf froze for two seconds, and I braced for an explosion, but she took a deep, slow breath. “Do not interview the employees again.”

“He lawyered up anyway.”

Scharf looked down, shaking her head in disgust. And froze.

I winced. I wasn’t fast enough. She took two quick steps toward the dumpster.

I held out a faint hope she hadn’t seen him until she flipped her cell phone to her ear. “Mrs. Henderson?” she said, speaking to the secretary of her office. “Deploy the team and contact Metro. We’ve got another body in the alley.”

Fuller spun, a look of glee on his face. “He’s escalating!” He bent towards the corpse. “Exactly to pattern!”

Scharf looked incredulous. “How can he keep to his pattern exactly and escalate? Never mind.”

She crouched down to get a better look at Everglades’ deformed face. Carlisle enfolded Melissa in his arms and turned her away. “Don’t look, Mel. Don’t look.”

Scharf reached towards the dumpster.

“Stop!” Fuller cried.

Scharf froze. “What?”

“We need to take in the scene.”

Scharf stood up. “We need to call the morgue. We need to post the scene. Again. We need to knock on doors.” She stopped. “Not you. Other people need to knock on doors. It’s the middle of the day. We don’t know how long this body’s been here.”

I couldn’t help feeling a little relieved that her hero-worship had worn off.

Scharf got a closer look at the goblin’s hand. What did she see? I was going to have to go visible to her and ask her. Pressure was building in my brain to keep my word. We’d made a bargain that I would help her with supernatural cases in exchange for a place to live. This case had taken a definite turn toward the supernatural.

*Notice me.* She didn’t even jump; she just sighed.

“Why didn’t you say something?” she whispered once she’d checked that Fuller was busy pontificating about escalation to his adoring audience.

“You were seconds behind me. And it’s… It’s….”

Scharf nodded and looked almost compassionate; she’d been there when I’d seen my first dead body and freaked completely.

Which was why I really didn’t want to tell her what I had to tell her. “I knew him.”

“What?” she said, a little louder than intended.

“Melissa isn’t used to this,” Carlisle said. “She doesn’t have to see this. It doesn’t have to be in her head.”

Scharf twisted around. “Absolutely, please get her out of here!”

Instead, with great, gulping breaths, Melissa walked closer. She didn’t look scared. She looked almost gleeful.

“What do you see?” Fuller said, his hands fisted together under his chin. “Are you seeing the crime in your mind’s eye?”

“Yep. That’s it. I’m totally seeing the crime.” Scharf looked at me, and if looks could kill, there would be two dead little goblins in this alley instead of one.

“Oh, it gets better,” I said.

“Better than that?” Scharf muttered.

“He’s a goblin.”

She grabbed tufts of her short, dark hair with two fists and yanked as hard as she could.

“Looks like your serial killer *was* a magical creature,” I told her.

Fuller crouched on the ground to examine the goblin’s body; evidently, the stopping phase of the investigation had ended. When he didn’t run screaming from the alley, I figured they really couldn’t see what I did.

To me, he looked grotesque and far from human: deep-blue skin, claws, and long, floppy ears. The huge, purpling bruises on his neck and his swollen face pointed to what had happened.

Scharf walked away and called back, “I’m going to meet the units at the front of the alley.”

Carlisle snorted. “By now, they ought to know the way.”

Scharf gave a high-pitched laugh that sounded nothing like amusement and jerked her head from side to side, beckoning me to her.

“We should stay away from this block. It’s deadly,” I said, turning to walk backward to get another glimpse of the hand on the ground, which was all I could see of the goblin from the mouth of the alley.

“I wish.” Scharf glanced back, cursed, and dug her phone out of the holder on her belt. She pressed it to her ear and repeated, “I wish.”

She often spoke to her phone when I was invisible, so it wouldn’t seem like she was talking to herself.

“I know him,” I said. “We can solve this and—”

“Orange, you’re no longer a consultant. You’re a *witness*. Walk me through exactly how you know him.”

“What are you talking about? I just met him yesterday!”

“If you knew the victim and saw him *yesterday*, you might have witnessed the events leading up to the crime. You could be called to the stand. You could be a suspect again.”

All humor fled, and every muscle in my body tightened. The last time I’d been a suspect, I’d ended up in FBI custody getting interrogated about a presidential assassination plot. “I didn’t kill him, Scharf.”

She wheeled away from me. “I know that.”

I jogged so I could face her again. “Do you?”

She was silent for a long moment, and the tension in my body ratcheted higher, cranking my shoulders to my ears.

“Tell me what happened,” she said matter-of-factly.

I crossed my arms. “Should I get a lawyer first?”

She didn’t laugh. “That’s your right, of course.”

I coughed. “Seriously?”

“You weren’t on my couch last night.”

“I was at Juya’s!”

“In the middle of the night?”

“She needed help. I was baking, for God’s sake. Ask her. Since I apparently need an alibi.”

I wanted Scharf to say she didn’t suspect me. Instead, she took a breath and repeated, “Tell me what happened.”

I considered being petulant. But half a dozen crime-scene investigators were about to descend on the alley again, and Scharf was the only law enforcement officer I *could* tell. “I swear I’d never seen him before last night. He confronted me on the subway.”

“Accidentally or deliberately?”

“How can anyone accidentally confront someone?”

She rolled her shoulders. “Did he follow you onto the subway or was the meeting an accident?”

“Oh,” I nodded, trying to quell my panic and concentrate. “Deliberate.”

“And?”

“He said, ‘I challenge thee.’”

“How did you know he was a goblin?”

“What do you see when you look at him?”

She peered down the alley, her brow creased. “Male, White, brown hair, and brown eyes, thirties, less than six feet tall, maybe less than five. Strangled. Huge mullet.”

“Fingernails?”

She shrugged. “They looked like fingernails.”

“Ears?”

She scowled. “I didn’t notice.”

“Well, to me, he looks like a goblin. His skin isn’t white—it’s blue. He has claws and ears like mine.”

“Ears like yours?”

I winced. It was my habit to keep my hair long and covering my ears. Until that moment, I’d forgotten she’d never seen them.

“Show me your ears,” Scharf demanded.

My head felt like it was on a gimbal, shaking violently. “No. That wasn’t part of our deal.”

“Okay, you can stay at my place if you show me your ears.”

“You already bargained to get my help in exchange for my staying with you,” I told her. “You can’t rebargain for something else.”

“You just made up that rule.”

“No, I didn’t. It has to balance!”

“Balance? How about I strangle you and then we’ll be even?”

“Cease attempting humor immediately.”

“Who said I was joking?”

My stomach hollowed, but then Scharf laughed and said, “How does every conversation with you go in circles? The body, Orange. Who was he?”

I sighed and tried to think of the best way to tell this story. Something flickered in the window of the music shop. I peered inside but didn’t see anyone. The window display held a dozen string instruments I didn’t recognize and a half dozen violins that I did. The sign over the door didn’t have English lettering below its Chinese characters.

I wanted to explain to her that talking back and forth about a subject was how normal conversation worked, something she rarely indulged in, but when I opened my mouth, I said, “I claimed the box my father left me.”

“Don’t change the subject.”

“I’m not! I’m trying to tell you how this goblin came into my life. My father passed on his box to me, so I am now the *Peallaidh*. As of a couple of weeks ago.”

“What was in it?”

I scuffed at a bit of ice, cracking it under my feet.

She bit her lip, suppressing a smile, then schooled her face. “You still can’t get it open?”

“Apparently opening it is not a requirement for inheriting it.”

Scharf glanced down the alley to Fuller, who stood with his eyes closed and hands outstretched over the body in the light rain. Was he giving it a blessing? Melissa and Carlisle huddled over her notepad, ignoring us. Scharf turned to me. “So this goblin is connected to your father?”

“Not really? Now that I’m officially the *Peallaidh*, all the goblins in my kingdom get to challenge me to make sure I’m worthy of leading them.”

“All the goblins,” she repeated.

“The goblin last night was the first, the weakest one, so it was a pretty quick fight. We wrecked a subway car.”

Her face went white. “You *fought* with him?”

“Not to the death! And fortunately, he knew a shortcut, so I don’t have to fight the rest of them. I have to go to the top of the heap and fight the most powerful goblin. I mean, the most powerful after me. Hopefully. Then he had to kill himself to restore his honor.”

“Oh, my God.” She hurried away from me, sloshing through the rain to the curb. “Orange, get a lawyer,” she said over her shoulder. “Stop talking.”

I tramped after her, hopping over the puddles. “No, not like that. He didn’t actually die. He jumped out the window.”

She pivoted slowly and stared at me with two round eyes. “Repeat that?”

“But he was fine! And technically, he jumped through the window, not out the window. So there might or might not be a few cuts and bruises on his face that were probably from the window. Or possibly my fists.” I tried to speed through that part. “But he really was fine.”

She didn’t even tell me to stop talking, only looked up into the falling rain as if it would save her.

“Hey, I’ve jumped from higher than that.”

Scharf jabbed her finger at me. “No jumping out of buildings.”

“You know, it looks like you’re pointing at the air.”

She glanced around, but the only other person around was a homeless man sleeping in a bus shelter across the street. Since the riot and Rodger’s murder the night before, the neighborhood was far less busy.

Scharf didn’t move. “Make me a deal. No jumping out of buildings unless you have to.”

I turned to Scharf. “What if I go bungee jumping? Is that allowed? If I have a little rubber band around my ankles, is that okay?”

“Bargain, Orange.”

“What do you have that I want?” I said, perhaps a little too cocky.

“Orange.” It was her FBI voice.

“Fine,” I said. “Deal. I won’t voluntarily jump out of a building unless I have to.”

“Define ‘have to.’”

“Ugh. I shouldn’t have taught you about bargains.”

She tapped her foot.

“‘Have to’ means my life is in danger or someone else’s life is in danger. Okay?”

“Good.”

I crossed my arms. “It wasn’t a building, anyway. It was a train.”

“Any height!” She shouted.

“Okay fine, deal!” Provoking her right now was not the smartest thing I’d ever done.

“You never saw the goblin before he challenged you on the subway. You don’t know who did this. And he was alive when you saw him last.”

“The only…” I trailed off, reluctant to tell her the next part.

“The only what?”

“The only other thing is that I made a deal with him, too. He was going to find the Lubber Fiend for me. The most powerful goblin… The second most powerful goblin. In exchange for letting him jump out the window.”

She narrowed her eyes again; this time they were nearly slits. “So you could’ve stopped him?”

“You had to be there. And they can replace the window.”

“I wasn’t worried about the window!”

“There’s a chance he might have contacted the Lubber Fiend, who might or might not have found him and now is wandering around DC trying to fight me… Find me!”

“My God, Orange.” She paced in front of the music shop and the teashop. I was reasonably sure I saw somebody watching us from inside, behind a row of instruments.

Scharf’s phone dangled from her fingers, forgotten, as she said, “And what about the serial killer? Fuller is in the alley, crediting this kill to him.”

I gaped at her, having momentarily forgotten about Fuller and the others. “I told you the victim jumped through a window out of a moving train. No human could have killed him.”

“That’s not what the top profiler in the country is saying right now.” She paused, the blood draining from her face. “Unless we’re *both* right and it is the serial killer, but he’s a goblin.”

“Or worse.”

“Worse?”

“Something that can kill goblins.”

She threw her hands up and almost dropped her phone. She snatched at it as she said, “I’m so screwed. My career is so screwed.” She turned to me, looking bleak. “I know you said you’d help with this case, but I can’t do this again.”

“You don’t mean that,” I insisted, stunned by her sudden meltdown. We’d gone off a bridge before, and she didn’t even blink. She had the best poker face of anyone I knew. Hell, she’d been to war and back with at least one deployment that I knew about; nothing DC could throw at her would compare.

She stared at me; her dark eyes haunted.

I stopped fretting and started thinking. Scharf was panicking, and she never panicked. I was beating people to a pulp, and I never did that. The top profiler, skilled at breaking psychopaths, was provoking restaurant owners to violence and holding seances over dead bodies. The city had rioted in celebration of a hockey game when it’s normally a challenge to get anyone to even applaud. Juya was sick, and she was never sick. Something was wrong with DC.

The heavens opened on us.

Scharf looked up into the streaming rain. “Oh, that’s mature.”

I crossed my arms. “I don’t do weather.”

“And now the crime scene is ruined!” she exclaimed, then sprinted down the alley.

I wanted to go after her and tell her she was acting crazy—scary crazy—but I didn’t move.

I peered down the alley at the dead goblin, then up at the sluicing gutters. Juya was sick and Juya could control the weather. Scharf’s entire life was dedicated to her job and now she thought she was incompetent.

“Something is really wrong,” I said under my breath.

For the second time in a day, I turned invisible even to Scharf.

# 10

My phone shattered the silence as I ran through the abandoned streets, following my orange line north to Juya. I fumbled in my coat pocket and answered it. “Jack!”

“Hey! You wanna get some food or something?”

“Jack!”

“Are you okay?” he asked, suddenly wary.

“Are *you* okay? You’re a demon!” Jack packed a heck of a lot more firepower and way fewer morals than I. If something was harming the city, would he be immune to it?

“Uh, I thought we’d gotten over that a while ago. Are you all right, Orange? What’s going on?”

“I’m fine. You haven’t…” It probably wouldn’t do our relationship any good if I accused him of accidentally killing anyone in the last couple of days. “Don’t go to Chinatown.”

“Well, yeah, after the hockey riot, I don’t have any particular desire to.”

“Something’s wrong,” I said as I paused at a bus stop. My orange line didn’t end; it dipped into the street near the leaning metal sign. I’d learned not to walk blindly into traffic when it did this and just wait for the bus.

“Okay, but can we still go to dinner?” He said “dinner” with a leer in his voice, and I rolled my eyes, but then a hint of a smile brushed my lips as he added, “There’s this new grilled cheese place in town.”

“You know what? I’m not even hungry.” A number 70 bus heading north wheezed to a stop at the corner moments later.

“You don’t sound okay at all,” Jack said, more alert, more demon. “What did you mean by ‘something’?”

“One sec.” I waved my SmartTrip card at the front of the mostly empty bus to pay and collapsed near the back doors, hopefully out of earshot of the driver. I couldn’t tell him to forget me since I was the only one on the bus. Still, I kept my voice low. “You haven’t noticed anything different lately? About yourself? A short temper? A sudden urge to take somebody out?”

He laughed, and it wasn’t a comforting sound. “Honey, that’s me every day of the week. All it takes is driving on the beltway, and I’m mad enough to murder.”

I sighed. “Don’t tell me that, Jack.”

“But I don’t do it, Orange!”

I watched the watery world slide by and tried to shake off the panic clenching my throat closed, trying to *think.*

Not for the first time, I questioned the wisdom of dating a demon. I tried to tell myself they were simply spirits with the power of humanity, the same power I used when I hobbed. But they had to have gained their nasty reputation somehow.

“I have broken no laws of any country in the last two days,” he added when the silence stretched.

“Funny.”

“On the other hand… We could skip the grilled cheese and you can come over and we can try to break a few.”

My libido stirred sluggishly, but I said, “I have to deal with this. A goblin died in Chinatown.”

“And now that you’re the *Peallaidh*, you think you have to find out who did it?”

“Well, yeah. Plus, I hate rain and I need to turn it off.”

“You need to turn off the rain?” He shifted from demon to worried boyfriend.

“Listen, I have to go.”

“Orange, you’ve never turned down food in your life. And now you’re talking about goblins dying and stopping the weather. What are you—”

“Just don’t—drive on the beltway, okay? And stay inside if you can.”

“No beltway. No Chinatown. No going outside. This would be fun if you were here holed up with me.”

“In fact, maybe get out of town.”

“Get out of town?” He didn’t sound angry; he sounded incredulous. “What have you wandered into?”

“I’ll let you know when I know.”

“If I leave, will you come?”

I wanted to do that, but right now, I couldn’t trust myself to be around anyone I cared about. “I wish.” I hung up, winced, and called him back.

When he answered, I could hear the smile in his voice. “They have smoked cheese in their grilled cheese.”

“Goodbye.”

“What?”

“Not goodbye goodbye. Just, I hung up on you and didn’t say goodbye. Goodbye.”

“Orange, what is going on?”

The bus passed into Columbia Heights and I hauled on the yellow string to signal a stop. “Look, I’m here. Goodbye. Again.”

“Where’s here?”

I hung up before he could say more and pocketed my phone.

I stepped off the bus and jogged through the downpour. Juya’s courtyard was empty, but it heartened me to see that it looked like her normal townhouse and not the derelict building I’d come upon earlier. I pounded on her front door.

No one answered, so I pounded again. I tried to remember what time it was but failed. I’d gotten no sleep last night, and the fatigue was getting worse.

Finally, Osman opened the door. He was dressed in the same jeans and sweatshirt. It was still the same day. It felt endless. He didn’t jibe me about being unreliable or riling Juya and for a second, I forgot why. *Mert.* I looked around frantically.

“He’s gone,” Osman said carefully. “Said something about jumping off a bridge.”

I sagged in relief.

“You’re happy about that?”

“If he’s jumping off bridges, he’s fine.” *I hoped.* I stuffed the shame somewhere deep. “I have to talk to Juya. I don’t have time for witty banter.”

He stepped aside promptly, his eyes empty. I used to hate how he poked at me and never seemed to trust me. This deference was infinitely worse. I ducked into the house and then turned to him.

“Osman, I’m so sorry.”

“*Pasa nada.*” He didn’t meet my eyes. “It’s nothing.”

After a second, I twitched away, not having any idea how to fix this, and made for the stairs.

I knocked on Juya’s door and opened it without waiting for a reply from within. She lay there, wan and listless, dozing against her lace pillows. Someone had pulled her hair into a low ponytail, so she looked less like the invalid I’d seen before, but she was still terribly sick.

I crouched next to her bed. “Juya.” I gently shook her shoulders. “Juya, please. Something’s wrong with the rain.”

Juya’s eyes cracked open. “Orange, is there something you need?”

“No! I’m fine.” But I wasn’t fine. Something was magically killing goblins and screwing with the rain. Or worse, somebody was magically killing goblins and somebody else was messing with the weather. “Juya, please, I know you’re sick, but can you tell me if there’s something in the rain that’s wrong? Is that why you don’t feel well?”

Her eyes slipped closed, and my heart ached at the sight, but I couldn’t afford to let her rest.

“Juya!” I exclaimed.

Her eyes flew open once again, and she said, “I should have thought of it. I could kick myself.”

This time, when she closed her eyes, I didn’t disturb her. I could feel *something* in the air, like a storm gathering. Finally, it dissipated, and she opened her eyes all the way, hunching to sit up. I helped her, stuffing a pillow behind her thin shoulders.

“There’s magic in the water,” she said, before unleashing a string of phrases in an ancient language. As she did, the room vibrated.

Osman burst in but stopped short when he saw Juya.

“What is she saying?” I asked him.

He shook his head. “I’ve been trying to learn it, but it’s not connected to English or Spanish. She might be the only one alive who still speaks it.”

Whatever she was saying, it was working.

Until Osman said, “I don’t think Juya even knows what she’s saying half the time.”

The energy gathering faltered.

“Hush! Don’t listen to him. You know what to do!” I told her, but the flow of words slowed and the electrifying atmosphere around us calmed. It rattled the bones of the house until Juya sank deeper into her pillows.

“He’s pushing on the rain. I am in your debt again.”

I smiled. “The scales are so unbalanced that I’ve given up keeping score.”

“How did you know?” she said weakly. “About the rain?”

“I’m not sure.” I shrugged uncomfortably. “A goblin died in an alley today. I didn’t really know him,” I added when I saw her eyes round. “But it wasn’t a human who killed him—it was something more powerful. And beyond that, everything’s going wrong. Scharf fell apart today, which she never ever does. It feels like I’m making it all up, but—”

“It’s not your imagination. This is nothing I’ve ever encountered before. I’m so angry I let him get to me.”

“It’s a him?”

She narrowed her golden eyes. “I can’t be sure. But I think a female would be a little more vindictive.”

“I thought you would have said nicer or something,” I said, gingerly sitting on the multi-colored quilt.

Juya harrumphed. “Let’s just say that if I wanted to ruin the city, I wouldn’t be so slow about it.”

“Ruin the city? Is that what he’s trying to do?”

Her head lolled, and she forced herself into a sitting position. “That’s what he is *doing*. The world’s turning cold. I don’t like the signs.” She looked at me, and I noticed many of her wrinkles had disappeared. Oh, her skin still looked like old tree bark, but it was a healthy tree again. “I would tell you to stay away, but you won’t.”

“Of course, I won’t. For one, I still owe Scharf, and she definitely won’t stay away. And for another, I gotta stop this thing before you get hurt. More hurt.”

“I’m strong enough.”

Her gray skin and clammy brow belied her words. “Yes, of course, you are.” I looked at Osman. “Is she?”

He said nothing.

I wanted to ask what to do about “him,” but she was already losing the burst of energy she’d summoned. Whatever he was, it was taking all she had to fend him off. I didn’t hold out much hope for myself. “Will you be okay?”

“Absolutely.”

I sighed. “I’m going to come back here and make a deal with you, so I know if you’re lying.”

Through gritted teeth, Juya said, “For a while, I can hold on. If I knew what I was fighting, it would be easier. But *m’hija*, that was not a suggestion for you to go find out.”

“I challenge thee!”

Everyone froze.

“No, not right now!” I basically flew to the window. As I pulled aside the diaphanous pink curtain, I gasped.

Goblins are small. Mrs. Henderson, Scharf’s office secretary, passed for a little person with her glamour on. And I’m shorter than everyone I know except her.

The thing standing in Juya’s courtyard, however, would have to duck through door frames. His skin was leathery and red. His head was wide, and his ears extended several inches on either side, their ragged lobes dangling. He wore jeans and a sweatshirt, and his greasy hair was caked in ice.

“I challenge the *Peallaidh*!”

I squinted. They kept saying that. “Let me guess, the Lubber Fiend?”

He roared.

“I’ll take that as a yes.”

He cracked his knuckles, and I rolled my eyes before closing them to summon my magic.

I found—nothing.

I opened my eyes as the creature’s footsteps sent cobblestones trembling.

I stumbled away from the window and tripped over the rug. I crashed onto my back.

I flipped, human slow and uncoordinated, to my hands and knees and scrambled out of Juya’s room. If he closed in on me, he’d have me trapped up here.

“Lock the door!” I told Osman as he followed me to the threshold. I slammed the door in his face and repeated. “Lock it now!”

I dashed for the stairs, knowing I’d only have seconds to flee. Without my magic, this was going to be a real short fight.

When the Fiend knocked the front door down, I ran out of time. I tried to make it anyway, sliding down the banister.

“I don’t accept!” I cried in desperation and spun around the newel post at the bottom, trying to get to the back of the house and away.

He growled as he got a hand on my jacket, but I wrenched free and sprinted past the kitchen.

“What happens if I don’t accept?” I said, “I just won’t fight!”

With my earth power, I mentally reached for any of the dozens of metal implements in the kitchen. But nothing flew into my hands.

Then I tried my hob powers to tap into human emotion—my own, in this case, since half of me was the only human around. The rage and fear and despair welled up in me but didn’t translate into power of any kind.

The beast jumped the length of the hallway, ratty hair swinging and fingernails raking down my back, but he didn’t get a purchase on the leather. I kept running but had to twist when a burst of flame whooshed by my head. I glanced back.

Fire bloomed between his fingers. The ice crystals in his hair melted in an instant and dripped over the rest of his burly frame.

“Nice trick.”

He thrust his hands at me, and the fire whipped from his fingers. I dove for the floor.

As the Lubber Fiend cackled like the goblin he was, my throat went dry. If he landed any fire on me, it would be all over in a second since I didn’t have my magic. My heart sounded like a hummingbird in my chest. What had happened to my magic? What was wrong with me?

He let loose a blast of flame that singed my hair as I dodged again.

*I’m going to die*, I thought. Hadn’t Everglades said the apex fight was to the death? A *real* death?

As the Lubber Fiend jumped for me again, I dodged again, and he ended up on his face.

I ran. I had no idea if there was a rear door, but most DC townhouses were long and thin with an entrance front and back. I dodged right and left and scrabbled toward the only rear door I could see.

My nemesis roared behind me.

I dashed through and slammed it, muting his growls. I was in a mudroom full of dripping jackets and boots, and I blessed the anemic sunlight shining through an outer door at the back of the house.

“Come on, come on, come on,” I muttered to my magic as I ran for it.

An explosion of lightning and a crack of thunder boomed outside the windows, and the house lights flickered and went out, plunging me into darkness. I looked behind me and stopped.

If I went out that door, he’d tackle me in seconds.

I opened the door but didn’t go through it, and instead threw one of the random shoes from the hallway down the steps into a narrow alley with a few fancy cars parked near the back doors. Then I buried myself amongst the coats. As the inside door burst open and the Fiend flew through, I had time to put one boot in front of my legs.

Could goblins see in the dark? I couldn’t. He passed within inches of me, growling his way toward the exit.

In his misshapen face, I saw the reason goblins were called the beasts of the magical world. The Lubber Fiend looked like a red, marauding water buffalo walking upright.

He paid no heed to the coats or me, though I almost threw up when he kicked the boot in front of me on his way out. He lumbered into the rain and away.

I counted to thirty and then to sixty, nerves shrieking, human senses telling me nothing except that I was freezing and wet and the rain was loud outside the door. Without my magic, I was a sitting duck. No, worse, a sitting human. A duck could have flown away. I forced more oxygen into my lungs, telling myself not to pass out from the fear.

When he didn’t circle back, I slipped out of the coats to slam the back door and hid again, calling on the orange line that magically told me the route to safety wherever I was.

Nothing happened. I didn’t know the way home.

When the inside door opened, Osman found me clutching a boot in front of me like a weapon, piled with coats.

“*Que pasa*?” Osman whispered and then licked his lips and asked in a stronger voice, “What was that?”

I took another gulping breath. “All it wants is me, and I’m going.”

I didn’t know how to get home and I didn’t want to wander the streets with that thing on the loose, so I brought up a rideshare app and requested a driver who happened to be one of the few other humans on earth who knew I was a goblin.

I crouched down amongst the coats. “Go upstairs, barricade the door. I’ll be gone in ten minutes, and you’ll be safe.” I hoped they’d be safe. They had to be safe.

“But…”

“Go!”

Osman retreated, and I tried to figure out what on earth had happened.

I had reached for my magic, something that had glowed steadily in my body since I’d bargained for it when I’d fought the djinni. I’d traded having my magic at my beck and call in exchange for…

“My life.”

I sank into a wet puddle on the floor in shock. I had bargained my life for my magic. So the only conclusion was that I hadn’t been alive the past few days. I hadn’t slept; I hadn’t worked and had snuck around doing nothing I enjoyed or wanted to do. Or even needed to do. Goblins were homemakers. We guarded hearths and played with the minor magics of the world. It was the stuff of life, and I’d ignored all of it. This wasn’t living.

I fought the despair that welled up. Goblins were challenging me everywhere I went; dead bodies were piling up, and an unseen force was making everybody angry. DC was supposed to be a fresh start, but I seemed to slide ever deeper into a hole.

I looked around at the piles of outerwear, clawing my way out of that hole. *I’m not going to die here, doing this.*

# 11

The app dinged, and I flew out the door, thrilled to see a huge black Chevy Tahoe through the sleet.

I’d forgotten about the ice.

My feet slid out from under me, and I slammed into the ground. I lay there, stunned. I’d fallen the same way hours before and had barely felt it. Now, human-weak, the back of my head felt squishy. I couldn’t breathe, and my tailbone screamed.

I heard a car door slam and a shout before Hassan Bishara’s face came into view. He was an Afghan native who’d served as a translator for Scharf’s unit there and now worked for a rideshare company. He was about the same age as Scharf, with his black hair in a trendy cut, now sopping wet, and a puffy black jacket that was quickly getting soaked.

“Orange, you okay?”

“Ow.”

He grabbed my hand, and I tried to sit up but gasped and collapsed down when my diaphragm protested. Fortunately, the movement kickstarted it and precious, freezing oxygen filled my lungs.

On the second try, he got me to my feet, and I fought the whirling swoop of my stomach. “Ow.”

We limped to the passenger side of his SUV, and I sank into the heated seat with a moan. Hassan slid behind the wheel and peeled off his coat. He folded it neatly, water and all, and put it at my feet. Under the coat, he wore a T-shirt with the Star Trek insignia on it. It was his favorite show.

He had just the beginning of crow’s feet around his eyes that usually crinkled when he laughed but were now squeezed in concern. “You wanted to go to Scharf’s, but maybe I’ll take you to a hospital?”

“No! No, I’m a goblin, remember? This will pass.” As soon as I located my magic.

Finally, he turned the key and started into traffic. I watched the rain make little rivers down the front passenger-side window, keeping one eye out for a hulking red giant. I tried to think, but my brain was mush in my skull.

“How’s everything with you?” I asked, mostly to forestall more worrying.

“Everything’s ice.” I thought he was speaking metaphorically, but he gestured at the windshield. “They say it’s the storm of the century.”

I peered out the window at the driving rain and sleet. “The century isn’t that old yet.”

“It was like learning to drive all over again. My daughter, Amina, is *having a ball.* She loves the ice.” Although he spoke with an Afghan accent, his English was almost perfect.
