She tsked and said, “Phone call.” She picked up and put down her receiver, then closed her eyes and muttered something. I felt the air shift as she opened her eyes. “And now I’m invisible to my own coworkers. Thank you very much.”

“How is that my fault?”

“What do you want?” She meant: *go away.*

I took a deep breath, my rage stirring and the edges of my vision going red. Fortunately, my dismay made the red retreat. Mrs. Henderson wasn’t challenging me, and she was Scharf’s administrative support. I couldn’t punch her face in. “Sorry to ruin your fun, but I challenged the Lubber Fiend.”

Her face lost color, turning an unhealthy lime green for a second. “The *Lubber Fiend*?”

“Yeah! The dude who first challenged me said I could go right to the top.”

“You need to practice first! Hone your fighting skills. You can’t start at the top!”

“I don’t want to spend the rest of my life fighting my own kind!” My hands fisted. I shook out my fingers.

“It took your father at least three centuries to get through all the challengers.”

I sat down. Or rather, my legs gave out.

Mrs. Henderson stood up and peered over her desk. “And he was pretty quick about it.”

“Exactly how old was my father?”

She shrugged. “I don’t know. At least a couple of millennia.”

How did I always end up collapsing in front of Mrs. Henderson? I had suspected from my research he was older than he thought, but to hear it confirmed so casually? I would not lie down on the floor or give in to the panic. “I’m going to live forever?”

“Obviously not. Someone finally dispatched your father.” She paused and added, “Sorry to be blunt about it.”

“No, that actually makes me feel better.” I gulped, figuring I was probably heading to a light yellow instead of orange myself. I hoped not.

Someone shouted from inside the office, and I took a step closer to the closed door.

Mrs. Henderson shook a finger at me, sending her gold bracelets jangling. “It’s too late to whine, Orange. You claimed the box. You said, ‘I want my legacy.’ I said, ‘Don’t do it. Think about this.’ But no. You just had to jump in there.”

“Okay, the cat has left the barn. The door is open.” I closed my eyes, trying to remember the cliché, but failing. “It’s happened. How do I defeat him?”

“The Lubber Fiend has an unusual skill. He holds elemental fire, not earth. I think that’s where he got his name.”

“What, ‘Lubber’?”

She nodded. “People called him ‘live by the fire.’ Lubber Fiend. He’s one of the few goblins I’ve ever met who likes flames.”

I examined the backs of my hands, thinking of the djinni. “It’s always fire. We always get burned.”

She arched an eyebrow. “We?”

“Scharf.”

“Well, keep her away from the Lubber Fiend. And keep him away from here.”

The shouting escalated, and I recognized Scharf’s voice. *Scharf* was shouting. At her boss. We had to fix the weather, or she wasn’t going to have a job soon.

Mrs. Henderson worried her top lip. “In fact, you should get out of here now.”

I didn’t move; I merely ran one pointed nail over my knuckles. “How did he die?”

“The Lubber Fiend isn’t dead. You’re going to have to do that.”

I stood up. “I meant my father.”

The gleam left her eye.

“I got a letter telling me about the box,” I said, “saying it was my inheritance upon his death, but that was it.”

She slumped at her desk. “He was here in DC, fighting. He was always fighting. I don’t know who finally beat him. I’m amazed he lasted so long.”

“He never fought anyone while he lived with me.” We’d spent a blissful twelve years, he, my mother, and I in northern California, eating lemons off a tree in the front yard and playing with dolls that moved by themselves. I only figured out months ago that it was my father’s earth powers that allowed us to have lemons all year long and animated dolls.

Mrs. Henderson stared into the middle distance. “Even she wasn’t enough to keep him away from his world in the end.”

“She who? My mom? My mom tried to keep him out of this world?” An old grief stirred up in my gut. I didn’t know if it was sorrow at losing him and her, even though she was currently still alive, or anger at the fact that he’d left me a long time ago. Neither were very safe emotions in this weird ice storm. *Keep it together*, I told myself.

“Listen, we’ve got a bigger problem than that,” I said, aware that Scharf could not yell at her boss forever.

“No one’s bigger than the Lubber Fiend.”

“You don’t understand. I need the autopsies of the two victims.”

“Why?”

I considered trying to explain our theory about the blood alcohol levels but went with something I knew she’d care more about. “The second victim is a goblin.”

She jumped up. “No one’s supposed to die until the final fight. What did you do?”

“I didn’t kill him! He jumped out a window and survived. He seemed thrilled about it. But now he’s legitimately dead. Found behind a dumpster in Chinatown. This is about more than my fight for the throne or whatever I’m fighting for.”

Mrs. Henderson shook her head. “There is no throne, Orange.”

“Thank God for that. So listen, I need the case files from the Chinatown murders.”

“Oh, no.” She crossed her arms. “I’m not doing any more favors for you.”

“We need them. Scharf is here for them.” And judging from a now more ominous silence from the office, I didn’t know if she’d been in a position to get them.

She wagged a finger at me. “I can’t give you those files.”

Now it was my turn to be confused. “Of course you can. Hand them over. *Please*. We’re running out of time. This is way bigger than one grumpy fire fiend.”

Her little green face grew stern. “I may be a goblin, but I’m still a federal employee. And I have a security clearance to maintain.”

“You told me you got this job to help your fellow creatures. Well, your fellow creature needs your help.”

She snarled at me, baring her razor-sharp teeth once again. “You always get me with that one. You always get me.”

“What can I throw in to sweeten the deal? The last time I saw you, you were talking about how you wanted a boyfriend.” A random meet-cute with one of the many goblins after me seemed like a perfect task for my hob powers.

“Don’t you even dare!” She reached up a hand, claws extended like she wanted to go for my throat. I backed up fast.

Her face turned a mottled green. I’d hit a nerve, but even so, she was boiling hotter than I’d ever seen her. She always took delight in poking at me, but never literally with her claws.

“Listen to yourself!” I shouted. “It’s affecting you, too. It has something to do with the storm. I beat a challenger to a bloody pulp. And I don’t do that sort of thing. You want to rip my throat out, and as much as I annoy you, you don’t do that sort of thing either. Scharf is in there yelling. Something’s messing with all of us.”

Her face regained its usual color, and she quietly sat down. “What can do such a thing? What can spur anger? What can ruin weather?”

“I don’t know. That’s why I need the files.”

She sat down and started to dig in an overstuffed filing cabinet next to her desk. “Two alley murders. These aren’t the official case files, only the copies I made as the reports came in. I assume the latest is your goblin,”

She fished out a thick pile of papers and handed them over. My head spun with relief; she was helping me.

“The autopsy reports are in there?”

She nodded. “Don’t screw up, Orange.”

Scharf burst out of her boss’s office, and I was immeasurably relieved to see her gun and badge on her hip. The last time she’d been in there, she’d been stripped of both. Then I saw Carlisle seated at a table in the office she’d burst out of. She headed for the door without looking at me.

“That’s not good,” Mrs. Henderson said.

“Thanks!” I said, dashed to the printer, and grabbed whatever Scharf had printed off. I tucked the files under my jacket and ran after Scharf.

She stood by the elevator, stabbing the down button over and over again.

“What happened?”

“I’ve been reprimanded for unprofessional conduct. OPR is investigating.”

I’d heard the acronym before but forgot what it meant. My confusion must have shown on my face because Scharf said, “Office of Professional Responsibility.”

“But you haven’t done anything! We haven’t even crashed the car.”

“I used inappropriate language interrogating a suspect.”

It took me a second to parse the official speak. “But Fuller did that. Not you.”

“The best defense is offense?” she said as we finally made it to the elevator.

“Offensive is right.”

“Not that kind of offense.” She smiled, but it died quickly. “And I’m off the case. I’m not to go anywhere near the investigation.”

“Oh, no.”

“Oh yes. Fuller and BAU will run everything out of their office.”

“But he could be the one who did it!”

She hit the wall with the side of her fist. “You’re right. The absolute best defense is to investigate your crime yourself. Damn it.”

We got out in the garage and made it to her car, distinguishable from all the other FBI sedans in their reserved parking spots by its color and age. We slid into the car, but she didn’t start it.

“What do we do now?” I asked.

She took a deep breath of her own, staring down at her hands in her lap. “How dare they?”

“We emote. I can do that,” I said, realizing she was in no state to investigate anything. “How dare they!”

“How dare they!” She hit the steering wheel. “To think they could put a mark on my permanent record.”

“Terrible.”

“And… are you humoring me?” she demanded, turning all her built-up rage at me.

“No, Sir! Uh, Madam. Ma’am?”

She deflated in her seat and the energy seemed to leach out of the car. “Worse though, they’re going to go back to Joe and question him again. Sometimes I hate the law.”

“Don’t say that.” I was truly alarmed now. Law was Scharf’s north star. If it was in a procedure, she was fine. “Happy thoughts! Unicorns and rainbows!”

“You’re right. Happy thoughts. Unicorns and what?” She scrubbed both hands over her face like she was trying to take off her skin.

“Rainbows!” I supplied quickly.

“Rainbows. If I could just get to the files…”

“Well…”

“Orange, what did you do?”

“Nothing! Only something.”

# 15

I pulled the stack of files and computer paper out of my jacket and handed them to Scharf. “Don’t ask me how I got them.”

Scharf raised an eyebrow and pointed to Mrs. Henderson’s signature under “Received.”

I widened my eyes, trying to look innocent.

She shook her head and flipped open the first one, chewing on her lip. “This is good. Very, very good.” She slammed her fist against the files before handing them back and turning on the car.

“What’s the rest of those?” I asked, pointing to the printouts I’d swiped.

“The original autopsy reports from the original murders.” She pulled out of her slot with undue haste and rocketed up the parking garage ramp.

“Where are we going?” I asked, clutching the door.

“I need a medical examiner. I know when someone is drunk or not if they’re still alive, but not what it does to their blood alcohol when they’re dead.”

“Pickle them?” I asked. My stomach twisted. “Wait, the medical examiners who cut open dead people.”

“Yep.” She checked her mirrors and sped into traffic.

“The morgue and prison all in one day. This gets better and better.”

The DC morgue was in the basement of another office building south of the National Mall and it was a quick drive with the light traffic and Scharf’s lack of self-preservation.

As she maneuvered south, I asked, “Why are we going to the DC morgue? Don’t you guys have your own? Who you are not allowed to go anywhere near,” I said, answering my question.

Scharf did not comment as she pulled to the curb to park.

The building was new, with the glass-and-metal look that was taking over the city; it went well with the endless cement of the 70s brutalist cement architecture. Not.

In the lobby, Scharf flashed her badge, and I stayed invisible as we headed to the elevators.

I thought of the last time I’d been to the morgue, on the edge of panic at the sight of my first dead body. How much had changed in a few short weeks? I’d seen so much worse than a dead body in a morgue.

The elevator dinged on the bottom floor, and Scharf led the way to the DC Medical Examiner’s Office. Scharf said, “He knows you already, so no point disappearing.”

I nodded, and she clicked open the door. Still, I kept my eyes half-closed until I saw the metal table in the middle of the room was empty.

“The FBI agents!” Wearing a lab coat and weary smile, Dr. Robert Lange spun from his desk at the side of the room. His eyes looked foggy through his tortoiseshell glasses, and his dark skin gleamed under the sickly fluorescent lights. He was probably in his forties, ten years older than Scharf, and one of the definite good guys in my book.

“Dr. Lange. Thank you for your time,” Scharf said, sticking out her hand.

He shook it, then reached out for mine. “Your name’s Red, correct?”

“It’s Orange,” I said before I remembered Scharf had introduced me as Red—and a fellow FBI agent—on our last visit. I winced.

He shrugged. “I knew it was a color.”

I cleared my throat. “You’re good with names.”

“One would hope so. Half of doctoring is remembering what to call everything.” He guffawed.

I blinked. He seemed his same cheerful self, unbothered by the rain. Though I didn’t know him well enough to know if it was an act.

“What can I do for the Bureau? As far as I’m aware, no DC cases cross with you guys. We’ve been very popular the past couple of weeks, but I wasn’t aware of federal involvement.” He was one of the city’s pathologists and normally did not work on FBI cases unless they had federal charges attached.

I frowned. “Very popular the past few weeks?”

He nodded sadly. “We have three more enjoying our hospitality overnight. Normally when the weather turns, I get a bit of a break, but this spree doesn’t care about statistics.”

Scharf tensed beside me. “Spree? A detective I know mentioned the thefts. I hadn’t realized the murder rate was going up.”

“Oh, not your official definition of a spree, I suppose. But fifteen homicides in two weeks match the worst weeks in our history. And that’s not counting the others.”

“The others?” Scharf and I echoed together.

“Ten suicides, thirty accidental deaths. We’ve had to ship a few bodies to Maryland and Virginia.” He gestured to the wall of feet-sized steel doors behind the tables.

“I’m sorry,” I said when the silence stretched.

“Which case were you looking at?” Dr. Lange said, sadness tinging his voice.

“Not at DC one,” Scharf said with no little chagrin.

When she trailed off, I said, “We’d like you to review some files.”

I crossed my arms like she always did and widened my feet, trying to look official. Yes, I was physically stronger than any agent, but no one would know it from looking at me, and I didn’t think I could ever intimidate anyone. Even so, I tried to look professional.

Scharf nodded. “If you can spare a moment.”

“But not a DC case?” There was a wariness in his voice that was missing before.

For all her enthusiasm about consulting with other experts, when faced with the task of unofficially sharing FBI business, Scharf wasn’t finding it so easy.

“The Restaurant Killer,” I answered for her.

Dr. Lange’s jaw fell, and interest lit his brown eyes. “I read in the paper that they’d found another body in Chinatown. It went to your labs, of course. Do you think he’s back?” He leaned forward, rubbing his hands. “Serials are a bit of a hobby of mine, you understand.”

I wanted to tell him I absolutely did not understand, but instead, I nodded, trying to match his enthusiasm. Again I looked at Scharf, whose mouth opened and closed, but nothing came out.

“We’re seeking—second opinions?” I elbowed Scharf.

“Second opinions.” She nodded. “On the autopsy report.”

The interest in his eyes faded to suspicion. “You’re telling me for the first time in the history of the FBI that a federal pathologist wants my help?”

“You see, Dr. Lange—” I started, my tone serious.

“Bob.”

“Bob…” I faded out. Not because I couldn’t come up with a lie. But because I had no idea what would sound plausible to a medical examiner. I glanced at Scharf.

“Bob,” she said, “the folks at the FBI are intrigued about the potential reemergence of the serial killer. And they’re also understandably disturbed that we might have the wrong guy. The problem is, I don’t think we do have the wrong guy.”

Dr. Lange raised an eyebrow. “The one who’s in prison, you mean? John Hastings?”

“Right. Some physical evidence is pointing toward these new murders being the work of the Restaurant Killer. But we’ve… received new information that there may be important discrepancies between the original cases and these, particularly surrounding blood alcohol levels.”

“You’re asking me to do what here?” He retreated a little into the room, back to his desk, and sat down. “Off the books?”

Scharf shook her head. “Not at all. I need fresh eyes, who did not see the original or the current autopsies. As the case agent, I have a lot of latitude regarding how I conduct my investigations, Bob. But yes, it is unusual for me to consult local law enforcement.” She seemed to get over her initial reluctance the more she spoke.

Dr. Lange met her gaze. “Instead of the other way around, you mean.”

“Well, yes. Will you help?”

He leaned back in his chair and laced his fingertips together. “On the one hand, I’d salivate to get my eyes on those reports. As I said, I followed the original case—well, as much as I could from the papers.” He gave us a sheepish grin.

I plopped the files down on the metal table.

“Don’t put those there.” He leaped up and grabbed them off the stainless steel.

Although I told myself I was completely fine, I blanched at what my hand had brushed. I knew it was probably the cleanest table in DC, but my stomach heaved a little.

Scharf said through her smile, “I need someone who knows what they’re looking at. I can read an autopsy report, but not enough to draw different conclusions from the raw data.”

Clutching the files, Dr. Lange glanced from Scharf to me. “I can’t help feeling we’re doing something illicit.”

“Just tell me yes or no. Do those original autopsies match these, in particular, the alcohol levels? Were the victims drunk? That’s all that I want to know. Tell me I’m being a paranoid idiot, and this is exactly what it looks like, and we caught the wrong guy. Please.”

I handed over the two new murder files in bulging folders I’d gotten from Mrs. Henderson.

He stood with paper spilling out of his hands. “A copycat? A copycat that is too good, like the murderer was involved with the original investigation?” Apparently, there was more to doctoring than remembering names.

Scharf nodded once.

He took the stack of paper to his desk. He put the old ones aside and opened the two newest next to each other, one for Rodger Clark and one for Everglades Park. I gulped. I’d looked through the second file but didn’t see anything obvious that said, “This victim is not human.”

When Lange didn’t shout something similar, I took a deep breath. Apparently, when goblins died, we were human enough to fool a pathologist. Everglades was simply a man visiting DC who got strangled in an alley.

Scharf stayed perfectly still, her arms on her hips as the doctor read. I, on the other hand, fidgeted. I wanted to pace but didn’t want to get any closer to the back wall of steel refrigerator doors than I already was. For a second, I considered doing cartwheels down the hallway or something.

“That’s different,” Lange said after a few minutes.

“What?”

“Your first victim had large amounts of phenobarbital in his system. That’s a change.”

“What is it?” I asked.

“A medication, but not very common.”

“He was an epileptic,” Scharf said.

“Oh, yes,” he looked disappointed. “Well, that’s one thing this treats. But outrageous amounts.”

“Deliberately large amounts?”

Dr. Lange met each of our eyes. “Possibly. But no alcohol whatsoever.”

Scharf took a breath. “And the other victim?”

“This is interesting,” Dr. Lange said a minute or two later, switching files.

“Yes?” Scharf asked. “Do they match?”

He glanced up and laughed. “It’s going to take me a little while to figure that out. But the force listed here must be a typo.”

“Which one?” I asked.

“This here. The second victim. Second?” He checked the top of each file. “Yes, the man found behind the dumpster. It must be wrong. No one can generate that kind of force to snap someone’s neck by hand from the front.”

I winced. “What does that mean?”

He looked up at me. “From this, I can only conclude that your murderer was the Hulk.”

I laughed hysterically. I usually tried not to do that because I sounded like a cackling goblin. Scharf’s hand landed on my shoulder and squeezed. Hard.

“I can check into those numbers with our ME,” Scharf said, hand still on my shoulder. “Should we come back later?”

Dr. Lange didn’t answer, just flipped quickly through the old printouts of the original autopsies.

“We can come back,” Scharf repeated.

Dr. Lange held up a finger, switching back to the new murder files. “I would love to dig in more, but at first glance, I can tell you they are likely not the same killer as before, either of them. And not the same killer as each other. And neither had any alcohol or drugs in their systems except the anti-seizure medication in victim one. And every original victim was three sheets to the wind.”

I elbowed Scharf. “See?”

Scharf controlled her excitement, but I could feel her vibrate. “You’re sure?”

“I’ll read through these, make some notes about how I reached that conclusion, but a first-year med student could read these tox reports.”

“But you’re sure they’re not the same killer as each other? Their alcohol levels can’t show you that.”

He smiled briefly. “No. Your first killer was right-handed, while your second was left-handed. Your second was as tall as—as I’ve ever seen—while your first was around the same height as the victim. The first likely knew his killer—the strangulation happened from the front and his fingers are unmarked.”

“No defensive wounds,” Scharf said.

He nodded. “He knew the killer and wasn’t braced. Trusted him—or her.” Frowning, he looked between the two files. “I’ll have more for you tomorrow.”

I got my hand on the door when he said, “Wait a second.”

“Yes?”

“I can also tell you Rodger Clark’s records have been altered.”

Scharf rushed over to him. “What are you talking about?”

“Someone has rewritten parts of these files. The handwriting doesn’t match.”

She cocked her head. “You’re a handwriting analyst?”

“No, but as I mentioned, I do have fabulous attention to detail. Or was that my fabulous memory?” The twinkle in his eyes gave lie to the hubris of the statement. “Regardless, this writing isn’t from the same hands. Someone has altered this first file.” He pointed. “Here, here, and here.”

Scharf leaned over his shoulders. “Changed what?”

“Mostly about blood. About bruising patterns and things like that. A pity. It was done well. I can’t tell what it originally said.”

“Why?” I asked.

“I couldn’t speculate, but someone involved in the original investigation did not want anyone to see the original autopsy.” He rubbed his eyes, knocking his glasses off the bridge of his nose. He replaced them as he said, “I’ll mark those places. And then I trust you’ll take my conclusions far, far away from me.”

“Absolutely.”

Scharf met me at the door, and we got out of there.

“How do you think he managed to change the file?” she asked.

“Fuller? I don’t know, magic?”

She shuddered. “Don’t even joke.”

I laughed again but gnawed on my lip to stop. “I wasn’t.”

Like our last visit, we took the emergency stairs to the main level. Unlike last time, I wasn’t hiccupping or crying.

Like last time, Scharf stopped halfway up the stairs. “*How* did he do it?”

I sat on the stairs, watching the stripes on her jaw get redder and redder. She was clenching her teeth, angrier than I’d ever seen her. I wanted to tell her to take a deep breath but instinctively knew that would only make things worse.

“Not how, but when could he have done it?” I asked.

“That’s not the question.”

“It’s not?”

She shook her head. “*Why* did he alter the autopsy reports?”

“You know more about this stuff than I do.”

“Dead bodies?” A glint of humor momentarily returned her face to its normal color.

“Bureaucracy.”

She sat down beside me. “Fair enough.”

“Let’s go backward,” I said. “We know this ended with Mrs. Henderson giving me altered files. It probably wasn’t Mrs. Henderson. No reason for her to change bruising patterns.”

“No.”

“So, where did they start?”

She rubbed her hands together. “The ME generated the reports at our lab in Virginia during and after the autopsy. It wasn’t him, because he could have altered it as he generated it, and nobody would have known.”

I nodded. “And from there?”

She shrugged. “He uploads it to the case file for the agent in charge. Mrs. Henderson prints it out and puts it in the physical files. The prosecutor will also have access to that online file.”

“Who is the agent in charge?” I asked, suddenly afraid of the answer. “What happened after I left the first crime scene?”

“It was me. They were my files.”

I stopped. Everything in me stopped. “What?”

“Orange, breathe. I didn’t attend the autopsy. I had that damn dumpster to deal with.”

“Who did?” I asked, but I knew the answer.

“Carlisle. He volunteered.”

“And?” I prompted her when her jaw kept looking angry.

“And Fuller.” She stood up abruptly and slammed a hand into the wall. “Damn it!”

I jumped to my feet again. “Hey, deep breaths.”

“The fork, Orange.”

I looked around. “What fork?”

“The one in the victim’s eye.”

I rocked on my heels. “Um, what about it?”

“It wasn’t from *Pasta Joe’s.*”

“They had a ton of forks!”

“Not fancy compostable ones.”

I nodded, remembering piles of plastic forks in the back of the restaurant. None of them had been compostable. And how did I know what a compostable fork looked like? “The hockey game!”

Scharf hit the wall again. “I knew it looked familiar. It was your chili dog! It was the same fork!”

I physically recoiled, bumping my head into the plastic emergency instructions that rattled against the wall. “I didn’t do it!”

She growled. “I didn’t accuse you! An entire stadium full of people had those forks.”

I closed my eyes. “And Fuller was at the game.” I opened my eyes. “What are we going to do?”

She shook her head. “I’m going to Quantico and get a sample of his handwriting.”

I tried to buck myself up for a field trip. “Yay.”

“But first, we are going to the arena to get a fork. And then you’re going to make a little bargain with the foremost profiler in the FBI.”

# 16

In minutes, we were rocketing up Seventh Street into Chinatown between an endless row of fast-casual restaurants for tourists interspersed with high-end steakhouse chains and fusion restaurants for the office workers in the buildings above.

But before we got to the arena, we hit the crowd.

People trudged north up both sidewalks. They were not the usual DC denizens in suits and business casual grabbing lunch or indulging in DC’s weird cupcake obsession. These people were in jeans and puffy jackets for a long stay outside, marching in lockstep. As I passed, one blond woman unfurled a banner with angry red letters on it. I couldn’t see what it said.

Scharf turned off the radio but didn’t stop driving. She fumbled for her phone and hit speed dial.

“I’m rolling up Seventh and there’s a crowd of people gathering. What’s the deal? Okay. Okay.”

I strained but couldn’t hear the guy on the other end. She dropped the phone and was forced to slow the car as the crowd swelled and poured into the street in front of the arena.

“What is it?” I asked, my eyes on another banner, trying to make out the words.

“Protest. They have a permit. But not for blocking the street.” She wrenched the wheel over, waving a hand at the crowd to get out of her way so she could get to the curb.

“Protest of what?”

She rubbed her temple. “Freedom?”

“Wait. They’re protesting freedom or protesting for freedom?” I thought about it for a second. “Or other people’s freedom?”

“No one will ever figure that out.”

“What are you gonna do?”

“Find the leaders and get them out of the street before we have another riot on our hands. They started on the Mall and some genius said to take to the streets.”

“I take it DC doesn’t get freedom?”

“Stay in the car.” She dug a radio out of the glove compartment with an earpiece attached to a squiggly wire. Things were never good when Scharf broke out the squiggly wires.

“I could help?” I would be less vulnerable than she was out there, and my hob powers might calm some of them down.

She paused for a second, meeting my eyes carefully. “Stay in the car.”

She opened her door, and the roar of the crowd flooded in. She slammed it and jogged off. I sunk in the seat, grateful for the tinted windows as the marchers flowed around the sedan, rocking it occasionally and sending my heart rate soaring. But they were focused on moving north.

Then somebody crashed into the trunk of the car hard enough to shove it forward an inch.

“I spoke too soon!” I twisted around. “Get off! This is an official…”

The words died in my throat as I got a glimpse of a dark red torso through the rear window. It bent down, and the Lubber Fiend leered at me. He thumped a fist into the trunk, and I screamed, “Not the car!”

I reached for magic, thrilled to find it come at my call, and stumbled out my door into a burly dude with a beer gut who was pretty surprised when I didn’t budge at his shove. He sprawled to the cement instead, taking a few more protestors with him. His face turned purple, and he started to bellow. I pulled on my hob powers to calm him down.

Nothing happened. Hob magic drew from the emotions around me, and there was no calm anywhere near here. I couldn’t create something out of nothing. “Crap!”

I dodged around the car and into the street with a vague plan of fleeing again when the Fiend slammed a huge fist into my back. I went flying and only landed on my feet with a pull of magic. My kidneys ached and I groaned. He was *so* much stronger than me.

A line of people marched in front of me, and I had seconds out of his sight to decide whether to stand and fight him or run.

I dove into the crowd behind me, losing sight of the Fiend in seconds. I twisted and turned, working my way deeper between people with a vague plan of hiding somewhere until he lost me like last time.

I was almost to the other side of the crowd, which had slowed to gather around the Chinatown arch when somebody yanked on the collar of my jacket. I windmilled backward into a hard chest and twisted to see the Fiend towering over me.

“I challenge thee!”

“Crap!”

In the dimness of Juya’s hallway, he had been terrifying. In the light of day, the Lubber Fiend was an order of magnitude worse with grotesque features and deep maroon skin. His stringy dark hair fell over his extremely muscled body and a ragged pair of jeans. His face looked like a caricature of an ugly goblin with a bulbous nose, huge ears, and crooked, pointed teeth.

I had no doubt his fingernails were claws too, but they were currently tangled in my jacket, so I couldn’t see. I spun until he lost his grip and hissed, “Not now!”

Anywhere but here and now.

“Here!” the Lubber Fiend said, flexing. “The site of my first victory in this land.”

I saw red. Literally. A haze drenched my vision. “What did you say?”

The Fiend pointed toward the arena. “I defeated your minion!”

“It was *you*,” I growled, not recognizing my voice. I leaped closer to him; I was half his size and didn’t care. “*You* killed Everglades.”

A boy with a baseball bat staggered into me. I stole it from his hands with magic and resisted the urge to keep it. I tossed it up on an awning so nobody could use it and turned back to the Fiend. “*You* snapped his neck!”

“Did I snap it?” He shook his head like a confused dog.

I couldn’t tell if that was a denial or surprise at his accomplishment.

The ugly chanting dropped off, and the crowd jostled. I looked left to see a line of police.

“Oh, great.” Because that was all this situation needed.

I was buffeted by another young guy in a messenger service uniform who looked scared. I pushed him towards the edge and fought to stay near the Fiend.

I grabbed a greasy hunk of his hair to get him to look at me. “Why? He was just delivering my message. I thought only I was in danger in these challenges! Why hurt him?”

He looked a little chagrinned. “Well, I didn’t mean to. It was so much power.”

“You didn’t mean to? Meaning you did kill him.”

He nodded.

The moment he confirmed it, I lost the battle for control. I leaped for him, straight into a fist to the chest, and bounced to the ground.

A loud bang cut through the noise of the crowd and bitter, stinging smoke filled the intersection. I used magic to keep it away from my face as I scrambled up and leaped again.

This time, I got in a punch before a spout of flame from his hand passed my face. I was so far beyond caring that I didn’t even duck, just hardened my skin against the heat and clawed at his eyes as his flames licked over me. They lit up the people around us through the smoke, now all either pushing toward the police or away.

But this wasn’t like any other fire. This was goblin fire, and I was a goblin. It seared my cheek and my neck. I screamed and landed on my hands and knees in a puddle of freezing, oily water. In agony, I crumpled.

The ice cracked at the edge of the puddle from my fingers and knees as I used my magic to roll away with a groan and poured energy into my bones, cooling my skin, but not fast enough. It drained power I didn’t have to lose. I hadn’t done nearly enough living to get the magic to fix first-degree burns and fight an enraged goblin twice my size.

“Do not accuse the best of us of dishonor and expect to live,” he said with a fist on his heart.

Despite my hurts and my human strength, I spat at him. “You killed him!”

The Lubber Fiend got his hands around my neck. “And this place will help me kill you too.”

He kept saying things like that. I fought for air and used a burst of magic to peel his fingers off my throat. I rolled away, ignoring orange lines streaming almost in a circle away from the Fiend, and asked, “*Why* this place?”

“Can’t you feel it? Like all the magic in the world is being sucked away?”

The sudden hope I felt was disorienting. “You know what it is. Causing all this? What is it?”

“Hold still!” He batted at me. “Not spirit. Not beast. Something in between. Something… Not of our world.”

I fought my anger and my pain. I had to keep my head clear. “*Where* is it?”

He scratched his head, a familiar goblin gesture, incongruous with the hatred in his gaze. Then he waved his hand toward the east. “The eye is over there. I had to get close to find the most power.”

“Whose eye?”

“The eye. The center?” He was only confused for a second before he went for me again. I dodged away and landed on my feet. The street was clearing, and I had a little bit more room to work.

“Where in actual geography?”

He shrugged. “Where I killed him.”

“The source is a dumpster in an alley?” I asked, deflated. Then a sudden light dawned. “Not the alley, the pasta shop. Is it in the pasta shop? Whatever is doing this?”

The Lubber Fiend reached for my neck. He’d made up his mind.

His hands met my burned skin, and I lost my footing as pain flared white-hot along my collarbone.

“Get off her!” somebody yelled.

I heard a shot and the fiend reared and roared. The crowd erupted, screaming to get away. Behind the fiend, I glimpsed someone with the biggest shotgun I had ever seen.

I curled in the fetal position, terrified I would be next, but no second gunshot followed.

I rolled on something hard and pulled it out from under my ribs. It was a bright green toy bean bag that looked like a jellyfish with a tiny head and little strings dangling off it for legs. “What the?”

I looked up. Carlisle stood cradling the huge gun, a pouch of the little green bean bags in his hand. It was a bean bag gun.

“Carlisle?” I asked as he lowered the gun, oblivious to the screaming crowds.

He glanced at me. “Orange? Scharf’s shadow? I should have known.”

The fiend erupted off the pavement, one arm dangling, and tackled Carlisle, who collapsed beneath him in a crumpled heap.

I leaped on his back, forcing all of my magic into his bruised shoulder.

The fiend screamed, drowning out the protest and the pounding feet and everything else. He reared up and lumbered away, sparing me only a snarl on his way past.

I bent down to Carlisle, straining to turn him over. He was limp.

I jammed my fingers to his throat, trying to find his jugular. That was an easy feat in a CPR class that does not at all prepare you for a floppy, unconscious man in the middle of a riot. I heard the crash of glass, leaned over to shield his face, and felt a whoosh of breath on my cheek. He was not moving, but he was not dead.

As the crowd scattered, I heard the march of boots and used the last of my strength and my one good arm to drag Carlisle out of the middle of the street to the gutter by a bus stop and told the world to forget us both.

<<<>>>

It took my breath away how quickly the street turned into a crime scene, the riot gear giving way to technicians and ambulances. When the level of energy vibrating around us had subsided to merely irked, I took a deep breath, regretting this already, and went visible. “Over here! Hey! Help! He’s an FBI agent!”

A medic in a blue uniform jogged over. So did Detective Lee in a bright puffy coat, clearly new. I was so used to the trench coats it took me a moment to recognize him.

“Orange Sinclair,” he said sadly.

I shook my head, “Oh no, you’re not arresting me again. I was saving him! And I got hurt, not the other way around.” I lifted my burned shoulder. The pain had dimmed considerably, but it still looked pretty ugly.

“Orange!”

I glanced over as Scharf came running. She’d acquired another bean bag gun too, or maybe she had the real thing. I couldn’t tell the difference.

“I’m okay!”

“What happened?” She looked me up and down, lasering in on my shoulder. “I told you to stay put!”

“She’s with you?” Lee asked, his arms crossed.

Scharf pivoted, keeping her gun pointed down and away from both of us. “Lee?”

“I need a stretcher here!” the medic said, and Scharf looked down.

“Carlisle!” She crashed to her knees, expertly jamming two fingers into his neck. She looked between the two of us. “What happened?”

Lee looked at me, arms still crossed.

“I don’t know! I…” *fought a giant red goblin that Carlisle saved me from.* “I found him on the ground and tried to keep him safe.”

“And your arm!” Scharf said, pivoting to me and peering at the angry flesh on my shoulder. It had gone from blistered to bad sunburn in the past few minutes, which meant I’d be okay. Not that the screaming pain was any sign. “Something hit it. Something really hot. I don’t know.”

“*Something* really hot?” Scharf asked.

“What are you doing here? Both of you?” Lee asked.

We jumped out of the way as a medic rolling a stretcher joined the one on the ground, and they heaved Carlisle onto it.

“I should probably go with them,” Scharf said with no enthusiasm.

“He hit his head when he fell,” I told her. “He’ll probably be okay.”

“I thought you found him on the ground,” Lee said.

“From the goose egg on his head, that’s what I am guessing! I’m not a suspect. I’m a consultant with the FBI. I was trying to help.”

“What happened?” Scharf asked.

Lee looked around. “We all got pulled in.”

I followed his gaze. Most of the wounded were getting loaded into ambulances or getting taped up outside the open doors of one. More law enforcement milled around, questioning people, dealing with a few in handcuffs.

“Let’s do what we do,” Scharf said.

I cocked my head at her, like she often did to me, summoning her away. I stepped under the awning of an Irish pub. Lee shook his head but didn’t follow.

“What is it, Orange?”

“You can’t do what you do. We have to go.”

“Orange, the fork can wait. Fuller can wait.”

“I know who it is!” I said, resisting the urge to tug on the sleeve of her jacket to get her to listen.

“Who?”

I shook my head. “Not the murderer. The…” I pointed to the now completely smoke-filled, bloody street. “The this.”

# 17

As we walked, I peered into every doorway and at every person who passed us, ready and waiting for them to produce a gun and shoot me, or each other, or do something else crazy. I didn’t know exactly what was causing this run of crime and death, but we were heading right into its lair. Anyone who spent any time here would be affected.

My shoulder pulsed with each step, but the bubbled skin was numb now, soothed by the icy rain and my shock. My heart beat fast in my chest and the edge of my vision pulsed, but I willed myself not to pass out as we jogged by restaurant after restaurant toward the pasta shop.

Scharf seemed oblivious to her surroundings, but that couldn’t be true. As soon as I had that thought, I saw she was scanning constantly, her hand by her weapon. The usually fidgety energy that infused her every second of the day had focused. She was the only person I’d ever met who calmed as a crisis approached.

“So it’s some kind of creature?” Scharf asked.

“Yes. That’s making everything happen.”

“But you don’t know which creature?”

“We don’t come with luggage tags!”

She took a deep breath and relaxed her hand by her side. “So I just walk in there and ask Joe if he’s doing magic that’s wrecking the city?”

“Yes?” I glanced up into the spitting sky. Was I supposed to fight a creature who could do *this*? That was not going to go well. But if not a fight, how would we get him to stop?

When my nails pierced my palm, I realized I was wringing my hands. I had forgotten to cut the tips, and they’d grown into points. I wiped at the smear of blood and shoved my hands in the pocket of my leather jacket.

We didn’t have to fight. We just had to find out what he was trying to get with this stunt and give it to him in exchange for… all the music and sunshine in the world. “What do I have that is worth *that*?”

“What?” Scharf asked.

I shook my head and sped up.

We jaywalked across the intersection at the end of the street of restaurants to approach the shop. This far from the arch, there were only a few fleeing protestors. We even passed a few workers in suits with lunch in their hands, normality clinging to the edges.

One guy in a black trench coat stepped out of Pasta Joe’s as we made it to the door. He held it open for us to duck inside.

The air smelled of tomato and basil. Of the dozen tables spread around the battered faux linoleum floor, half were full of people slurping pasta. Their conversations blended with clinking cutlery and shifting chairs, a strangely muted soundtrack to an Italian restaurant. But this was how all restaurants sounded now that streaming music was so unpredictable.

A young waitress wearing a black apron over a checked red dress that looked disturbingly like the tablecloths skidded to a stop in front of us. She had a drink tray tucked under one arm. “There’s no hostess today. Just sit down, and I’ll swing by soonest.”

“We need to talk to Joe.” Scharf flashed her badge, cupping it discretely in one hand.

“Oh, no,” she paled as she looked at it. “He said he won’t talk to the police again.”

“I need to hear that from him,” Scharf said, unperturbed.

The girl nodded and hooked a thumb towards the swinging red doors to the kitchen that I’d snuck out of the last time we’d been here. When Joe had been throwing pots out of windows. *Happy thoughts, Orange.*

“I hope it’s good news,” the waitress said. “Because I can’t keep making excuses for the cursing coming from the kitchen. He doesn’t understand that we can *all* hear him out here now that the sound system doesn’t work.”

Scharf nodded and headed towards the back. A skinny kid in a misbuttoned plaid shirt stood up from one table with two untouched plates of spaghetti in an unnaturally red sauce on it. He thrust a phone in our faces. “Are you with the police? Could I maybe get a quote?”

Scharf looked him up and down. “No comment.”

He turned to me. “Are you with the investigation?”

Scharf took a firm grip on my upper arm and hauled me towards the back.

“I have a true-crime podcast and we’re doing—” the kid shouted as Scharf towed me through the red swinging doors.

Joe stood at a stove with two huge bubbling pots spouting bursts of steam. I tried to keep my eyes off the spot at the back of the kitchen where Rodger had lain.

He glanced up. “No customers back here. Didn’t you see the sign?”

I had, in fact, not seen the sign and opened my mouth to say so when Scharf said, “I’m here from the FBI.”

“Get out,” he said, brandishing a spoon. “I got myself a lawsuit against you people.”

“I just—”

“I take the sixth!” he bellowed.

I blinked. “Don’t you mean the fifth?”

Scharf shrugged. “You assert your right to a trial before a jury of your peers? We can make that happen.”

“Of course, you know what the sixth amendment is!” I said to Scharf.

“GET OUT!”

“I’m not with the FBI,” I said quickly.

“Then you’re a customer and you’re not allowed!”

I stepped forward instead. “I’m with a true-crime podcast. I want your side of the story!”

Belatedly, I yanked my phone out of my pocket and waved it at him.

“Oh really?” he asked, spinning, looking a little bit interested. When boiling, weirdly pink pasta sauce flicked from the end of the spoon, I used my new water powers for the first time in self-defense to divert them away from my face. Unfortunately, I didn’t stop to think where they would land instead, and Scharf hissed as they hit her neck and her gray suit jacket.

I winced and tried to pull them back, but she was outside my little umbrella of control. She wiped at her collar and cocked a head at the swinging doors. I nodded, and she disappeared.

I spun back to him, scrambling for a question, struck again by the fact I could be talking to the most powerful magical being I had ever encountered in my life. Or a human who was at ground zero of his powers. Neither one would be exactly calm. “I have, um, a podcast about, um, magic and crimes.”

This did not impress him. He harrumphed and turned back to his pots, tipping one into a stainless-steel sink beside the stove. Little snakes of pasta and boiling water sluiced into a colander, sending a mushroom cloud of steam into the kitchen.

“Deal,” I began, “You answer my questions, um, truthfully, and I’ll…” I had no inspiration whatsoever for what he could want.

“You’ll what?”

“I’ll leave,” I said, naming my fervent wish.

He sniffed. “Good deal.”

The chime sounded and I gaped. I’d never had a bargain start so easily.

“Well?” he demanded.

“Right! Um, have you experienced any kind of unusual happening here?”

“No questions about how I killed the kid?”

“Um…” I replayed my conversation with Scharf about Joe. “Don’t you have an alibi?”

He stopped stirring. “Yeah!”

“Well, then you couldn’t have killed him?” I held out both hands, trying to look professional. “Yay?”

“Yeah! I mean, no, I couldn’t have!” He tossed the colander up and down to get all the water out and left the noodles in the sink to go back to the sauce. “I wouldn’t have either. He showed up to over half his shifts. Kept having damn seizures the rest of the time, but you don’t get that kind of reliability every day!”

“You sure don’t,” I responded when he looked at me expectantly. When he didn’t say anything else, I said, “Sooo, back to my question. Unexplained? Homicidal urges, perhaps? Storms? Magic? Miracles?”

When I said miracles, he paused, but then shook his head and grabbed an aluminum spice shaker from a shelf above his head and poured a spectacular amount of a dark dried herb into the sauce pot.

“Miracles?” I repeated.

He shrugged. “Granny was hardcore Catholic. Insisted she saw a miracle every other day. I ain't ever seen one.”

“What miracles?”

He snickered and turned to me, propping one hand on the stove and the other on the pink-smeared white apron spread across his girth. “You see, we bought this metal thing you add to a toaster that puts Jesus on the side of your bread?” He scratched his nose, cursed, and retreated to a hand sink by the door. I tried to keep my surprise off my face at his level of food hygiene and my horror at where he was standing so casually, right where the body had stretched. As he scrubbed his hands, he shouted, “I always thought the toast looked more like John Lennon than Jesus. You know, from their later period. Like *Abbey Road*.”

I couldn’t summon a picture but said, “Sure.”

He returned to stirring the pot. “My brother added that thing to her toaster every three or four months just to watch her declare another miracle.”

The story was fascinating, but not the kind that would lead to a city in chaos. “How often did your mom come down here?”

“Here? Hell, never. She didn’t want to cross the bridge to Jersey, let alone all the way down here.”

I sagged. “So no actual miracles?”

“Oh, I think God pulled one. We didn't know ‘til she died that every time she saw Jesus, she gave another thousand bucks to that priest down the street who’d been diddling kids during confession for years.”

“Ouch!” I said, feelingly. “I mean, I’m sorry. My listeners will be so fascinated.”

“Half our money went to toaster Jesus.” He started stabbing at the pasta sauce rather violently, sending splatters flying. I had to do some more water defense but directed them toward the floor. His face heated, matching the sauce. “That’s the only miraculous thing I ever seen.”

Worried about the pile of sauce I was creating, I rocked backward, and a chime sounded. Technically, I had started to leave, and the bargain thought it was fulfilled. “Thanks for your help!”

I backed towards the swinging doors before he threw another pot. Everything I’d heard pointed to the fact that he was a victim of this phenomenon, not its instigator.

I told everybody not to notice me and slid under the swinging doors like I’d done on my last brief visit so I could avoid the real true-crime podcaster. I looked around the dining room, and I was shocked to see Scharf greeting Hassan Bishara just taking off his coat by the front door.

“Hey!” I said as I dashed over. Scharf nodded, but Hassan didn’t. I started to get offended before I remembered I was invisible.

I squinted, trying to get only the two of them to notice me and nobody else. It was possible, but it wasn’t easy.

“Hey!” I tried again.

“Orange!” Hassan said with a smile. “Good afternoon.”

“What are you doing here?” I asked. “I mean, it’s nice to see you.”

“My daughter is next door. She takes piano lessons.”

I vaguely remembered that Mrs. Bao taught piano.

“Best teacher in the city,” Hassan said and then sobered, his gaze pinging between the two of us. “Please, what’s wrong?”

“Umm,” Scharf began.

He looked at me. “Some of your problems?”

“Probably?” I said, my voice unnaturally high.

“It’s okay.” Scharf looked around at, for all intents and purposes, a quiet afternoon at an Italian restaurant. “It’s okay.”

“Should I get my daughter?” The fear in his voice tipped it an octave higher than usual.

“Wouldn’t be a bad idea,” Scharf said.

“It’s not an emergency,” I added, “Or anything like that. Probably. But maybe not a bad idea to be as far away as possible from here as soon as you possibly can… If possible.” I shut up when Scharf elbowed my side.

He just nodded. I realized he was like Scharf: the worse the situation, the calmer he got. It made me slightly sick to know how he learned to do that. He shrugged back into his jacket and ducked outside.

We followed him out in time to watch him enter the teashop next door to a tinkle of chimes. Scharf ducked under the awning of the music shop next door, and I followed her to get out of the downpour. It was tipped slightly towards rain this morning instead of sleet, which didn’t hurt so much when it hit your face but got just as miserable fast if you stood out in it too long and let it soak you.

Scharf opened her mouth, but I held up a finger and dug for my phone.

I called the one person I thought might have any expertise in miracles. A demon.

“Jack?”

“Do I now have to flee to the East Coast or stay off the third floor?”

“The third floor of what? Never mind. What do you know about miracles?” There was no answer. “Jack?”

“You're asking *me* about miracles?”

I shrugged, fighting the fear in the pit of my stomach. “Do they have anything to do with the weather or music?”

“Depends on the saint.”

“But there’s no saint of weather *and* music? Who maybe had a fondness for pasta?”

“Cecilia is the patron saint of music,” he said slowly. “And she was Italian, so one assumes she ate pasta. Third century. Nice lady. Untemptable.”

“Jack.” My stomach dropped to my toes. I realized I did not want him to explain, nor did we have the time. “What about the weather?”

“Sorry, I don’t remember.”

“Medard,” Scharf said quietly.

I looked at her, remembering she’d gone to Catholic school.

“The patron saint of weather is Medard,” Scharf said louder. I sighed and swiped at my phone to put it on speaker.

“The FBI lady is right,” Jack confirmed.

“Don’t call her…”

Scharf put a finger on my arm. “Did you know him?”

“Never had the pleasure,” Jack said. “But if I remember right, an eagle sheltered him from a storm. Don’t know why he was sainted when it was the eagle doing all the work…”

Scharf snickered.

“What was the last one you asked about?” Jack asked.

“Miracles,” I said.

He guffawed. “Orange!”

“What?”

“That’s the definition of a saint. Miracle worker. They all did miracles.”

“So this Cecilia and Medard never met each other?” I asked, but I knew this was a dead end.

“No,” Scharf said. “Different time. Country. Not possible.”

“Okay, thanks. Just out of curiosity, when you said untemptable…”

No answer. I glanced at my phone. He’d hung up on me. I sighed. “Goodbye, Jack.”

“Miracles?” Scharf asked.

“Joe mentioned something about his Catholic granny. And how Jesus toast got all their money.”

“What? Never mind.” She crossed her arms. “Am I supposed to continue to ignore the fact that you’re dating a demon?”

Everything in me got a little colder. “Look, I know they have a reputation.”

“Reputation is one word for it.”

“But honestly, all your priests do terrible things, too. I don’t think it’s fair to write off somebody because of what they are. I think we should wait for them to steal an old granny’s inheritance because of Jesus toast and then judge them.”

“Orange…” She sighed and cycled through a couple of different things to say before she settled on, “Okay.”

“Okay? That's it, just okay?”

“You have a point. I know a bunch of *people* who might as well be demons. I will drop it, pending further evidence.”

“Thank you!” I thought through her statement. “Hey!”

“Are we dropping it or not?” she asked.

I rocked back on my heels. “It’s dropped!” I squeegeed the water out of my hair, an almost automatic gesture by this time, and said, “Maybe I should go ask the waitress. But she didn’t strike me as a massive magical creature that can cancel all the music in the city.”

Scharf looked up at the teashop and said, “Music…”

“Or, you know, start an ice storm for fun.”

“Like maybe the music from a piano teacher?” she asked as a businesswoman exited the teashop with a coffee cup in one hand and a cell phone in the other.

We listened to the chimes tinkle and watched them dance as the door closed on the shop where, at this moment, Mrs. Bao was teaching the daughter of one of Scharf’s dearest friends.

“She seemed like a nice old lady,” I said as Scharf’s hand crept toward her weapon. I thought of Juya and her ability to manipulate reality at a fraction of the power that she was used to working with. “Though I would be careful of nice old ladies.”

Scharf hesitated at the door. “Mrs. Bao knows I’m with the FBI. I shouldn’t go in there.”

She slid sideways to look in the front window.

I followed, resisting the urge to clutch at her hand and keep near her. I realized we had to be getting closer because my emotions had gotten more volatile. Inside, I could see David by the coffee urn, and in the back at the end of the long counter, Hassan stood talking to Mrs. Bao.

“Anything?” Scharf asked.

“I don’t have super eyes.”

“Don’t approach while she’s near Hassan. Let’s just keep everybody calm. When Hassan and Amina are clear, do the same bargain with her. Let’s see if we can finally get some answers.”

“You want me to go in there alone?”

She glanced at me. “What did you think I meant when I said, ‘I shouldn’t go in there?’”

I thought she’d meant neither of us should go in there. I didn’t want to go in there. I wanted to move. We could all just move and leave this city to the creatures who want to freeze it and silence it.

I took a deep breath and opened the door to the cascade of chimes.

Inside, I looked around, scanning for a creature, magic, or *something.* The floral, grassy scent of dried tea overpowered my senses.

Clicking drew my eyes to the tables opposite the counter where a group of Asian ladies sorted through mahjong tiles. I had learned to play the game at my mother’s nursing home from an old woman who remembered nothing more recently than her high school graduation in 1968, but who never lost a game. She played the Americanized, simplified version of this insanely complicated game, like poker on steroids with tiles instead of cards. I could not play like these women, flicking tiles in blazing-fast play, but the familiar sound was comforting.

Now that I was inside, I saw a little girl who looked a lot like Hassan standing between him and Mrs. Bao near the stairs at the back of the restaurant. She had to be younger than ten, wearing pink jeans and a puffy blue rain jacket that matched Hassan’s. Her hair was up in two pigtails. She held both adults’ hands and swung between them.

I dawdled at the other end of the counter near the window. I looked back at Scharf through the window, who made a shooing gesture. I shook my head and turned around.

David approached me from the other side of the counter. “Can I help you?”

I winced. “Um,” I cast about for a reason to be there and almost rolled my eyes. *You’re in a teashop, Orange.* “Can I have some tea?”

“Hey, don’t I know you?”

I grimaced. I’d forgotten I met David the night of the murder. When he glanced down to pick up a mug, I quickly dropped my glamour, turning my hair strawberry and curly and my skin orange.

He jerked when he looked back at me, squinted, and said, “Never mind. Sorry.”

“I’ll take a tea,” I said, lowering my voice.

David jerked a thumb behind him at the chalkboard menu with at least thirty different options in carefully printed capital letters. “Take your pick.”

I cleared my throat. “You pick.”

He grinned. “You like green, black, white, fermented, herbal?”

I swallowed. “What’s your best lightly flavored tea? Think very weak.”

He smirked. “Green tea with honey in a big mug?”

“Great.” I leaned on the counter as he pivoted to grab one tin behind him and started pouring pale leaves into a stainless-steel pot. I licked my lips. Maybe he knew what his aunt was, and I wouldn’t have to confront her.

“So…” I began. “Crazy weather we’re having?” I cringed at the most obvious line in history.

He grinned at me, and I realized he took it as a pickup line. I felt weird flirting for information and straightened back up.

I traced a circle on the spotless wood counter and tried again. “It’s almost like someone is artificially changing the weather to be horrible all the time!”

“You sound like my aunt,” he said as he got a coffee mug from beside the aluminum urn. “It’s not real!” he said in a perfect imitation of her voice.

“Wow, what does she say is doing it?” I asked a little loudly. When he stiffened at my tone, I forced a heartiness into my voice. “Or… who? Just out of curiosity? A hugely powerful, destructive magical creature? Theoretically?” He didn’t say anything else, just added hot water to the pot. I exhaled. “You know my dad believed he was a goblin? Like an actual goblin.”

He blinked twice.

“With magic. Hahaha. Ha…” I trailed off.

He put a strainer over the mug and poured the leaves and the water through it. “Weird.”

“And the dude next door, well, technically his grandmother, believed in miracles because her toast got burned.”

“Joe? He told me that whole thing was a prank.”

A prank apparently the entire neighborhood got in on. “I’m just saying, magic weather is not the craziest story I’ve heard today. Haha.” I forced myself to shut up when my hysterical giggle threatened to turn into a full goblin cackle.

“It’s nothing,” David said, setting the mug in front of me. I dug for cash and took a sip. The flavor was rich, subtle, and unlike any tea I had ever drunk before.

“This is so good.” I took another sip, marveling at the floral explosion in my nostrils. “Like, so good.”

He smiled and put the bill in an old-fashioned register without a computer attached. “You’re welcome.”

I took another hasty sip, wanting to take an hour and savor, but I leaned closer to him, forcing a grin. “Top that story.”

He leaned closer to me, smiling again. “Okay fine. My aunt believes there’s a dragon in the building and he’s causing the storm.”

# 18

“There’s a dragon in the building?!”

At David’s sharp look, I slammed my lips shut.

“And that’s why she’s such a talented piano player,” he continued, cleaning up his side of the counter. “Because of the dragon. Not because she spent most of her life practicing. And why I should play my damn violin more. Because apparently gaming is not a viable career path.” The upside-down mugs and cups on the counter were jiggling with each swipe of his rag. “Even though I make more in an hour than she does in a day.”

“I think it’s a brilliant career path!” I resisted the urge to reach over and grab his wrist. “Maybe think really happy thoughts about it right now. All unicorns all the time.” If we’d truly tracked the source of this, he’d been at ground zero for *weeks*.

“You’re right.” He took a breath and dropped the rag.

I put the mug down. “Um. Just out of completely idle curiosity, where is this supposed dragon?”

“I don’t know.”

I sighed.

“He closes his shop early these days. I don’t know if he’s there or not.”

I straightened. “*His* shop?”

David hooked a thumb over his shoulder. “Yeah, totally. Supposedly, the old dude next door is the god of music. And thunder.” He picked up a rag and cleaned a precise circle directly in front of him on the spotless counter.

“Not another fire-breather.” I gripped the counter to keep upright.

“What?”

“Nothing!”

“He wouldn’t breathe… Just no. Chinese dragons are good luck. They don’t breathe fire.”

“Of course,” I said, mostly to myself, “the djinni didn’t breathe fire. He *was* fire.”

“What?”

“Since no one has run screaming from the building, I take it he doesn’t look like a dragon?”

“Are you okay?”

I nodded, visions of silent radios and malfunctioning music apps dancing in my head and the endless, silent ice. “You’re right, that’s such a super weird thing for your aunt to believe! Um, thanks. Good luck with board games!”

“They’re not… Have a nice day.” He turned to serve a guy who had poured himself a coffee.

“Amina!” Hassan cried out, panic drenching his voice.

I ran around the counter to see Mrs. Bao and Hassan, but no little girl.

“Amina!” Mrs. Bao echoed. “Where could she have gone?”

“My car’s out front. Maybe…” Hassan ran towards us.

Mrs. Bao spoke to a few customers in rapid Chinese, but they shook their heads. “I’ll check the back!” Mrs. Bao said and ran in the opposite direction toward the back door and the alley.

David launched himself over the counter and ran towards his aunt. He said, “I’ll check the apartment!” and ran up the stairs.

Hassan ran past me and burst through the front door without seeing me. He waved wildly at Scharf.

Feeling sick, I looked back at the archway into the dragon’s lair. The archway was as wide as two doors and dim, but I could see racks of instruments. If I was a young girl who enjoyed music, it’s exactly where I would go. I took a breath and stepped through.

At first, all I could see were instruments I didn’t quite recognize. There were string instruments and reeds that looked vaguely like the guitars and flutes I knew.

“Amina?” I said, far too quietly.

I took a step forward and froze. In the back far corner of the shop, an old man sat on a stool staring at the floor.

He sat behind a short beat-up wooden counter with an old-fashioned metal cash register perched on it. The monstrosity had hidden him from my view. He leaned against a precarious pile of boxes with customs forms and Chinese script stamped up the side of them.

I tried but failed to see a dragon in the beaten man with his straight black hair and wrinkled face, wearing khakis and a cardigan. For a second, I questioned my sanity. And yet… He had a presence like Juya had sometimes, the sense of something *more* beneath the exterior.

Then I saw a little hand reach up from behind the counter and pat his hand. I raced to the end to look around and see Amina staring up at him, absolutely fearless. I could also see from this angle that he only had one leg. It ended shortly below his hip and his pants were sewn around it. He had a pair of crutches propped by him.

“Don’t worry, I’ll help,” Amina said to the dragon.

At that moment, I saw double. An enormous red serpent, like a hologram hovering above him, nodded. I froze in terror. She was inches from a creature of unfathomable power. And she was patting his hand.

I blinked, and he was only an old man again; the disturbing dual perception of serpent and human was gone. She sang an unfamiliar melody in a warbling voice, “*Lalo, lalo.”*

The dragon shuddered, a full-body convulsion much more like something cold-blooded than human.

I gathered all the magic I could summon as Amina finished her song, wanting to pull her out of there, but not wanting to anger him.

“It means sleep,” she said brightly. “It’s from my old home, across the ocean. My Baba sings it when I am scared.”

She laughed, and as she trailed off, the dragon smiled.

And then the rain stopped.

I rubbed two fingers by my ear to be sure I could still hear, shocked by the sudden silence. The constant humming drum on the windows and roofs of every building I’d sheltered in was gone. The dragon joined in her laughter and thunder deafened us.

“Thank you very much for your beautiful music,” he said slowly, with the slight drawl of a DC accent.

My jaw dropped in shock. I hadn’t consciously realized I was spoiling for a fight, even as I knew how imbalanced our power was and how unlikely I would be to win.

And Amina had just stopped the rain with a lullaby.

I crouched down to be at her level. It didn’t take much; she was only a foot shorter than me. “How did you know he needed a song?”

She cocked her head. “Mr. Liu shows me his instruments. He plays really good, but he said he was too tired today, so I said I would sing him to sleep. Like my Baba does.”

I glanced at the dragon for half a second; he hadn’t moved.

“That was really smart!” I said, deeply meaning every word. He didn’t need fighting; he needed cheering up.

When another peal of thunder shook the building and the rain began again, the crushing pressure in my head that everything was hopeless returned. I hadn’t realized how oppressive it was until it lifted for a few seconds.

I wanted to chain Amina to the counter singing songs for a week. I wanted to snatch her away and tell Hassan to drive to Antarctica to get away from him.

I noticed the serpent again because he swung his head towards me, even though the man hadn’t twitched.

Running won.

“Amina!” I said much more loudly. “That was a beautiful song. Thank you! Your Baba is looking for you, though.”

She cocked her head at me. “You know him? Who are you?”

“A friend of his,” I said. It took everything I had to pull my eyes away from the centuries in his gaze and smile at the little girl. “My name is Orange.”

She giggled, and the world got a little warmer for a second. “He told me about you.”

I couldn’t imagine what he would say. “Oh, that’s good! You want to go back to the teashop and find him?”

“Amina!” Hassan shouted from the arch. We didn’t have to find him. He’d come back in.

To my infinite relief, she skipped toward him, and I backed away from the dragon after her.

“I found her!” he shouted to the room behind him.

He murmured to her in Pashto, their native language, as he gathered her in his arms.

Mrs. Bao joined them seconds later and put a hand on his shoulder.

“No more small talk,” I said, pulling the corners of my mouth up as I shooed them all away from the music shop. It was a travesty of a smile, but I could not summon even fake amusement. “Go, go, go.”

Hassan turned towards me, a question in his eyes.

“I found my *problem*,” I said through gritted teeth.

“Roger that,” he said, with the ghost of a smile, though he looked drawn. This close to the epicenter had to be hard on all of us.

He bid a brief goodbye to Mrs. Bao, but this time did not linger.

When the door chimes sounded his exit, I spun. I found myself back in the music shop before I could think too hard about what I was doing.

“Why are you doing this?” I’d meant to sing a song or crack a joke but screw that.

His dark eyes left the counter and met mine. I had to step back from the millennia in his gaze. If I hadn’t already known, that look alone would have convinced me he wasn’t human. He was as far away from human as I’d ever encountered.

“The ice?” I asked, “The silence? The crime spree? What did the world ever do to you? And don’t pretend you don’t know what I’m talking about.” Even as the words poured out of my mouth, I told myself to sing a cheerful song or just shut the hell up, but I couldn’t.

“Goblin, what tales are you telling?”

“Why? Tell me why.”

After a long moment, he said, “You ask why we live? Why the sun rises?”

“Stop with the philosophy. This isn’t academic.” I pointed to the front window. “Real people are dying out there. What are you trying to do? Who pissed you off? Who…” I looked at this old creature, who flinched with each word I spoke. He looked nothing like a powerful, wrathful dragon. I was prepared for vengeance, but I had no idea how to deal with—indifference? “Who broke you?”

He straightened, which was the first real sign of life I’d seen from him. “I sell instruments. Teach children. Bring the rains. Soften the hail. That is my world.”

That may have once been his world, but it wasn’t anymore. And my questions were making him worse. Maybe he didn’t even know himself. *Happy thoughts,* *Orange.*

A joke seemed a feeble option, but it was all I had. “Why did the musician join Mensa?” When the dragon said nothing, I continued, “He was sharp!”

I smiled hopefully, but the old man’s expression remained lifeless.

“Never mind.” I looked around, fighting a growing certainty that all was lost, that all was hopeless.

*Unicorns and rainbows, Orange.* “If I wanted a musical instrument, which one should I choose?”

“The cymbals, goblin.”

“Got any around? We could do a duet. You’re a god of music, right?”

He coughed and shifted on the stool. I tried to look into his eyes but found I couldn’t meet them again. I gazed vaguely at his wrinkled, liver-spotted forehead.

“*Something* is different. We need to put it to rights,” I said gently since my magic didn’t seem to make the tiniest dent.

“What if it is right? Before now, everything was wrong.” He reached up and plucked one of his odd-shaped guitars from the wall that probably wasn’t called a guitar. It was old and made of a pale wood I didn’t recognize with deep grooves in the grain. He played a note and then a series of them.

They jarred my ears, and I was about to tell him he’d proved my point when I remembered that Chinese music doesn’t use the Western harmonic scale. Our music is in a major key that sounds cheery and simple. This went far beyond that.

A haunting, achingly familiar yet otherworldly melody filled the shop.

I hadn’t thought it could get better until he started to sing in a cracked, gravelly voice in Chinese. Immobilized, I found myself lost in that voice.

Sometimes, while watching the Olympics or listening to an inspiring speech or a concert, I got the feeling I was watching perfection. Or at least as close to perfection as you can find in this world. The control and the nuance and depth of every movement seemed inhuman.

But that was nothing compared to a god of music. Every twitch of his fingers was poetry, every sound a universe in itself. Tears streamed down my cheeks.

If I lived all the centuries my father had, I’d hear nothing so perfect again.

A string snapped on the instrument, stealing the music from me. We both stared at his hands, and he looked up blindly. “Silence is a music.”

Still smarting from the raw emotion the song had evoked, I stepped away.

“This was no new year,” the dragon said.

“No new year?” What was he talking about?

But then it struck me. I remembered the day this started. The day the rain fell and people began to hurt each other. I’d been in Chinatown at a riot. *This was no New Year.*

I’d heard the jeers about DC’s Chinatown. The Starbucks and the McDonald’s with Chinese script on the door. The same restaurant ten times in a row. Two thousand dollars for a broom closet-sized studio. The genuine Chinese community of Chinatown was dying out or moving to Maryland, exactly like the Baos. It was a business district now, only interested in making money.

And then they staged a corporate-sponsored parade. Barely anyone had come before a bunch of hockey fans had trashed the neighborhood. Instead of picking himself up one more time, he’d stopped.

I told him the first thing that came to mind. “The music isn’t dead.” It felt like a lie. “Thank you for your beautiful song.”

He didn’t respond.

“I have other music.”

I dug out my phone and scrolled through songs and picked the most upbeat song I could find. The one that had played even during the ice storm. I hit “play,” and the thumping beat filled the shop. His eyes flew open, and he stared at my phone and groaned.

In his anguish, his form grew fuzzy, and I jumped backward, rattling the end of a shelf of metal flutes and other pipes. One flute hit the ground with a discordant clang.

My phone screen cracked, and the phone broke in my hand. I dropped the jagged pieces. I still could see the old man at the counter, with his one leg and his crutches. But if I blinked or tilted my head, a coiled serpent with red scales and tufted ears, a gaping mouth, and gigantic eyes grew before me.

I ran out of the shop.

# 19

As more instruments crashed, I shouted, “Okay, maybe I can’t cheer him up!”

“Can’t cheer who up?” Scharf asked from the archway where she stood with Mrs. Bao. “What happened in there?”

I glanced back. Finally, the din quieted, and a pile of instruments settled in the archway, blocking it.

“We must help!” Mrs. Bao said, surging towards the archway.

“We have to get out of here!” I grabbed Scharf’s elbow and Mrs. Bao’s hand and tried to pull them out of sight. When Scharf didn’t move with me, I used magic to force her along. Fortunately, the Mahjong games had ended, and the shop had emptied.

“What’s going on?” Scharf asked, at last jerking free of my hand and stopping near the front door.

“It’s him,” I said in a stage whisper, waiting for a dragon to come barreling through the archway. When he didn’t and no more instruments crashed, I met her eyes. “All of this. The weather. The anger. He’s a dragon, and he’s kind of, I don’t know, upset?”

Scharf dug out her phone. “I can have SWAT in here in ten minutes.”

Mrs. Bao turned to her, aghast. “You can’t SWAT a fundamental force!”

Scharf paused. “You knew about the dragon.”

Mrs. Bao clutched her hands together until her knuckles turned white, but her gaze was level and clear as she said, “Yes.”

“You knew he was doing this!” Scharf said.

“No!” Mrs. Bao insisted. “I mean, I wondered... I worried. What our leaving would do. The new renters want to put in a parlor for temporary tattoos. Perhaps that upset him?”

I blinked. “I’m sorry, did you say *temporary* tattoos?”

She nodded miserably. “We wanted to stay, but we could not. The building rent tripled and fewer and fewer people want proper tea.”

“It’s fantastic,” I whispered. Having finally tasted even their simplest real tea, I didn’t know if I could ever drink anything else. “And I don’t think it was that. This started on the Lunar Near Year, remember? That’s what he said! That this was no new year. Maybe he didn’t like the parade?”

“He didn’t like the parade?” Scharf ran a hand over her hair.

Mrs. Bao stiffened, and I remembered she’d been on the committee.

“I’m sure it was a lovely parade!” I said, waving my hands, horrified. “Maybe he just wants to move, too. You’re right.”

“I don’t know why he stayed so long.” Mrs. Bao gripped her fingers again. She seemed to be holding herself together with two hands. “He followed a child here who wanted to see the New World.”

“The New World? By so long, you mean centuries?” I asked.

Mrs. Bao bobbed her head from side to side. I didn’t know how to interpret the gesture.

“And he hasn’t flambeed anyone in all that time?” I said slowly, unable to stop myself from asking.

That dried her tears; she even smiled a tiny smile. “I forget all the terrible things your dragons do in myths. To us, dragons are luck and fortune. Kui brought music to the world. Dragons in China are not fire-breathing. We are blessed to live in his sphere of influence. Do you think I would be the best piano teacher in the city if I didn’t live where I live? It is a matter of Kui’s benevolence.”

I chewed on my lip as I thought that through. On the one hand, a harbinger of luck and fortune sounded great, but what happened when you made him angry? I swallowed.

“Did he know Rodger?” Scharf asked casually.

Mrs. Bao gave a little half-shrug. “Rodger came in here, but I did not see him go next door. Kui doesn’t get close to anyone. That’s not… I don’t know. He did not hurt Rodger. He could hurt no one. That’s not—possible.”

I scratched my chin. “How do we cheer up a dragon?”

“We could…” Mrs. Bao said softly, staring at the back of her shop and the pile of instruments. “No, it’s too crazy.”

“I’m standing here discussing how to cheer up a dragon,” Scharf said flatly. “I’ll take any and all suggestions.”

“We could put on another parade. There are many community groups the committee did not approve of. We could… I could...”

I felt the vaguest stirring of hope; it was an alien sensation. “That would make *me* less sad.”

“It is not his sadness that matters,” Mrs. Bao said, visibly frustrated. “He bestows his bounty on the world. And he withdraws it as he chooses. He has withdrawn it.”

Everything in me sunk. I stared down at my unmarked knuckles, remembering the rage and the blood. “So, this isn’t him making the world angry; this is him *not* making the world *less* angry? This is *normal*?”

She nodded vigorously. “When the first cold day at the end of summer arrives, it feels so cold. But the same day at the end of winter feels very warm. This will feel warm again, one day.”

Scharf shook her head. She looked pale. “The weather too? This is normal?”

Mrs. Bao shrugged. “The atmosphere will adapt.”
