I sat down on one stool at the counter. “So normal feels cold because we’ve been so used to his, uh, warm influence? And you think offering him a parade would get it back?” I stared through the front windows at the falling rain, feeling the urge to apologize to the sky.

Mrs. Bao didn’t look confident, but she nodded. “If the New Year is what offended him, we could try.”

“I can’t believe I’m solving a crime with a parade,” Scharf said.

There was a wild light in Mrs. Bao’s eyes. “Except we couldn’t. It takes a year to plan. To stop traffic. And the storm!”

I grinned. “I’ll worry about the weather. Scharf can do the traffic.”

Scharf raised an eyebrow. “Scharf can?”

I ignored her and kept my eyes on Mrs. Bao. “Thank you! You have no idea how much. Can we do it tomorrow?”

“Tomorrow?” Both of them asked together.

I remembered the look in his eyes, the three-thousand-year stare. “Yes, tomorrow.”

I could not shake the horror—that this was the normal world. He wasn’t affecting us, but exactly the opposite; he’d finally *stopped* affecting us. That the rage that had me pounding on an unconscious opponent was my natural rage.

“Tomorrow morning, first thing,” I said.

“I do not know who will come,” Mrs. Bao insisted.

“You can make it happen. We have to make it happen.” I looked back into his shop. I could just see the pile of instruments and no sign of Kui. “Maybe don’t go in there until tomorrow?”

Mrs. Bao nodded.

“He’ll be fine,” I said.

She smiled her tiny smile. “He will always be fine. It is the rest of us I am worried about.”

“See you tomorrow,” I said before Scharf could say anything else. I pulled on her with magic to get out of the shop.

The rain was worse than it had been in weeks. I ducked under the awning over the music shop, staring in the window, but I couldn’t see anyone past the rattled displays.

“How am I going to stop traffic?” she asked. “And how are you going to fix the weather?”

I grinned. “Help. I get magic help, and you get one of the dozen cops you know. Somebody has to be able to block off a street. We cheer him up. He turns off the rain and we live happily ever after.”

She sighed. “I’ll call Lee.” As she dug out her phone and dialed, she said, “We still haven’t solved the murder. Either murder.”

“Yes, we have,” I said, and then realized I hadn’t told Scharf the Lubber Fiend killed Everglades. I cringed, turtling my head into my jacket.

She ended the call just as someone answered and said, very quietly, “Orange?”

“I forgot!”

She crossed her arms and said, “You forgot to tell me who the murderer is?”

“Happy thoughts!” I said, pointing through the window. “Remember where we’re standing!”

“Orange.”

“Unicorns and rainbows!”

“Orange!”

“The Lubber Fiend killed the goblin.”

“The Rubber Fiend, who is coming after you? How do you know?”

“It’s actually Lubber… Never mind. He told me.”

“Orange! Dear God, what are we going to do?” She paced, got doused by a sluice of water from the awning, and jumped back next to me. “What are you going to do?”

“Oh no, this isn’t the judge, jury, executioner thing, is it? I can’t execute him!” I meant that in the moral and literal sense of the word. I still had no idea how I was going to win our inevitable fight, let alone punish him for his crimes afterward.

Scharf looked up into the rain. “Just the goblin? What about Rodger?”

I shook my head, but then stopped. “It didn’t come up.”

“It. Didn’t. Come. Up?” she asked with two red lines striping her cheeks where she was clenching her teeth.

“Happy thoughts!”

“Hey, Scharf!” a voice shouted.

We both pivoted to see Carlisle hugging the building to stay out of the rain as he approached us from down the block.

“What are you doing here?” we both asked.

He looked between the two of us. “I could ask you the same question.”

“Are you cleared to be here?” Scharf asked, and I remembered that the last time we’d seen him, he was getting hauled away on a stretcher after the Lubber Fiend had almost squished him.

“Agents are tough.”

“Carlisle,” Scharf said.

“Minor concussion.” He rolled his eyes. “It’s fine. We had a tip. Actually, Melissa had a tip.”

Scharf raised an eyebrow.

“She listened to a podcast. There’s a waitress who had a beef with Rodger. They broke up. The podcaster interviewed her. Now *we* get to interview her.”

I shook my head. “I knew I should have bargained with the waitress.”

Scharf laid a heavy hand on my shoulder, and I shut up.

“Um, okay,” Carlisle said. “Fuller and Melissa are searching her home right now.”

“*Melissa* is searching someone’s home?” Scharf asked.

Carlisle crossed his arms defensively. “She’s observing. We’d already searched the restaurant, but I’m going to try to speak with her now.”

I smirked. “Trying to cut out the foremost profiler in the world?”

He ducked into the pasta shop without a by-your-leave.

“Is he supposed to do that alone?”

Scharf shook her head. “The case agent in charge has a great deal of discretion.”

“Who’s the case agent in charge?”

Scharf sighed. “That’s a good question and a problem we’ve had from the start. A journalist is searching a suspect’s house.”

“What are *we* going to do?”

Scharf looked at her phone. “Cheer up the dragon and leave them to it, I suppose.” She sounded like she was going in for a root canal while they went on the best vacation in the world.

I sighed. “Okay, deal, we throw a parade and I help you bag Rodger’s murderer.”

She raised an eyebrow. “I already have a magical bargain to make you help with the murder.”

“I mean specifically help with getting the waitress before Carlisle. It can’t hurt.”

Her raised eyebrow told me what she thought of that statement.

“How much worse could this get?” I insisted. “Don’t answer that.”

“Okay fine.”

A chime sounded, and I rubbed my hands together, reveling in the rush of magic. I tried to rely on my bargain with myself for magic, but sometimes it was just easier to do it the old-fashioned way, though I had far less control when I bargained with somebody else.

When nothing happened, I rubbed my hands together again.

Scharf shook her head. “I don’t know how you stand this—just waiting for absolute chaos to break your way.”

I was about to protest when the door to the pasta shop opened and a familiar, skinny kid ducked into the rain. I'd last seen him shouting about podcasts.

“Hey!” I shouted. “Hey, I’m ready for that interview.”

He turned towards me and crossed his arms. “I heard you in there. You’re a rival!”

I poked at Scharf. She didn't react. I poked her again.

“What?”

“Show him your badge thingy.”

She obligingly flashed her shield as she whispered. “Who is that?”

“He’s a podcaster,” I said. “Didn’t you hear Carlisle? He got the waitress from a podcast!”

“Can law enforcement pose as a podcaster?” the kid asked, fascinated. “I could build a whole episode around this. Now they're trying to be us!”

“I’m not law enforcement,” I said. “But I will make a deal with you. Give us everything you have on the waitress, and I'll give you the scoop of a lifetime.”

He shook his head. “I already have the scoop of a lifetime.”

“Okay,” Scharf said. “I won't arrest you.”

“For what?”

“I'm not sure yet.”

“That's illegal!”

“Probably for helping a murderer. Aiding and abetting.” Scharf continued in a scary, calm voice.

“Deal?” I insisted.

He thought about it and a half-smile spread across his face. I squinted; he looked far too happy. After my experience with John Hastings, I was far less confident in my bargains for the truth.

“Deal,” he said, and the chime sounded in my head. “So long as I can record this.”

He dug out a palm-sized, black machine too thick to be a phone. I didn’t recognize it except for the tiny microphone on the end of it.

Scharf crossed her arms. “I don’t consent to be recorded.”

“DC is a one-party consent state.” He smirked. “Only I have to consent to be recorded.”

I frowned. “DC isn’t a state.”

From her silent death glare, it seemed Scharf already knew about the consent problem.

I poked her again. “We need him. Let him record.” I turned to him. “We get you too, if you record.”

Scharf sighed and let her hands drop. She whispered as quietly as possible, “Tell us what you know.”

“All right!”

A chime sounded, and I resisted rubbing my hands together and tried not to worry about bargains within bargains and how they might interact.

He shrugged. “Since all of this is already on my podcast, I can spare five minutes to tell you she insisted she was at home that night watching a *Bachelor* marathon.”

Scharf cocked her head; this wasn’t news to her. “Why don’t you believe that?”

“It’s not an alibi!” he shouted.

“Happy thoughts!” I shouted back.

Scharf put a single finger on my forearm.

“It isn’t,” Scharf agreed and continued to whisper. “But there’s a jump from, ‘I can’t confirm she was on her couch watching TV’ to ‘I *can* confirm she was at a restaurant strangling a teenager with her bare hands.’”

He perked up. “She used her bare hands?”

Scharf cursed so quietly that I could barely hear her. “No.”

“No?”

“No,” Scharf repeated.

He shrugged. “I can quote you. You said it.”

Scharf ignored that and repeated, “How did you make the leap?”

I was getting increasingly nervous, standing here so close to the dragon in a confrontational interview with a kid with a microphone and Scharf’s face growing paler and paler.

When nobody said anything, Scharf started fishing for her handcuffs and he backed up, “Okay, okay… On the night of the murder, somebody saw a woman run out of the alleyway.”

“Who?”

“Oh, you couldn’t find them? How fascinating.”

“Who?” Scharf said even more calmly.

The kid gulped. I gave him props; it took some law enforcement professionals much longer to recognize her voice of death. “Somebody who was at the game was running this way to escape the riot. They called my podcast last week about a woman in the alley.”

Scharf rolled her eyes. “I need their name.”

“I don’t have to reveal my sources.”

I scoffed. “You’re not a journalist.”

“Well, I… I still don’t have to reveal my sources. And I have proof!” He clutched at his battered messenger bag, a tell even I could recognize. Scharf’s eyes zeroed in on his white knuckles. This time, she took the handcuffs off her belt, opened one of them, and stepped forward.

He spun to get away and stepped out from under the awning and onto the ice. He did an impressive dive and skidded. As he did, his bag burst open, revealing a bigger microphone, more esoteric recording equipment, and a little baggie that leaped straight into my hands on a puff of magic I could barely sense.

I held it up and saw a little bracelet made of silver in a bag for snacks, not an official evidence packet. I flipped it over. Printed on the bracelet was a red cross, the universal symbol for medical alerts, and tiny letters: Rodger Clark, Epilepsy.

Sometimes I *loved* my magic.

I showed it to Scharf. “I did bargain for him to give me everything he has on the waitress.” I snapped my fingers, remembering my half of the bargain. “Oh yeah, here’s your big scoop.”

“Yeah?”

He looked interested, even as he gripped his elbow and tried to get up. I leaned down. “This ice storm is being caused by a dragon.” I pointed to the music shop. “He works there.”

“What the hell?” he demanded.

Scharf plucked the baggie out of my hand and kneeled beside him, making no move to help him up. “Where did you get this?”

“I don’t have to—”

“Where?” she asked, deadly quiet.

“It was in her locker! Like right there. I don’t know how you guys didn’t find it.”

“When?”

“An hour ago?”

“You are under arrest for tampering with evidence.”

As Scharf flipped the podcaster over, she gestured with a chin. “Can you magic that?” I looked at the recorder and snapped something in it. “And go tell Carlisle to arrest the waitress.”

Carlisle burst out of the restaurant. “She’s gone!”

“Naturally.” Scharf sagged.

Carlisle looked down. “Who’s he? Never mind. The waitress walked out on her shift fifteen minutes ago.”

Scharf dug for her phone on her belt. “I’ll put out a BOLO.”

“*I’ll* put out a BOLO,” Carlisle said and scrambled to get his phone.

“What’s a BOLO?” I asked.

“Be On the Look Out,” Scharf said. “Don’t get distracted. He can handle this. Go fix the weather. We have to stop the dragon before anybody else dies.”

Carlisle turned back to us, his phone at his ear. “Did you say dragon?”

# 20

I hadn’t exactly told Scharf the truth. I couldn’t turn off the weather. But Juya could. I left Carlisle and Scharf duking out who was going to inform what agency about the missing waitress and headed for Juya’s. I just didn’t know if Juya was going to be in any shape to do it.

I passed Astrid in the courtyard of Juya’s home, sitting at the picnic tables under the umbrella. She was selling a meager pile of lumpy *cocadas*, Juya’s coconut cookies. She looked more put together in jeans and a peacoat than the last time I’d seen her, though her coiled hair was hidden in an elaborate green turban with a clear plastic cap protecting it. She hailed me, but I waved and continued for the door.

“Things to do!” I said, racing away.

Juya’s helpers didn’t even bother stopping me when I burst into the house. Whether they were still afraid after the fight—both fights—or had given up, I wasn’t sure. I took the stairs two at a time and pounded on the door to her bedroom for half a second before rushing in and announcing, “I need warm weather by tomorrow!”

I looked down at her on the bed and winced. She’d aged and lost weight in a few days. Osman, sitting beside her in a chair, looked as though he had aged as well even though I was almost certain he was human.

I rushed over. “Juya, you can’t let them get to you!”

“*Hay muy frio*.”

I took her icy hand in mine as I knelt beside her bed. “I know, but it’ll stay cold if you don’t help me.” Her eyes opened, and I added, “I figured out who it is.”

“*Quien*?”

“A dragon.”

Her mouth dropped open, and she sank further into her bed with a dry laugh. “Who?”

“Kui Liu. He looks almost as old as you.”

Her eyes flared. “You met this dragon?”

“Yep.”

She coughed and curled into her pillows. It hurt deep in my solar plexus to see her like this. I couldn’t help feeling guilty, but guilt wouldn’t help her.

Osman handed her a lozenge that smelled like moss.

“The dragon’s not doing it on purpose,” I said. “At least we don’t think he is. It started during the Lunar New Year, so we’re gonna redo the parade and try to snap him out of it.”

When I leaned closer again, Osman stood up and grabbed my shoulder. “Leave her alone.” His voice was urgent but quiet.

I shook him off. “There can’t be a parade if the whole street is covered in ice. If I did it with my water powers, it would take me a month, and we don’t have a week. We need unicorns and rainbows now!”

“Unicorns and rainbows?” Osman asked.

“Water powers?” Juya asked.

“I got my father’s box open. A little.” I took my finger and stirred the water in the glass on her side table without touching it. Like before, I had to get my finger within a foot of the water for it to work. Juya laughed, and it subsided into a dry cough as I said, “Oh, yeah, that’s hilarious. Can you help at all?”

“I am not sure,” she said, but her eyes were keen.

Gazing at the ceiling, I said, “I think it’s a vicious cycle. Everyone around him is freaking out and trying to kill each other, and that makes him more depressed, and then everybody freaks out more. In another month, we’re going to end up with the city completely frozen and overrun by homicidal maniacs who choke goblins.”

“Who do what?” Osman asked.

I took a breath, trying to stay calm. “You really can’t summon the power for dry pavement?”

She shook her head. “There were times when I could summon a *huracán*”

She looked at Osman, who nodded.

“There were times when I was feared and loved across the land.” She focused on the window with a faraway look in her eyes. “Such powers even you cannot imagine, even a powerful dragon cannot imagine.”

Kui had frozen the capital and started a crime spree. And she said she could dwarf that?

Juya laughed. “But now, who would worship me?”

She had laughed, but I didn’t. “Me, for one.”

She sobered and looked at Osman, who said, “*Yo también*. Of course, me too.”

Juya scoffed. “Stop being nice.”

I stepped closer. “I’m not nice. If this dragon has proved anything, it’s that I’m not at all nice. But Juya, I love you. You saved my mother. You saved me. When the world stole from me and tried to stop me, you fed me and protected me. I love you! How’s that for power? And Astrid does too. And so many more.”

“It’s true.” Astrid stood in the doorway, pulling the plastic hood off her head but leaving the bright-green cloth. How much had she heard? “She fed me too, Orange. I fell behind last year. My rent, school fees, and she gave me a day’s worth of food for a quarter. Every day for six months.” She looked at Juya. “I don’t even know how you found out.”

I savored the small smile her story brought to Juya’s face and said, “Magic.”

“We’re out of food today,” Astrid said. “So we’re closing. Which is a miracle since I baked.”

I leaned in close and asked Juya, “Is that good enough for you?” Then Astrid’s last line registered. I straightened. “You baked?”

Juya tried but failed to smile. “It is so cold. I am so old.”

I glanced at Astrid and crouched to whisper in Juya’s ear. “Bull. Don’t tell me you’re old and you’ve lived your life. That’s the dragon talking. I’m staring down the business end of a dozen centuries, and you’re lying here saying you’re giving up?” I stood. “If I have to, I’ll have the entire neighborhood knock on your door and tell you how much they love you.”

“*Abuela*,” Osman said, “you’ve been doing favors for everyone for years. Do you honestly think no one’s noticed?”

She sighed. “It is my twilight years.”

Astrid frowned. “What’s she talking about?”

I dragged the quilt off her. “Nothing. She’s being crotchety and contrary. Pretending her life is over because it’s been long. Get up.”

Osman jumped. “Orange!”

“No! She doesn’t need us to sit vigil over her. She needs life!” I spun to confront Osman. “Can you get her to Chinatown tomorrow morning?”

He looked at Juya with such love in his eyes that it dwarfed my feelings. He nodded. “Yes, I can do that.”

I took a deep breath and closed my eyes. “*Gracias*. Thank you. I’ll be waiting,” I said, gripping Juya’s hand as hard as I dared until Osman put a hand on my shoulder and gently pulled me away.

Once we hit the stairs, Astrid asked, “Why Chinatown?”

“Uh… Well…”

“It’s her favorite place,” Osman said quickly.

“Really?”

“Sure!” I looked at him as I added, “Right under the arch by the Metro.”

He nodded.

I looked between the two of them. “Can you spread the word? Everyone who can come.”

“What do we ask them to do?” Astrid asked.

I looked at Osman.

“Worship her?” he said with a wince.

“Metaphorically. Well, kind of,” I added. “Just what I said. We need to tell her how much she means to us.”

Astrid seemed to take that in stride. “But Orange…”

“What do you need?”

She let out a sigh. “Orange, what are we going to do about our cakes? We won’t have a business if they won’t stay fresh.”

“You’ll be surprised how much better that will go after tomorrow…” I hoped.

“That’s not our only problem. I’ve barely seen you lately.”

I smiled. “Astrid, I’m all yours. I’m all in. I quit my other jobs.” Remembering Juya, I felt my shoulders tighten. “Except I have to do this tomorrow. For Juya.”

“Of course.”

“And then I’m yours.”

She shot me a doubtful look. “Orange, you’ve said that before.”

“And then I flaked. I know. But I’m not going to do it again, okay? I’ve changed.”

“You’ve changed?” she laughed. “Nobody changes. They succumb to social pressure.”

“Which psych class did that come from?”

Astrid bit her tongue. “Orange!”

“Okay, lately I’ve endured an unreal amount of social pressure not to be myself.” I stopped. “That came out wrong. To be me, only a grown-up. I’m already a grown-up. To be my best self? Stop me before I become an anxiety medication commercial.”

She flashed me a grin. “I believe you.”

“I’m going to be there for you.”

She sighed and sat on the front step. “It’s not just you. It’ll be nearly impossible to pull off this business. They don’t tell you about the exhaustion in any class. It’s unreal.”

I fought a smile. I punched goblins. Scharf shouted at her boss. The Lubber Fiend killed people. Juya almost died. But the dragon only made Astrid a little tired. Though seeing her defeated eyes, I knew it went deeper than that.

“You should sleep, and I should…” *Fight a dragon, catch a killer, and bake some cakes*… “Also sleep. We have a deal, right? You and me together, launching the grand food empire, or whatever you called it.”

Astrid sniffled. The tears in her eyes shocked me. “Sorry,” she said. “I don’t cry.”

I sighed and sat down beside her. “There’s a lot of power in tears.” As a hobgoblin, I would know. I had a dozen platitudes ready as I reached for my hob powers, but I stopped.

“I never do this,” she said.

Tentatively, I laid a hand on her back. “Then you were overdue.”

I felt the desperate pressure to make a bargain, but I couldn’t magic our way to success or bargain for her faith in me. Or faith in herself. I thought of a superb joke but bit my tongue.

“Thanks for your help tomorrow,” I said instead.

She wiped her nose with the back of her hand and straightened. “You know, if you give me a week, I can put together a parade for Juya.”

I laughed. “Someone else is taking care of that part.”

She paled. “She’s that bad? This isn’t the moment we take her to her favorite place and say goodbye, is it?”

“No.” I needed to lie to her about this one. If we didn’t take care of the dragon, Juya’s dying was a genuine possibility. “You can gather a crowd?”

Astrid nodded. “Half of her customers have been by today already to check on her. She honestly doesn’t realize she’s the heart of this neighborhood?”

I shook my head. “I don’t think any of us have any idea how we affect the world.”

# 21

The next morning, I sat on a blue metal *Washington Post* dispenser under the Chinatown arch on the corner of Seventh and H streets and put my head in my hands. I felt entirely alone in the sleet. The weather looked miserable in the dim, gray light of dawn through the clouds.

The buildings surrounding the intersection were festooned with screens shining advertisements into the mists of the continuing storm. It wasn’t quite Times Square. Without skyscrapers, nowhere in DC could match that energy, but it was colorful. The crosswalks were painted with dragons. I hoped they weren’t the only dragons on the street today.

Ragged red lanterns were still strung from the arch to nearby buildings overhead, leftovers from the disastrous celebration nobody had taken down. Other than those and one boarded-up window of a shop across the street, the riots had mostly been cleaned up. I suddenly wondered at the madness of gathering another crowd days after that. But we *had* to learn how to gather again without killing each other, and we had to learn it fast.

I said to the rain, “Well, everyone can live with the de-icing bills and the lost revenue from the complete lack of music. Future presidential inaugurations will feature people talking instead of pop stars singing. The Kennedy Center will become a museum for lost art, and the Lubber Fiend will wipe the floor with me.” I sighed. “It’s been a good run for us all.”

Red and blue lights flashed, and a police car pulled up at the end of the block and I sat up straight. An officer got out and started waving cars along. Detective Lee had come through.

I hopped off the newspaper box, and as soon as the last cars passed me, I wandered out into the street to look the other way, where another police car was parking. I winced. They’d only cordoned off a single block.

“Okay, it’s not a parade.” I forced myself to smile. *Happy thoughts.* “It’s a block party.”

I spotted a few commuters hurrying to work on foot, eyeing the police cars, and moving faster. A homeless man slept in the door to Walgreens, and a guy in spandex bike shorts jogged out of the metro to dance in circles through the intersection as the light turned.

“We’re here. Where do you want us?”

As I spun around, I couldn’t fight the tears springing to my eyes. Astrid emerged from the Metro escalators with a collection of half-familiar people from Columbia Heights. Many more followed, each carrying a flower or a piece of art or a baked good.

“Thank you,” I told Astrid.

One of them shrugged. “Anything for Juya.”

“She’ll be here,” I said.

She had to be here.

Hearing a rumble, I turned to see Mrs. Bao rolling the small, battered piano from her shop into the intersection. She wore a red nylon jacket with Mandarin script and the English words “Happy New Year” on it. She grinned at me. “I called everyone I know.”

All I could say was, “Thank you.”

And then I smelled Juya. Cinnamon and coconut. Osman was helping her from a taxi into a wheelchair just beyond the crime-scene tape. A cheer went up from the group as they converged on her to give her their offerings. I’d worried it wasn’t official or the worship she was used to, but watching them hug her, it seemed even more authentic: gifting a grandmother, blood or not, with little tokens of love in a ritual as old as humanity itself.

Teresa exited the taxi next, carrying enormous bags that explained the smell. Of course, Juya had brought breakfast. She handed little cakes out as she rolled through the group and collected her gifts.

I knelt on the ice by her chair and put my hand in hers. “I didn’t bring a gift.”

She cackled and pushed an empanada into my hand.

“No!” I said, “You were supposed to get gifts, not give them.”

She closed my fingers around the tiny pastry. “Get, give; it is the same.”

I nodded and sniffled. “Thank you for all you have done for me. That’s all I can say.”

“You arranged all this.”

I shook my head. “Astrid did, and Osman, and the whole neighborhood. Is it enough?”

Juya closed her eyes. In a few seconds, the rain seemed lighter, though maybe I was imagining it. I stood up and turned to the crowd.

I shouted, “Three cheers for…” *Old ladies who save the day?* But as grateful as I was to Mrs. Bao and Juya, it wasn’t just them. “For neighborhoods! For DC! For us!”

Many people came to this city, like me, with an agenda, a protest, or a symbol, but looking around at the very different crowd than the hockey spectators or the ugly riots, I realized it was also becoming home, as it had always been to most of this crowd.

Juya seemed to grow in her chair after the people cheered. The rain stopped.

The cheering petered out to shocked murmurs.

I craned my neck to look straight up, something I hadn’t done in weeks. The clouds still hung low above the intersection, but the dry air tasted fresh. I looked around and saw that many in the crowd followed me, standing in the street, staring open-mouthed upwards.

They didn’t know she had done this, but they exclaimed as the temperatures climbed above freezing for the first time all month. Commuters stopped and gawked as well. A few workers ran out of their restaurants to stare at the sky.

We looked up at the clouds like we had suddenly entered a grand cathedral. The silence felt holy, broken only by a few bubbles of laughter.

Then the moment passed, and somebody let out a whoop and the whole crowd cheered, and the clouds got lighter still. One man took off his coat.

I realized we would be okay. Without the dragon’s positive influence, we could build our own.

Of course, it would be faster with him.

“If you don’t have anywhere to be, stick around!” I announced. “Thank you for honoring our favorite baker on this, her birthday.” Juya snorted, and I grinned as I said, “A block party will be set up here in a few minutes with entertainment and musicians! This is our city. This is our neighborhood! Let’s celebrate!”

The crowd cheered again. And most of them stuck around, not because I’d told them to, but because of Juya, who sat perched in her wheelchair next to the newspaper box right under the arch, and Mrs. Bao, who was gathering a crowd of her own carrying instruments, mats, and costumes.

Juya called me over and pulled me down so she could whisper in my ear. “You’re very brave, *duende*. And you have an hour before the ice returns. Unless you can bring more worshippers.” And then she laughed her familiar laugh, resonant and rich.

I sucked in a breath. I had less time than I’d thought.

“Orange!” Mrs. Bao waved me over. A bunch of young people in matching maroon jackets were crouched at her feet, pulling band instruments out of sturdy cases.

“Thank you,” I told her. “Thank you so much.”

“I told them they’d have an actual crowd to perform for.” The smile slid off her face. “They’re very young. Most of these were the acts the committee rejected in favor of older or more experienced groups. Or the ones with more money.”

I looked at another motley collection of teenagers emerging from rain gear like gangly butterflies. I wasn’t sure what their contribution was. “This works. It has to work.”

“Where’s Mrs. Scharf?” she asked.

“I’m here,” Scharf called, climbing out of the eggplant sedan she’d parked next to a police car. I started toward her. To my shock, Fuller, Carlisle, and Melissa piled out of the car after her.

“What are they doing here?” I asked her quietly when I reached them.

“I told them I couldn’t stake out the waitress’s house because I had to go to a parade in Chinatown. They insisted on coming.”

“Why?”

“They think I’m going to catch her here or something.”

“I’m going to the Pasta Shop,” Fuller announced.

“It’s closed,” Scharf said.

Melissa raised her hand. “There’s a teashop next door.”

Scharf turned to them. “You know what? That’s a great idea! Definitely stake out the teashop on the off chance she comes back to work four hours before they open. Great use of time and resources.” She spun back to me. “Where’s Kui?”

“I was just going to go get him,” I said.

“Shouldn’t I do that?” Scharf asked.

I shook my head, watching the trio head toward the tea shop. “Let me, please. If things go south, I’m much more likely to survive than you.”

She glanced up and down the street. “Okay. Fine.”

“Seriously?”

“I don’t think things are going to go south.” She hit the purple hood of her car. “The car made it through.”

I glanced at the dents the crowd had put in it at the protest. “It’s early yet.”

“This is going to be the weirdest takedown of my career.”

“Well, hopefully, it’ll be a pulling up, not taking down.”

The marching band collected themselves and started to play a Sousa march that sounded slightly off-key and off-rhythm, and excessively American, but very upbeat.

*It’s enough.*

Off to the side, Mrs. Bao spoke to a bunch of older men dressed in brilliant yellow silks. One of them fit a dragon’s head over his own. The rest of them held the silk that would become a dragon’s body. A group of kids set up a mat and one did a handstand and then flipped over; acrobats ready to perform.

I looked over the few spectators, munching down on Juya’s treats and clapping along to the marching band, and fought a feeling of disappointment, trying to cling to my inspiration from minutes ago. This was a genuine community. It would have to be enough.

As I approached the corner a block away, it heartened me to see the dragon in the window of his shop, watching the scene in the street. I glanced in the teashop and saw Fuller, Melissa, and Carlisle settling in the front window with big mugs of coffee. I told them not to notice me. Behind them, David and Mr. Bao stood at the counter, serving a huge crowd.

I ducked under the awning of the music shop and opened his door to a tinkle of bells. The dragon perched on a stool beside the door. Kui’s face was gray, and his hair was grayer, strands of white shooting through the black.

“Dragon,” I said.

He didn’t twitch.

“Dragon Kui Liu.”

He sighed. I took that for a response.

“I’ll make a deal with you. I’ll never come back if you walk outside with me now.”

“Why?”

How would I tell him the truth without strengthening his despair? “I cannot imagine what you have seen and survived in all your centuries. And I know we don’t deserve it, most of us, but some of us do, and I’d like to ask you to…” to what? *Influence?* I should have thought this through. “Stay?”

He didn’t respond.

I was going to say something about a fun morning; I was going to keep things as light as possible, but my awkward words spouted out instead. Sure, I didn’t pack the same punch as a dragon, but I wasn’t a powerless creature, and a millennium is a long time. I needed to know it was possible to live it well.

“Please?”

He shook his head.

“Okay, no deal. Just a favor. I’ll be in your debt. If you could come outside, you’ll see people do still care.” I had to force each word out of my mouth; it was physically painful as a goblin not to balance the scales. To ask him to do something for me and tell him I’d pay him back when and how he wanted. My intestines twisted.

“Why?” he repeated.

“Because Mrs. Bao worked really hard for you!” I gulped against the sudden tears at the back of my throat and in my eyes, trying to think of a more spectacular reason, and failed in the face of his ancient stare. My patience failed me. “Fine. You want honesty? We wanted you dead. But Mrs. Bao convinced us you’re not a lost cause. She worked all night for you. But even if she fails, we’re gonna be fine. I’ve got water magic. I don’t care how much ice you throw at me—I’ll never slip. Stay here and rot.”

I wanted to slam the door of his shop, but it was on automatic close, so it ponderously shut behind me as I tried for a dramatic exit.

As I reached the crowd, my righteous anger slid into panic. *What had I done?*

I almost turned around, rehearsing arguments in my head. It was nearly impossible to think when I was near him.

But I didn’t turn back when I noticed how our attempt at bringing joy back was going. The band had switched to the theme from *Jaws*; one dancer in the middle of the dragon’s body had slipped, almost taking the rest of the silk dragon with him. One acrobat had fallen and was getting his ankle bandaged.

No one’s face looked joyous. The bystanders jostled one another. The band fought to keep in tune and on tempo.

Near the barricades, I found Scharf.

“Is he coming?” she asked.

*Sure* was on the tip of my tongue, but then I remembered where we were. “I don’t know.”

The jostling among the bystanders turned into a shove and an angry shout. I pulled on my hob magic, seeking the lingering bits of happiness, and funneled them to the potential combatants. It worked; they calmed, but the effort left me winded. And the crowds remained restless.

“It’s not working,” I said.

“Well, we tried amusing a serial killer, and that backfired spectacularly. We can’t force people to be happy.”

I hung my head. “We should’ve tried for confetti.”

“Confetti is illegal in the District of Columbia, but sure.”

“We’re trying to throw a party in the most uptight city in the country. This is a complete disaster.”

“Happy thoughts, Orange,” she said with the same stoic grimness she always had.

Many in the crowds started wandering away.

“You’re right, a complete disaster would be too organized. This is an incomplete mess.”

Scharf spun to look at me. “Orange. Tell me the dragon is coming. He has to come.”

“Okay, the dragon is coming.”

“Don’t tell me that if it’s not true!”

“You told me to tell you… Never mind.” I watched a juggler’s ball slip out of his fingers and whack the end of a trombone, which mashed into its owner’s face, giving him a bloody, split lip. The band went silent after a final squawk of surprise as his compatriots tried to help him.

“Right now, it might be a good thing he isn’t here,” I said. “This whole affair might depress him more.”

Scharf’s phone rang, and she clapped it to her ear halfway through the first ring. “Scharf.”

After a few seconds, she hung up and stood staring into the middle distance, unmoving, ignoring the growing unrest in the crowd.

“What is it?”

Slowly, she pivoted to look at the music shop as she said through gritted teeth, “It’s her.”

“The waitress? Where? I thought we established that watching *The Bachelor* is not proof you are a murderer. Highly suspect, but not proof.”

Scharf shook her head. “Lee just got the print report back on the epilepsy bracelet. We have her prints because she wanted to embed with working agents. It was part of her background check.”

“What are you talking about?” I asked, and then the phrase registered, and I realized Scharf wasn’t staring at the music shop and the dragon. She was staring at the teashop and *Melissa.* “No. What?”

Scharf nodded. “It makes perfect sense.”

“It makes no sense at all. *Melissa* killed Rodger? Why?”

“For now, all we know is that Melissa’s thumbprint is on the back side of Rodger’s medical alert bracelet that was not on the premises when we searched initially but showed up in a waitress’s locker the day a true-crime podcast gets a tip that she must have been involved.”

“Melissa killed Rodger.” I shook my head. “Why though?”

Scharf shrugged. “Her Uncle was slipping out of the limelight. Forced to retire. Her book sales were tanking. The Restaurant Killer was locked up. She was there that night, remember?”

I closed my eyes. “She was at the game.” I tried to envision the pretty, perfect redhead with her fake nails wrapping her hands around a kid's throat. “It’s crazy.”

“The dragon makes you do crazy things.”

I shook my head. “No. The dragon keeps you from doing crazy things. Without him, you revert to the same asshole you always were.”

When Carlisle glanced out the window at us, Scharf pasted a huge smile on her face. “One way to find out.”

“What way?”

She finally looked at me. “You bargain, Orange. You make a bargain.”

“What could a deranged killer want from me? The bargain has to balance.”

Scharf smiled. “I don’t know about you, but I know she’s been wanting to interview me all week for the book.”

“I read the book.”

“The *new* book.”

I nodded, feeling a stirring of excitement that was so much better than constant dread. “That could work. You tell her about yourself. She denies the murder and I’ll know if it’s a lie.”

“No, she has to say it. She was on the crime scene after the murder; she has a very plausible explanation for how her prints got on that bracelet. She’ll look like a terrible journalist, but not a murderer. Reason number 456 why you don’t invite journalists onto crime scenes.”

I crossed my arms. “You have 455 reasons already?”

Scharf stretched her neck, cracking it. “I’m sure I could come up with them.”

“So I am bargaining for her to say the truth, not just for me to know the truth.”

“Exactly.”

My neck tightened. “I’ve never done that before.”

“You’ll be fine.”

*Happy thoughts, Orange.* I had 455 reasons why it, in fact, may not be fine, but now was not the time to voice them.

I spared a glance around. The acrobats had started making a pyramid. The band had moved on to a song I didn’t recognize. Mrs. Bao was playing along on her piano. Juya was still talking with her fans. They would have to do the cheering up on their own for a while.

As we got close, I was struck again by how young Melissa was. As we opened the door to the chimes, she scribbled in her notebook.

“Melissa, I have time for that interview today. Now, in fact.”

“Okay, good.”

Scharf didn’t sit down. “How about over here away from the window?”

“You don’t want to keep your eye out?”

“You think your uncle can’t handle it? Or Carlisle?” Scharf asked and Melissa nodded.

Carlisle smiled. “We got this. And if she comes, we’ll get her.”

I grinned. “I don’t think so.” All three of them looked at me and I bit my lip. I’d forgotten I wasn’t invisible. “I mean, good luck.”

Scharf led Melissa and me to a four-person table across from the counter which was crowded with customers. David swung by our table.

“Can I get you guys something?” he asked.

Scharf shook her head.

“Later,” I said. “You should check out the block party!”

He nodded. “I was going to. My aunt worked, like, all night.”

“Is that what’s going on out there?” Melissa asked.

“Let’s talk about the murder,” Scharf said, sounding stiff and forced to my ears, but Melissa didn’t seem worried. David’s gaze bounced between the three of us before he ran back behind the counter.

Melissa took out her notebook and glanced at me. “What’s your role in this?”

“PR,” Scharf said quickly. “She has no information about the case.”

Melissa nodded and scribbled as I mouthed, “PR?”

Scharf’s glare of death told me to shut up, and I schooled my face to neutral as Melissa looked up. “So first of all—”

“We’ll begin with the first body in the alley?” Scharf asked.

*“*Just a little background. You weren’t involved in the original arrest of the Restaurant Killer, correct?” Melissa asked.

“You mean John Hastings, the man currently incarcerated for the original six murders?”

I chimed in, “Hastings isn’t the Restaurant Killer. If you’re right.”

Melissa blinked and said, “Er, yes.”

“I wasn’t involved,” Scharf told her. “I was working at the Phoenix field office and transferred out here a couple of years ago.”

“And you’re in the Violent Crimes Division here, not Behavioral Science?”

“Yep.”

“Could you walk me through a quick bio?” She smiled. “For the book. Background.”

Scharf glanced at me as though she wanted me to stop the interview, but she returned Melissa’s smile. “Born in New Jersey. Degree from Rutgers. Served in the army in Afghanistan. Joined the FBI.”

“You’re a veteran?”

Scharf merely raised an eyebrow.

Melissa smiled again. “That’ll play well.”

“That’s exactly why I served,” Scharf said, and I cleared my throat.

“Do you have a favorite food? Hobbies? Let’s get some personality into this.”

“She doesn’t have a personality,” I said. “Can we get to the body?”

Melissa looked at Scharf, who showed her teeth again. I couldn’t call it a smile. “I enjoy waterfalls,” Scharf said, “and scones.”

I choked, and Scharf pounded me on the back.

Melissa frowned. “Okay…”

“And solving murders,” Scharf continued in the same tone. “It’s a really fun hobby.”

“Of course.” Melissa turned a page in her notebook. “You were the first to see Rodger Clark’s body, correct?”

“The first person to see the body was the killer.”

Melissa paled. “Of course. Right. But you’re the one who discovered it.”

“Wrong again. David…. A worker discovered the body and ran out into the street to get some help. We were… I was walking by.”

“Someone with you?”

Scharf shook her head, her lips in a thin line.

Finally, Melissa added, “So you immediately recognized that it might’ve been the work of the Restaurant Killer. Can you explain why?”

I had to hand it to Scharf for getting a murder suspect to interrogate her instead of the other way around, but we needed a bargain if this was going to work. I put a hand on Scharf’s shoulder and said, “We’ll answer all your questions about the first moments and the killer, but we’ll need your truthful recollections about *everything* that happened as well. For the PR.” I tried to remember what PR stood for. “Which means…”

“Public relations,” Scharf said slowly, and I nodded vigorously.

“Yes, for the public relations. We need… We need to know everything. Deal?”

Melissa said, “Of course.”

The chime sounded. My heart sped up.

Scharf turned to me. “You go first.”

I shook my head. “It doesn’t matter. Both have to happen for…” I trailed off.

Scharf sighed, and Melissa asked, “For what?”

“For the public relations.” I waved a hand wildly. “Agent Scharf, please tell her your recollections.”

Scharf nodded. “When I saw the body, I realized it was posed similarly to the victims of the Restaurant Killer. I’d seen the photos in the case files.”

“So even though you didn’t have anything to do with that investigation, you still reviewed the case files?”

“When I transferred here, I read over some of the most prominent cases in the area.”

Melissa tapped her pen against her temple. “I see.”

Scharf shifted on her chair. “For just such a situation, funnily enough.”

When Melissa scribbled that down, Scharf said, “And then I saw the fork. It was a pretty good sign of what had happened. Though, since we didn’t discover any plastic forks at the pasta shop like that one, we now know the killer brought it with her, which is a break in the pattern. You know the rest since you and your uncle showed up. Extremely fast.”

She giggled her horrible twittering peal again. “We were at the hockey game.”

“Right.”

I tapped my toes. I was waiting for Scharf to ask the question. When she didn’t, I said, “So what was your involvement at the crime scene?”

“My uncle got the call, and I came along. I’m trailing him for my next book. It’s all cleared!”

I tried to think of what a public relations person did, let alone what questions they would ask. “It’s vital for the—optics—that we get everyone’s exact movements that night.”

Melissa frowned. “I don’t see how that’s relevant. We were at the game. Once the crowd got a little rowdy, we walked out. My uncle got the call a few minutes later because he was familiar with the original case. They needed his expertise.”

“How did you get out of the arena?” I asked. “The crowds were awful… From what I heard.”

“We had terrible seats, thank god,” Melissa said, “Next to a door. My uncle left to get the car, but it was crazy in there, so I texted him I’d meet him outside.”

“No car could have gotten within three blocks of the arena,” Scharf said.

“We met a few blocks away.”

Scharf said, “Run through any alleys or particularly rowdy streets on your way to his car?”

“Everyone was going every which way.”

Scharf tapped a finger on the table. “So, timelines. Very difficult. You were on your own running through the streets, got in your uncle’s car, and got the call about the body. Were you with him?”

“Am I being accused of something?” she asked in an outraged voice.

“Timelines,” Scharf repeated. “You wandered through Chinatown, met up with your uncle, and showed up at the crime scene. Not through any alleys?”

Melissa shook her head, sending red loops of hair spinning. “Taking shortcuts is a stupid thing to do when you look like me at that time of night. Why does it matter where I was? How will that help break the case?”

Scharf dug at her phone clip on her belt. “We already broke the case.”

Melissa’s eyes glittered with interest. “How?”

“A new piece of evidence,” Scharf said casually and turned her phone to show a picture of the bracelet.

Melissa swallowed. “You found it in the waitress’s locker, right? Who would have thought she did it?”

Scharf said, almost casually, “You left a fingerprint.”

She gasped and staggered up. “I didn’t kill him!”

I heard a chime that hurt my head and groaned. Melissa was telling the truth.

Scharf said, “I don’t believe you,” at the same moment I said, “I believe you.”

# 22

Scharf looked at me with surprise. “She didn’t kill Rodger?”

Melissa rocked her head. “I was running; it was raining. My uncle insisted on playing valet, but you were right. There was no way to get back to the arena. I didn’t know where I was. And there was a body.”

Scharf deflated in her chair. “You stuck a fork in the eye of a corpse?”

Melissa clutched at Scharf’s hand. “I don’t know what came over me. It was so unfair, you know? Fuller was the best in the world, and they stuck him in a classroom, and nobody would take any articles I pitched. It was all old news.”

“So you stuck a fork in Rodger Clark’s eye,” Scharf repeated. “One from the arena.”

“I had one in my pocket when we got dogs. They’re so messy, but my uncle didn’t want a fork. He says you eat them with your hands like a real man. It was in my pocket.” She was literally wringing her hands. I’d thought it was just an expression.

“Did you strangle him, too?” Scharf asked. “Or was that done when you got there?”

“I had to make it look like the other murders! But I promise you, I did not kill him.”

“The body was unmarked when you got to it?” Scharf said very carefully.

Melissa nodded but did not make eye contact.

Scharf said, leaning forward. “You took Rodger’s bracelet?”

She nodded. Her face was blotchy, her eyes bloodshot. “I regretted it immediately. I couldn’t believe what I’d done. And then I heard someone running and screaming in the alleyway, and I knew I couldn’t be found there. I thought I might as well make the best of it.”

Scharf shook her head. “Might as well make the best of putting a very important conviction into question, and ruining your uncle’s reputation?”

“I was trying to help!”

I scoffed. “No, you weren’t. You were trying to sell books. You didn’t do your uncle’s career any favors. He caught the Restaurant Killer and put him away, and now there’s always going to be a question mark after it.”

“But I didn’t kill anyone!”

“It doesn’t matter,” Scharf said. “You committed several crimes. Can you go find Detective Lee?”

It took me a second to realize she was talking to me. “Here?”

“He’s supposed to be coming here.”

“But who did it then?” I asked. “We still haven’t solved the murder.”

Scharf slammed her hand onto the table. “There was no murder.”

Both of us said, “What?”

“The tox screen. The barbiturates? His epilepsy meds. Your bargains? Nobody was there except David, Rodger, and her.” She threw a hand in Melissa’s direction. “And she didn’t do it and David didn’t do it.”

I slumped in shock. “Rodger killed himself.”

Scharf nodded once. “The, uh, influences in that shop. And an unhappy kid.”

I nodded slowly. The dragon inspired Mrs. Bao, but what about an artist who listened to that perfection daily and was a bit too much of a perfectionist himself? It wouldn’t be inspiration he felt. And then one day all that luck disappears, and he’s left with his genuine talent. I thought of my loss of control; I’d lashed out at someone else, but it wouldn’t be a stretch to lash out at yourself.

Melissa sagged. “I don’t know what happened!”

Scharf looked at me with some alarm. “Is that true? She didn’t know what came over her? She wasn’t in control?”

Melissa whispered, “I wasn’t!”

I shook my head, thinking of Mert and his bleeding, unconscious face and his literal blood on my hands. She was responsible regardless of his influence; we both had to be. “She was.”

As she collapsed in sobs, I realized I didn’t have to get any detective. A booming voice said, “The cavalry has arrived!”

I glanced back to see Detective Lee, wearing the same suit he wore to the murder scene days ago. He pointed at me and said, “Your consultant is a menace and I want to arrest her.”

Carlisle stood up when he saw Lee and started towards us.

I hooked a thumb at Melissa and said, “Arrest her instead.”

Lee looked thrilled. “Is that her? Did you get a confession?”

Scharf shook her head. “Not for murder.”

“What for?”

I frowned. “Body rearranging?”

Scharf stood up. “We need to arrest her for disturbing the crime scene. She’s the one who put the fork in Rodger Clark’s eye and posed his body—after he was already dead.”

“That’s impossible!” Carlisle shouted. “The waitress is missing. She had a key piece of evidence in her possession. This is a slam dunk!” He always turned colors when he was mad. It made the world a little brighter to see Mr. Perfect turn purple.

Immediately, I told myself to stop that, because, with the dragon still not contained, minor jealousy or schadenfreude could turn into all-out criminal behavior.

Lee dug out his handcuffs. “Tampering with a crime scene is one thing, but what about the murderer?”

“There was no murder,” Scharf said. “Rodger Clark intentionally overdosed on his epilepsy medicine. She came along after he had died and stuck a fork in his eye.”

Melissa moaned.

I crossed my arms over my stomach. “Do you have to keep mentioning the fork?”

“You take her, Lee,” Scharf said.

Carlisle spun toward Scharf, incredulity in every symmetrical line of his face. “We’re giving this to Metro?”

Lee dug out his handcuffs from a clip on his belt.

“You want to write this one up?” Scharf asked and Carlisle shut up.

Lee dragged Melissa away, still babbling about innocence, and Carlisle made to follow them, but Scharf held up a finger. “Carlisle, who fed information about this investigation to a private citizen?”

“She has press credentials!”

Scharf groaned. “You brought her to the autopsy.”

“She was writing a book!”

I snapped my fingers. “The report. She altered the autopsy report!”

“She couldn’t have!” Carlisle shouted, his face cycling through several more colors.

“Yes, she could,” I said. “I read her whole first book. She dedicated a chapter to each autopsy. It was like half the book. She went into every number. It was awful. But she knew what she was looking for.”

“I didn’t know!” Carlisle screeched.

Scharf massaged her temples. “Don’t look so scared. *I* don’t run to OPR at the first sign of trouble.”

“Scharf, I—”

She cut him off. “Are there any words you could say right now to make this better?”

He closed his mouth and stepped away.

“What’s the meaning of this?” Fuller stomped up between tables, his gray hair askew.

“It wasn’t the Restaurant Killer,” Scharf said. “Congratulations, Agent Fuller. Looks like you caught the right man to begin with.”

Fuller looked down his nose at Scharf and me. “Agent, I don’t know whom you think you are, or who that is,” he said, a stiff finger pointed at me, “but I doubt making baseless accusations against a decorated agent is going to get you any further in the Bureau. In fact, I’ll make sure it won’t.”

Scharf crossed her arms, her fingers clenched into fists beneath her elbows. “I didn’t accuse a decorated agent of anything. Except for extremely poor judgment. A civilian confessed to tampering with a crime scene and desecrating a corpse.”

I winced; I knew there was a name for it.

“How dare you!” Fuller came for her with arms outstretched like claws. I blinked and held him back with magic, though I knew in seconds I wasn’t going to last long this close to the dragon.

Instead of a quick Scharf quip, she got up in his face. “How dare I? How dare you!”

“Remember the agon-dray!” I said in a sing-song voice.

“You’re a washed-up nobody with terrible investigative technique who should have been pushed out of the Bureau ten years ago!”

“Scharf!” I said, sounding even to my ears like an outraged mother hen. Other people had put down their tea and stared.

“Says the girl who was investigated by OPR two seconds ago!” Fuller said with a minor smile.

“Says the agent who is so hungry for fame he turned his niece into a sycophant!”

“Okay, that’s enough,” I said, pulling on my powers to hob. As I tapped into Scharf’s rage, the depth of it surprised me. She had sworn up and down that going on probation and missing a promotion hadn’t bothered her.

She’d seemed to kowtow happily enough to Fuller when he had first arrived, but this was a deep, old resentment. Scharf was a brilliant woman who’d spent a lot of her life smiling and nodding and shutting up. Add in a dragon that lowered inhibitions, and we had a career-ending breakdown on our hands.

I’d been worried she might pull out her gun under the influence of the dragon, but she viewed that weapon as an instrument of protection, apparently to the core of her being. She hadn’t been remotely tempted to use it in anger.

But giving a pompous windbag his comeuppance? Absolutely.

“He *still* might walk because your niece pulled this stunt,” she shouted. “You egotistical blowhard! She mauled that poor boy and you’re defending her!”

Time to end this. I didn’t bother searching for calm in anybody. I could *feel* the resentments and anger in the room and yanked on all of them. “*Walk away!”*

I forced power into my words. I hated compelling anyone—anyone I liked—and was concerned this was me giving in to my temptations under the dragon’s influence, but I couldn’t let Scharf torch her career.

There was a moment when nobody moved and I felt like someone was stretching a rubber band inside my head, and then it snapped and all the agents and customers walked away from me and each other, perfectly equidistant. I should have been more specific than “away.” When they got as far as they could, they stopped and stared at walls or tea, looking confused.

It looked like a giant chessboard. Carlisle had been facing the door, and he busted out and kept walking. I wasn’t sure when my influence would wane and he could stop.

I spun in a circle. Scharf had ended up over by the counter. She was staring at a jar of tea like it held the secrets of the universe. I approached gingerly.

“That felt too good,” Scharf said quietly. “But thank you.”

“Um, you’re welcome.” I leaned against the counter and watched as folks snapped out of my compulsion, some faster than others.

I couldn’t stop from cowering as Fuller came to himself and wandered over with nothing of the bravado of a minute ago. Scharf stiffened when she caught sight of him.

“Melissa?” he asked.

Scharf put a hand on his shoulder. “I’m sorry.” When he just sighed, she took her hand away. “Did you know?”

He lunged away from her. “No! Never!”

“But you knew something,” Scharf said evenly.

All the fight seemed to leak out of him onto the wooden floor. The Baos had returned to the counter and were waving their customers back. I felt guilty about disrupting their morning, but they seemed to be recovering without really noticing.

“She’s done this before?” Scharf said softly. “Something like this before.”

He shook his head.

“The best thing you can do to help her now is tell the truth.” Scharf was almost crooning.

Fuller mumbled something.

“What?” I asked and stepped back at Scharf’s glare.

“Tell me,” Scharf said quietly.

“She spayed her own cat,” Fuller said in a monotone.

“Ew!” I said, my stomach heaving.

“She what?” Scharf asked.

Bile rose in my throat, and I coughed. I couldn’t stop coughing and spun around to gulp at someone’s abandoned tea.

“As a child. She…”

“She *tortured* her cat?” Scharf asked quietly.

“She was so fascinated with Hastings.”

Finally, I gulped down enough to settle my stomach, though I didn’t think I would ever feel better again.

“You’ve been waiting for news like this,” Scharf said gently.

He nodded. He looked ten years older than a minute ago. “I watched her so carefully. Tried to get her work. Direct her.”

Carlisle burst back in the door. He strode over and put a hand on Fuller’s shoulder, though he kept his eyes on Scharf. “Let’s go file that complaint!”

“No!” Fuller said quietly. “There is no complaint. I am so sorry.”

Fuller stepped towards the door.

“No complaint?” Carlisle said like we’d denied him an ice cream cone.

Fuller kept going and Carlisle scrambled after him, whispering urgently in his ear as they exited.

“Is it over?” I asked.

“Yeah.” She shook her head like a dog shaking off water. “Yeah, it’s over. That poor boy.”

“You okay?” I examined her. Her color had come back; her jaw was unclenched, and her hand was nowhere near her weapon.

She nodded. “He’s done. They’ll probably retire him with full honors in about, oh, three hours.”

That didn’t seem fair, but she didn’t look upset about it, so I tried to let it go. “I’m glad it’s over.”

“I challenge thee!”

I turned to see a familiar figure tramping through the backdoor from his favorite alley. “Or not.”

The Lubber Fiend strode towards me, still wet from walking in the rain outside Juya’s sphere. He’d be dry soon though because his stringy, long black hair was steaming. He was wearing only the ragged jeans I’d seen him in before. He grinned with his sharp teeth.

I spared a moment to tell everybody not to notice me and told Scharf, who always noticed me, “You might be on your own.”

She spun around slowly. “No fighting in the middle of the teashop!”

I ran for the front door.

The Lubber Fiend pushed a customer out of the way. That guy pushed back. The enormous goblin flattened him without pausing. Small fights sprouted in his wake.

I burst outside into the crowd. It had grown since we’d been inside catching a body desecrator.

I ducked amongst the spectators and wound away from the teashop and the goblin as fast as I could without knocking people over.

The fiend plowed right through them, sending them scattering.

I stopped short when I spotted Mrs. Henderson clinging to a lamppost, looking between the two of us.

“I’m not running away!” I called up to her.

“Okay,” she said.

“I’m not revoking my claim or anything by running away.”

She shrugged. “Running away is a perfectly fine tactic. I think your father ran away from a challenge for a decade.”

I shook my head. “Of course, goblins would be fine with retreat. Half our magic is dedicated to running away.”

I looked back at another crash and cringed when I saw the tuba player trapped in his flattened instrument on the ground and the Lubber Fiend marching away.

I ran again.

The sound of sirens replaced the sound of music as more police cars screamed to a stop at the end of the block. Officers with megaphones disgorged from the vehicles and started running towards the crowd. It felt like we were in a room full of explosives and a burning fire.

Sure, nothing had exploded yet, but it was only a matter of time.

“I challenge thee!” the Fiend bellowed again.

When one acrobat flipped too close to him, he snatched the girl out of the air by her ankle and threw her on the mat. Her thin, high-pitched cry wavered over the sirens. She curled onto her side, clutching her wrist.

I stopped.

The crowds kept moving away from the police until the fiend and I stood alone, facing each other. He had to be a good eight feet tall.

“I challenge *thee*,” he said, pointing one clawed fingernail at me.

I squared my feet, not caring where we were. He had to be stopped. “I accept.”

He roared in triumph and as he did, fire sprouted from his hair and down around him like a cape. He ran for me, fire spiraling behind him.

“Cute,” I said and pulled every scrap of earth magic I could summon, which seemed pathetically little to me, and braced. I couldn’t waste any magic making a cape of my own, which was a shame, really.

He reached me and swung a fist the size of a dinner plate toward my head.

I ducked.

Then got slammed by his other fist and went flying backward through the air with enough time to marvel at a small patch of blue sky above me before I crumpled to the pavement.

The skin on my elbows ground into the blacktop, and every scrap of air left my lungs.

I coughed and gasped for breath and tried to get up. The sky disappeared as the fiend leaned over me. I rolled away milliseconds before his fist buckled the pavement beside my ear.

We both climbed to our feet, him shaking his fingers and me pounding on my solar plexus until something unlocked, and I sucked in a sobbing gulp of air, enough to say, “Ow!”

“Stay still!”

He charged again, and without anything else to defend myself, I raised my one good fist. He batted it out of the way with one hand and swung at my head with the other. I barely dodged in time. I would never win this way.

As his fist wound back again to smash my face, I froze.

*Who said I had to win this way?*

# 23

As I dodged the Fiend’s fist again, I said, “Whoa, whoa, whoa. What exactly are we fighting for?”

I dove under his next punch and kept going between his legs. He spun after me.

He lunged as he said, “Are you the best?”

“The best what? What are the criteria?” I thought back to John Hastings’s extensive definition of “amusing.” “The fastest? Strongest? Funniest? Shortest? What does ‘best’ mean?” I danced backward with each word as he staggered forward, trying to jab me.

The air whooshed out of him, and he repeated, “The best!”

But he’d slowed.

Mrs. Henderson called out from the lamppost, “The best!”

I heard a few other jeers and noticed a few multicolored short people with floppy ears in the crowd. Goblins.

“I want to bargain!” I told him.

The jeers turned into cries of approval. Goblins understood bargains.

“He’s at the top of the heap, right? He’s fighting for all of you, right? To make sure I’m worthy of being your leader?”

“What’s your bargain?” Mrs. Henderson asked.

As the Lubber Fiend came at me again, I held up a hand. “Whoa! I think we can conclude from the way you had me on the ground that you’re the better fighter.” When he thrust his arms into the air in victory, I said, “Wait! Goblins, do you want the best fighter? Can’t you fight your own battles?”

They jabbered and chanted and shook their heads. One said, “We can look out for ourselves!”

I pointed at the one who spoke. “Good. Excellent. So you don’t need the best fighter as your leader.” I turned to the Lubber Fiend. “We have to do something else to compare ourselves. I’ll even let you pick. What’s the next test?”

He squinted at me, and I didn’t breathe, worried he wouldn’t pick up the gauntlet I’d thrown and would keep wailing on me. After an agonizing twenty seconds, he said, “Speed.”

I tried to hide my relief that he’d agreed to play by my rules, whether or not he’d realized it. He could have flattened me in another few seconds—but that certainly didn’t make him the best.

“Speed,” I repeated. “On your mark. Get set. Go!” I took off and reached Mrs. Henderson’s lamppost before he even moved.

“I’m pretty fast. Goblins, do you need speed?”

“We’re all fast!” Since our main magic all had to do with running away, yes, we were all fast.

I cracked my knuckles. “I get to pick the next one. Shortest!”

The Lubber Fiend scrunched up his face. “Hey, no fair!”

“Well, if you get to be the best fighter, why can’t I be the shortest?”

A tiny purple goblin shouted, “Who cares if you’re short?”

I spun around. “Good point.”

I was getting into the rhythm now, beginning to have a prayer of besting him—if I could define “best.” “How about the best Shirley Temple impression?”

Again, he looked confused. “Who’s Shirley Temple?”

I gaped at him. “Shirley Temple. Icon of American cinema. Tap-dancing child star extraordinaire.” With a huge grin, I broke into a little jig and said, “Can you do that?”

A little purple goblin said, “What do we care about that either?”

I said, “Cartwheel!”

I did a cartwheel.

“We fight to the death!” the Lubber Fiend roared.

“Why?” I was genuinely curious. “What’ll that prove? How will that determine the best leader of goblins?”

A gentle rain fell. Juya had been optimistic about her hour. I backed slowly away from him. *Don’t get distracted, Orange.*

He followed and asked, “How about the reddest? How about the one with the blackest hair?”

I shrugged. “True, you win in those categories.”

“How about the oldest?”

“How about the youngest?” I retorted.

“How about the one who can jump the highest?” he countered.

I smiled. “Now there’s one we can both agree on.”

I leaped into the air, using magic to help me fly. I could tell who wasn’t human by whose eyes trailed me up in the air. The Lubber Fiend jumped, but not as high.

Then he leaped again as I drifted toward earth. He got his hands on my throat as we both fell. He was done trying to define “best.”

I snarled and wrestled his beefy hands away, but my already injured shoulder shrieked, and this time the pain shot up my neck. I forced cheer into my voice as I said, “Sure, you’re definitely the best strangler, but that doesn’t prove anything.”

Fortunately, he had to think that one through, and I could take a breath.

“Orange!” I couldn’t turn my head to the left anymore, so I had to twist my whole torso to see Scharf.

She pointed, and amid the crowd, I spotted a lone figure with one leg and two crutches limping toward us on the dotted line in the middle of the street.

The dragon had come.

I spared a glance around. The crowd had thinned. Juya looked cold. The acrobats packed up as they helped their injured members. The silk dragon was trying to rescue the fabric that had fallen to the ground. The band was still trying to extricate player from tuba.

“How about the easiest to squish?” the Lubber Fiend bellowed behind me.

I turned back. Goblins were very entertaining when we wanted to be. Often when we didn’t want to be.

I’d never felt less like laughing. “How about the funniest? How about your best Charlie Chaplin impression?”

He said, “I knew him!”

“Great! But everyone has to see you!”

He mimed falling to the ground and getting up again. Somebody in the crowd gasped and pointed.

I told the world to notice me and started a silly walk. “How about the best Monty Python impression?”

I did another cartwheel and bounced off the light pole. Then I bounced to my feet and did a backflip, using magic to immobilize my left side, ignoring the pain.

For years, people had called me out for not being serious about my life. I found it a little ironic that I was in the fight of my life and had to be as unserious as I could.

As the enormous goblin charged toward me, I dodged, so he ran straight into the lamppost. This time, the whole crowd laughed. The Lubber Fiend looked around and snarled.

“Good one!” I shouted. His frown melted a little when I clapped desperately. A few more people wandered away. We needed to make this bigger.

I crossed my arms and said, “Bet you can’t make fireworks.”

He smiled, and I fought the urge to back up. Goblin dental work was not my favorite. As he put up an arm, I said, “Make sure everyone can see them!”

The Lubber Fiend shot sparks from his fingers like sparklers up in the air. The exodus slowed as the street filled with colors.

I grabbed someone’s umbrella and did a sloppy version of the rain dance from *Singin’ in the Rain*. I barely remembered it, but I knew the key part about swinging around a lamppost.

Suddenly, music filled the square again. I turned to see Mrs. Bao at her piano, playing something light and cheery. Quickly, I danced along.

People clapped, and suddenly Osman asked Teresa to dance with a flourish, and they started to salsa to a Mozart concerto. The rain retreated, and for a moment I felt genuine hope.

“We don’t need no fireworks!” one goblin said in a nasal voice, and I turned.

“Yeah, we don’t need none of that!” another one shouted.

Mrs. Henderson crossed her arms over the jacket she wore and said, with a smile on her lips, “What are you offering, *Peallaidh*?”

I froze. *What was I offering?*

What did *I* mean by best? I was here because I was my father’s daughter, which was a form of government most humans had abandoned as a poor bet a couple of hundred years ago. What did I have to offer at all?

The Fiend straightened. “You don’t know?”

“The best is…” Why would anyone follow me?

The Fiend roared when he saw me hesitate and rammed straight into me.

We both went flying, and I saw the world almost in slow motion: pavement, sky, lanterns, buildings.

We landed. My body crumpled beneath his as he reared up to hit me, and something snapped in my spine.

The pain was indescribable, and I forgot all about the challenge and the dragon.

We rolled away as a geyser of water spouted into the air. I glanced down. I’d landed on a fire hydrant and broken it in half. With my spine.

The geyser of water was impossibly cold, quickly soaking me and the street. Mrs. Bao stopped playing and people struggled to move the piano away from the new rain.

I closed my eyes to harden my body with my earth magic, but I couldn’t feel anything below my waist.

Fear replaced pain.

Blood seeped into the surrounding water beneath me. I tried to get up again but failed. Finally, I collapsed into a whimpering heap. The pain in my spine was ice and fire and fear all at once.

Suddenly Scharf was there, her brown eyes round and haunted. “Don’t move.”

“I’m okay.”

“Orange, you’re not okay.”

Juya crouched next to me. It looked like she flew to me, but that was probably just my shock. The pain was escalating through my chest, but not below my waist. As Juya placed a hand on me, a small bit of warmth seeped into my cooling flesh.

“We almost did it,” I said.

Juya said, “*Silencio, duendecillo*.”

Someone jostled in the crowd and an angry roar went up. Someone threw a punch. I closed my eyes. “I guess we should have brought out the rainbows and unicorns.”

Juya cackled. “That is not what hope is made of.”

Scharf held one of my hands, and Juya’s magic shimmered around me, but I already knew it wouldn’t be enough. My magic wouldn’t be enough, either.

I was worried for a second about the Lubber Fiend attacking and finishing the job, but he posed like a bodybuilder a few feet away, crowing amid the other goblins, who all bowed.

I *knew* my challengers should have bowed to me.

Kui hovered over me, and I froze. I was getting colder and didn’t know if it was the blood I was losing or the freezing water I was sitting in.

“You see, goblin?” he said. “You see?”

“I haven’t given up!” It came out as a whisper, and even I didn’t believe it.

He turned away.

The rush of anger physically hurt, but I used it to seek my magic. Not to heal what couldn’t be healed, but to take this standing up. Surprisingly, my magic *pulsed* within me. I’d expected that the events of the last few minutes would have drained it, but even with the blow to my back, even with the blood loss and the grief in Scharf’s eyes, it hadn’t wavered. I guess there was more to living than baking scones.

I heaved myself off the ground and stood on my own two feet. “If I can get up without a spine, what’s your excuse?”

Before Kui could answer, the water took down one string of red lanterns. One of them landed over my head, momentarily blinding me.

Kui snorted.

I shrieked and toppled back. Someone caught me and helped me to the ground. The pain rushed back in full force, and I couldn’t help a totally inappropriate giggle that turned into a full goblin cackle.

“Get this thing off me!” I said, trying to stop my hysterical laughter. In seconds, I’d be sobbing. Someone peeled the lantern off my head, and I shook like a dog to get my hair out of my face.

The dragon laughed.

The crowd froze. Performers, spectators, workers, and commuters.

It sounded like trumpets, bells, the best marching band in the world, or the strongest spring storm. Kui bent over his canes guffawing.

It took an eternity for him to stop.

I didn’t want it to stop. I was growing colder.

He looked at me and bowed.

He raised an arm, and the clouds disappeared from the sky. Then he held out a hand to me, palm up. I screamed as my spinal cord knit together, each vertebra snapping into place, each nerve rewiring. In seconds, the searing agony had vanished.

Kui pointed above me, and the geysering fire hydrant froze in midair. I gasped as the shape emerged from the ice. He had carved a dragon into the frozen spray. A ray of sunshine struck the ice statue and flooded the street with rainbows.

Juya chuckled. “Well, I guess you were half right.”

“What?” I asked.

“There are rainbows.” She raised a hand and more exploded across the sky.

It gave me enough faith to sit up. I was terrified of what I would find, but my legs responded to my commands. I was winded and aching, but whole.

Kui dried the rain off the piano with a flick of his hand.

After all my talk about joy, the thing that had tipped the scale for him was the rawest, stupidest slapstick? A lantern had landed on my head, and it was so funny that he’d hoped?

I picked up the lantern and clutched it to my chest.

I looked at Mrs. Bao, bowing low to him. No, I had not been the one to turn the tide.

Feet pounded behind me, and I twisted to see the Lubber Fiend plowing my way.

“Stop.”

Halting, he said, “I have won, *Peallaidh*.”

Mrs. Henderson danced in a puddle, looking happier than I’d ever seen her. A few other goblins had joined her.

“No, I win,” I told him.

He shook his head. “You are injured.”

“Do I look injured? I win.”

“I challenge—”

“No. Stop,” I said. “To be the leader of goblins, what’s the one requirement?”

He shrugged. “Strength.”

I shook my head.

“Smarts.”

I shook my head again and said, “You need a follower.”

“What?”

Grinning, I threw my hands out, then immediately clutched my left hand against my rib cage. I still had a bit of healing ahead of me. “The one thing you need as a leader is a follower. Look around! These people followed me here.” Tears welled in my eyes. “I asked them to come. I asked them to help me. I couldn’t even tell them why, but they came.”

Osman stepped up to my side. I glanced at him in surprise as he put an arm around my shoulders and bowed his head. Juya stood beside him, her face vibrant and healthy.

I grinned as I turned to the Lubber Fiend and the goblins who stood behind him. “Who would you rather follow?” I asked them. “A Fiend who killed one of our own? Because he was annoyed? Or a goblin who can get entire crowds to come and melt the rain?”

Mrs. Henderson deflated and said, “You.”

Smiling, I turned to the Lubber Fiend. “You’re no leader. No one would follow you anywhere. I won this challenge the first time we met. And now we have another bargain to make.” If I was going to be a leader of goblins, I was going to have to lead. “You killed a goblin.”

He scoffed. “He was nothing.”

“No. *You’re* nothing. I’ll make a deal with you. You never do magic again. If you do, I will kill you.”

Scharf turned away and put fingers in her ears.

The fiend snarled and veered away, but the other goblins stood firm, blocking him. “Keep your end of the bargain,” I said, “and you live.”

“You won’t know.”

“Oh, I’ll know. If I don’t, they will. And they *will* tell me.” I looked at the goblins. “Bargain?” I asked them.

In lockstep, they answered, “Bargain!”

Even as I said it, I prayed to the gods standing in the square with me and all the other deities I could think of that he’d keep this bargain. Scharf was right—a prison would never contain him; he was mine to deal with.

“Bargain?” I asked the Fiend.

He snarled at me, his face a mottled red, his teeth sharp. For a second, I thought it wouldn’t work. He was going to call my bluff, squish me into the fire hydrant, and declare martial law. But he said, “Bargain.”

The chime nearly deafened me and rang throughout my already sore body. I staggered, but Osman kept me up.

“Now get out of here,” I said.

As we watched him lope out of the square, the world got a little warmer.

“Thank you,” the dragon said. “I didn’t want them to move. I did not realize I had so given up.”

“You didn’t want them to move? The Baos?” I fought down a laugh. Mrs. Bao had been right. He was sad about losing them, not about a parade. We’d done all this for nothing.

I glanced around. The band had finally rescued the tuba player and had started in on “Louie, Louie,” a song they could play perfectly. The dragon dancers had abandoned the dragon part of their costume and were simply dancing with the crowd.

*Not for nothing.*

Mrs. Bao said, “There is room in our shop in Maryland. You could come?”

He nodded. “I would like to come with you. I am sorry about the ice.”

It had been so much more than ice, but I bit my lip to keep from saying so. We could not keep him here. We had to make our own weather.

“But before you move, come home with me, dragon,” Juya said quietly. “I learned from one of your kind how to make dragon’s beard candy. I haven’t made it in centuries.”

He chuckled; it was a dry, hard sound. “My punishment is spun sugar?”

Juya smiled. “Your punishment is hope.”

He looked like she had struck him. “That is a just punishment.” He looked around the square at the party, which was now in full swing around us. “A very just punishment. This city is an aspiration. To see clearly and still aspire?”

I smiled. It was the perfect bargain.
